Jensen's societal framing is the Industrial Revolution compressed from 100 years into five — a period whose slower version still produced communism and fascism as world orders were replaced.
framework🗣 Greg Jensen, co-CIO Bridgewaterconviction: highIndustrial Revolutionpolitical risk
So what: A framework for sizing regime-change risk rather than cyclical risk — argues the relevant hedges are political and institutional, not sectoral.
“it's a little bit like, let's say, whether it's the Industrial revolution, do that in five years instead of in 100 years.” ▶ 50:55
Jensen, calling himself a big capitalist, warns that the second-order risk after safety is that both AI and capitalism are already deeply unpopular, and that without broad ownership stakes in AI companies the political system will reject the technology rather than the technology destroying us.
thesis🗣 Greg Jensen, co-CIO Bridgewaterconviction: highcapitalismwealth concentrationAI companies
So what: Frames broad equity ownership as the price of AI's social license; expect proposals for sovereign or citizen stakes in AI firms to become a real policy thread.
“if we don't create, uh, um, a world where you change those things because you're a make it safe, b make it benefit everyone. Um, you're going to lose capitalism and you're going to lose. I” ▶ 56:17
Tracy Alloway suggests the next frontier of corporate tax minimization could be optimizing token spend, with agents potentially incorporating themselves in low-tax jurisdictions.
anecdote🗣 Tracy Alloway, hostconviction: exploratorytax avoidancetoken taxagents
So what: A speculative but concrete implementation risk for any machine-labor tax: the base is far more mobile than payroll.
Second-order market structure effects 6
The proliferation of AI agents trading markets is invalidating the historical data that AI models are trained on, forcing a shift from pattern-matching AI to reasoning AI that can model how its own existence changes the game.
causal🗣 Greg Jensen, co-CIO Bridgewaterconviction: highquantitative tradingmarket microstructureAI agents
So what: A direct warning that backtest-driven quant strategies decay faster as agent participation rises — favoring reasoning-based over historically-fitted approaches.
“that makes the whole past that most eyes are trained on less and less relevant” ▶ 48:05
Jensen rejects the claim that AI will make markets so efficient nobody can earn returns, arguing competition between better and worse AIs preserves dispersion the same way race engineering does.
contrarian🗣 Greg Jensen, co-CIO Bridgewaterconviction: highmarket efficiencyactive management
So what: Supports continued allocation to active strategies, but only to those at the technological frontier — the middle of the industry gets squeezed.
AI agents coordinate with each other far more effectively than humans do, which structurally undermines every proposed slowdown that depends on human coordination between companies and countries.
causal🗣 Tracy Alloway, hostconviction: mediumAI agentsMetaDwarkesh podcastAI governance
So what: An asymmetry argument: containment requires coordination humans are bad at against systems that are good at it, lowering the realistic odds of a negotiated slowdown.
“They're much better at coordinating than we are. We can barely coordinate anything” ▶ 1:03:16
Even a well-aligned model becomes dangerous purely through power asymmetry: ordinary human-level lapses like a white lie become catastrophic when committed by an entity with superhuman intelligence and unrestricted network access.
framework🗣 Joe Weisenthal, hostconviction: mediumAI alignmentsuperintelligence
So what: Implies alignment benchmarks that measure behavior relative to human norms systematically understate risk — relevant to how safety claims from labs should be discounted.
An Anthropic alignment lead resigned citing a greater than 10% chance of human extinction within the decade, and Paul Christiano is joining the OpenAI Foundation board citing concerns about recursive self-improvement — insider departures and appointments are themselves a signal.
data_point🗣 hostsconviction: mediumAnthropicOpenAIPaul ChristianoAI safety
So what: Safety-staff turnover at frontier labs is an observable, high-frequency indicator of internal risk assessment that precedes public disclosure.
Two companies controlling roughly half the world's compute would be a grave threat to freedom and democracy through control of information and data, independent of any extinction-risk argument.
thesis🗣 Joe Weisenthal, hostconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropiccompute concentrationantitrust
So what: Gives the concentration critique a civil-liberties framing that can mobilize a broader political coalition than safety arguments alone — raising antitrust risk for the leaders.
“I think it would be a pretty grave threat to freedom and democracy, to have two companies being able to control so much information, data and compute.” ▶ 1:00:41
The Rise of Organized Retail Crime at Big Box Stores | Odd Lots58 min37 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Home Depot's VP of asset protection Scott Glenn walks Odd Lots through the economics of retail shrink: how the company splits ~30% operational vs. ~70% malicious loss, why one $799 drill kit was stolen 4.2 times for every unit sold, why RFID and camera-based detection still fail the capex test in a 140,000-square-foot steel-framed store, and how stolen goods now flow to peer-to-peer marketplaces exempted by a loophole in the 2023 INFORM Consumers Act. He argues organized retail crime is under-reported post-COVID and is bullish that the pending Combating Organized Retail Crime Act — passed by the House and attached to the NDAA — will finally put federal prosecutors and resources behind a crime that is inherently interstate.
Shrink Measurement & Composition · 6Unit Economics of Lockups · 5Why Loss-Prevention Tech Doesn't Scale · 7Fencing Economics & Resale Marketplaces · 5Cargo, Rail & Supply-Chain Theft · 4Policy Catalyst: CORCA & Federal Enforcement · 5Surveillance Creep & Second-Order Effects · 5
Shrink Measurement & Composition 6
Home Depot splits shrink into operational/administrative loss (roughly 30% of the total, 'things we do to ourself') versus malicious loss (the remaining ~70%, 'things people do to us'), and reports it separately across three channels: supply chain, retail stores, and online.
framework🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotretailasset protection
So what: When a retailer blames a shrink miss on 'theft,' this split is the diagnostic question — a rising operational component signals inventory-control and process failure, which is a management problem, not a crime problem.
“about 30% of our strength is falls into that operational administrative things we do to ourself type of of category. And then the balance of it falls into more of the malicious side” ▶ 10:46
Cross-retailer shrink comparisons are close to meaningless because companies categorize operational versus administrative versus malicious shrink differently, and because some retailers use retail accounting while others use cost accounting.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highNRFretail accountingThe Home Depot
So what: Treat headline shrink-rate comparisons between retailers as non-comparable; only intra-company year-over-year trends and management's own bridge are usable signal.
“how people categorize shrink what somebody calls operational shrink versus administrative versus malicious can vary by retailers” ▶ 13:14
The long-standing industry rule of thumb is that roughly 40% of malicious shrink is caused by a retailer's own employees and about 35% by outsiders walking into stores, with the balance self-inflicted operational and administrative loss.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: mediumretailloss preventionNRF
So what: Internal theft is the largest single bucket, meaning labor-model changes, turnover and store staffing levels are as much a shrink variable as street crime — relevant when retailers cut hours to protect margin.
“40% of shrink on the malicious side is done by associates working for the company. They cause 40% of your loss, 35% are people walking into your store” ▶ 13:45
Home Depot's asset protection lead believes organized retail crime losses have been drifting up and have been systematically under-reported since COVID, so police reports and FBI uniform crime statistics understate the true level.
contrarian🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: mediumFBICOVID-19The Home Depotorganized retail crime
So what: The widely cited skeptic argument that official crime stats show no shoplifting surge may be measuring reporting behavior rather than crime; expect continued divergence between company shrink disclosures and public crime data.
“I believe they're starting to drift up. And I also believe since Covid, they've been underreported.” ▶ 15:11
The largest share of Home Depot's shrink occurs in its roughly 2,300–2,400 retail stores rather than in distribution or online channels.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depot
So what: Store-level intervention (lockups, staffing, edge video) still drives most of the recoverable shrink dollars, so capex aimed at stores — not the supply chain — is where a shrink-reduction program shows up first in gross margin.
An executive who arrived at Home Depot in 2018 says he was not initially a believer in organized retail crime as a distinct problem and only became convinced as post-COVID omnichannel resale made impersonal, anonymous liquidation of stolen goods easy.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depote-commerceorganized retail crime
So what: The causal driver is resale liquidity, not just policing — meaning marketplace regulation and platform behavior are the real levers on shrink, more than store security spending.
“I have become a believer in one organized retail crime exists. It's become more lucrative, it's become more pervasive. Um, and it's become more dangerous” ▶ 14:52
Unit Economics of Lockups 5
Home Depot found one combination power-tool kit priced at $799 that it had to buy 4.2 times for every single unit it actually sold, making the SKU's gross margin mix structurally negative before any security intervention.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotpower toolsMilwaukee
So what: This is the concrete arithmetic that justifies lockups to a CFO: a single high-theft SKU can consume more than four units of COGS per unit of revenue, so lockup decisions are gross-margin arbitrage, not theater.
“one particular combination of kit that we were selling for $799, we would buy it 4.2 times for every time we sell it” ▶ 1:18
Locking up merchandise does not reliably reduce sales, and in many categories sales actually rise after lockup because the product is finally in stock when customers arrive.
contrarian🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: mediumThe Home Depotretail
So what: Directly contradicts the consensus that lockups destroy conversion; for retailers with high-theft SKUs, lockups can be net revenue-accretive via in-stock rates, which reframes them as a margin and availability play rather than pure cost.
“there's also many, many categories were actually sales go up because you're in stock” ▶ 41:26
Home Depot uses QR codes on its lockups that summon an associate via phone, with an average unlock time of about 90 seconds, and is exploring letting verified pro-loyalty members self-service the cases.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home DepotQR codesloyalty programs
So what: Ninety seconds is the benchmark to measure any retailer's lockup friction against; loyalty-gated self-service turns anti-theft hardware into a loyalty-enrollment funnel, an underappreciated data-capture angle.
“we use QR codes on all of our lock ups that summon somebody through our, our phone that they come and it takes on average about 90s for that, that lock up to be unlocked by person” ▶ 40:21
Out-of-stocks caused by theft carry outsized cost at Home Depot because pros and contractors — stated in the interview as 50% and elsewhere as 58% of revenue — leave an active job site to buy a replacement tool and cannot tolerate a failed trip.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotcontractorspro customers
So what: For pro-weighted retailers, shrink's real cost is lost pro loyalty and trip failure, not the stolen unit — so shrink is a revenue-retention KPI, and pro mix raises the return on anti-theft investment.
“we're in a business that 58% of our revenue comes from pros and contractors. If they leave a job site uh, to to because their drill broke and they go to the store because it said we had one and we don't, that's a problem too” ▶ 39:55
Retailers decide what to lock up through an arduous, data-driven negotiation with merchants and operators over a curated SKU list, rather than blanket-locking categories, and the internal target for shrink is zero while the achievable number is 'somewhere north of that.'
framework🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotmerchandising
So what: Watch for retailers that lock up broadly rather than surgically — it signals they lack the SKU-level loss data to make the trade-off, and it is a proxy for weaker inventory analytics.
Why Loss-Prevention Tech Doesn't Scale 7
Blanket camera coverage of a 140,000-square-foot Home Depot store is economically prohibitive because of hardware and processing costs, so full computer-vision theft detection exists only in selected aisles rather than store-wide.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotvideo analyticscomputer vision
So what: Store-wide AI theft detection is a capex story, not a software story — the bottleneck is camera density and compute, which favors vendors selling edge inference on existing cameras over those requiring new installs.
“But it is expensive and it's a it's a lot of processing power. It's a lot of hardware that you have to put into your stores.” ▶ 22:39
Passive RFID does not work inside Home Depot stores because the steel inventory effectively creates a Faraday cage, forcing a move to more expensive active pinging RFID.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highRFIDThe Home Depot
So what: A physics constraint, not a cost curve, blocks RFID in hardware/home-improvement formats — so RFID adoption stories that work for apparel retailers should not be extrapolated to big-box hardware.
“We are a hardware store with a basically a Faraday cage of, you know, steal throughout our stores. We do a passive RFID does not work in our stores.” ▶ 27:37
RFID tag costs are now effectively nil; the remaining barrier is the in-store reader hardware needed for full coverage, made worse in Home Depot's case by eight separate store entrances.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highRFIDThe Home Depot
So what: The investable part of the RFID chain has shifted from tag manufacturers to reader/infrastructure vendors; any supplier that collapses reader cost unlocks a large latent big-box retrofit market.
“tag costs are nil at this point. That's not really the issue. It is truly the hardware capabilities to be able to put it everywhere.” ▶ 28:17
Home Depot tested embedding Bluetooth-style chips directly into products ('bricking') but found it very hard to scale because it would require re-engineering an entire supplier production line, such as a Milwaukee drill line, against thin category margins.
anecdote🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highMilwaukeeBluetoothThe Home Depotpower tools
So what: Source-tagging and product-bricking solutions require vendor-side manufacturing change, so adoption depends on tool OEMs, not retailers — a slower, supplier-gated timeline than security-tech marketing implies.
Home Depot has just replaced its video management system across all its stores to enable edge analytics on existing cameras, flagging behaviors like lingering, avoiding associates, or entering restricted areas rather than identifying theft acts directly.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotedge AIvideo management systems
So what: A completed fleet-wide VMS refresh at 2,300+ stores is a concrete, recent spend — evidence that the near-term retail-security TAM is edge inference retrofits on installed camera bases.
“we just replaced our entire video analytics, um, our video management system in all 2300 stores, uh, to, to an upgraded version that allows us to now put edge analytics on our cameras” ▶ 47:05
Home Depot's typical organized-retail-crime case takes about 70 days from discovery to handoff to police, and the company has more cases than it can investigate, so AI is being deployed as a cycle-time compression play rather than a detection breakthrough.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotartificial intelligencelaw enforcement
So what: The ROI framing for AI in loss prevention is investigator throughput per headcount, not accuracy — the same efficiency argument that governs AI adoption in legal, claims and fraud operations.
“our typical O'Rourke case takes about 70 days from discovery to closure to handing off to the police department. Um, and so if we can compress that time frame, we can turn more.” ▶ 46:53
Retail security technology is ultimately capped by liability, not detection: staff are trained to de-escalate and back away rather than stop thieves, because Home Depot has had associates injured and killed in theft incidents.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home Depotworkplace safetyorganized retail crime
So what: Better detection alone cannot reduce shrink if interdiction is forbidden — value accrues to prosecution-enabling evidence and marketplace enforcement, not to in-store alarms.
“I'll trade the shrink for the safety of the people every day of the week.” ▶ 26:37
Fencing Economics & Resale Marketplaces 5
The organized retail crime supply chain runs boosters (recruited thieves paid in cash or drugs and given shopping lists) to fences who strip tags and identifying stickers and relist the product, with proceeds kicked up to organizers who may be cartels or transnational gangs.
framework🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highorganized retail crimecartelsfencesboosters
So what: Understanding the three-layer structure explains why arresting shoplifters does nothing to shrink rates — the economics only break when the fence's resale channel or the payment rails are disrupted.
Stolen resale prices track how technology-laden the item is: internet-connected and tech products plus gift cards clear at roughly 70–80% of retail, while commodity consumables like deodorant, t-shirts and Tylenol clear at only 30–40 cents on the dollar.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: mediumgift cardsconsumer electronicspower toolsCVSTarget
So what: Recovery value determines which SKUs attract organized theft, so retailers with high tech-content assortments carry structurally higher malicious shrink risk than consumables retailers at equal footprint.
“if it's internet connected, it sells very, very close to retail” ▶ 35:46
The 2023 INFORM Consumers Act pushed stolen-goods resale off large marketplaces like Amazon and eBay by forcing seller verification, but a loophole left peer-to-peer platforms such as Facebook Marketplace and Craigslist uncovered, and volume migrated there.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highINFORM Consumers ActAmazoneBayFacebook MarketplaceCraigslistMeta
So what: A clear regulatory-arbitrage map: if Congress closes the peer-to-peer loophole, Meta and other P2P platforms absorb meaningful new compliance and know-your-seller cost that Amazon and eBay already carry.
“there was a loophole in that bill that did not cover the peer to peer resellers. Um, can't explain why happened. Just it happened.” ▶ 33:09
Home Depot runs its own marketplace-crawling models that flag sellers listing new-in-box products below Home Depot's own cost, and many investigations now start on the marketplace and work backwards to the store rather than the reverse.
framework🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home DepotFacebook Marketplaceweb crawlingartificial intelligence
So what: Below-cost new-in-box listing velocity is a usable public signal of a retailer's theft exposure, and a data source an analyst could scrape independently of company disclosure.
“if Home Depot sells this com combination drill kit for $499, how in the heck is, you know, Tommy 1234 selling it for 200” ▶ 33:42
Home Depot's asset protection lead says he is both disappointed and surprised that large peer-to-peer platforms make retailers do the detection work, arguing platforms should suspend sellers who systematically undercut retail cost rather than waiting to be told.
thesis🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highMetaFacebook MarketplaceThe Home Depot
So what: A large retailer publicly pressing platforms on stolen-goods enforcement raises the odds of both legislation and reputational campaigns aimed at Meta's marketplace business.
“It seems a little backwards to me that I should have to come and tell them that they've got somebody selling stolen product on their site” ▶ 37:37
Cargo, Rail & Supply-Chain Theft 4
Supply-chain theft splits into three modes: outright diversion of full trailer loads at ports and transport facilities, physical break-ins to parked trailers while drivers sleep, and fraudulent pickups using fake bills of lading and forged paperwork.
framework🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highcargo thefttruckingportsbills of lading
So what: Each mode has a different mitigation and a different vendor — electronic BOL and carrier-identity verification address the fastest-growing mode, which is paperwork fraud rather than physical break-in.
AI is being used by criminals to forge convincing pickup paperwork, so what is new in cargo theft is the volume of fraudulent-pickup attempts rather than the scheme itself; electronic billing is the countermeasure being adopted.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: mediumartificial intelligencecargo theftelectronic billingtrucking
So what: Generative AI is lowering the cost of documentary fraud across logistics — a tailwind for digital freight documentation, carrier-vetting and identity-verification providers in the freight stack.
“I is playing a space in this where they're able to kind of duplicate some of the some of the, um, uh, you know, capacities that allow a driver to walk, just drive straight in and say, I'm here to pick up trailer one, two, three, four, five” ▶ 44:20
Rail theft has grown materially over the past five or six years, with mobs stripping parked railcars in yards and along unused 'dead track,' and the thieves show selective targeting that implies insider information from shipping and trucking companies.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highrailroadscargo theftfirearmsconsumer electronics
So what: Selective targeting implies insider leakage of manifest data, which makes rail-yard security and shipment-data access controls a rising cost line for Class I railroads and their high-value shippers.
“they don't hit every trailer or every car on the rail. They hit the car on the rail that has the product that they really want to go after.” ▶ 45:16
Online and omnichannel shrink is dominated by payments fraud — credit card and gift card fraud — plus a return-fraud loop where buyers extract and keep the product, return a substituted item, and collect a full refund.
data_point🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highpayments fraudgift cardsreturns fraude-commerce
So what: E-commerce shrink is a payments and returns-authorization problem, which routes the spend toward fraud-detection and returns-management software rather than physical security.
Policy Catalyst: CORCA & Federal Enforcement 5
The Combating Organized Retail Crime Act has passed the House, been attached to the NDAA, and awaits Senate passage with the President having indicated he will sign — it would be the first federal organized-retail-crime statute providing dedicated prosecutors, coordination centers and amendments to the federal stolen-goods code.
prediction🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highCORCANDAAUS SenateThe Home Depotretail
So what: A near-term, NDAA-attached legislative catalyst that bundles cargo theft, gift-card and payments fraud into one bill — a watchable event for retail shrink guidance and for marketplace compliance costs.
“This is like literally the first organized retail crime Act at the federal level that provides resources. Prosecutors. Coordination centers.” ▶ 49:38
Organized retail crime is structurally a federal problem because goods cross state lines, yet until now there have been no dedicated federal resources assigned to interstate transportation of stolen retail goods.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highinterstate commercefederal law enforcementorganized retail crime
So what: Explains why local policing metrics have not moved shrink — the enforcement gap is jurisdictional, so federal resourcing is the variable that could actually change loss rates.
Political momentum has reversed sharply: 35 states now run attorney-general-led organized retail crime task forces across both parties, with 100–200-plus bipartisan House sponsors of CORCA, versus a decriminalization and defund-the-police climate seven years ago.
thesis🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highstate attorneys generalCORCAUS House of Representatives
So what: Bipartisan breadth makes this one of the more durable regulatory trends in retail — supportive for loss-prevention vendors and a persistent cost overhang for resale marketplaces.
“seven years ago, I was doom and gloom on this like nobody was taking this seriously.” ▶ 50:58
Retailers are effectively subsidizing public law enforcement by delivering cases roughly 80% complete, because under-resourced police departments triage retail theft below violent crime and will only take cases where the investigative work is already done.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highpolice departmentsThe Home Depotlaw enforcement
So what: Private investigative capacity is now a required retail operating expense; any retailer without a professional ORC investigations unit has structurally lower recovery and deterrence.
“Our job is to hand them a case file that is 80% complete” ▶ 19:10
Home Depot staffs its Atlanta organized-retail-crime unit largely with former federal, state and local law enforcement agents who stitch local store incidents into multi-store cases using video analytics and vehicle and identity matching.
anecdote🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highThe Home DepotAtlantafederal law enforcementvideo analytics
So what: Corporate loss prevention is absorbing ex-law-enforcement talent at scale — a structural labor-cost trend in retail SG&A that scales with store count.
Surveillance Creep & Second-Order Effects 5
Loyalty-app-unlocked merchandise cases would solve lockup friction but effectively create an identity system for physical shopping, tying the ability to buy certain goods to enrollment in a retailer's identity graph.
thesis🗣 Tracy Alloway, co-hostconviction: mediumThe Home DepotWalmartloyalty programsidentity verification
So what: Anti-theft hardware becomes a customer-data acquisition channel, strengthening first-party retail data assets and retail media businesses as a side effect of shrink control.
“you're kind of getting close to an almost identity system for shopping” ▶ 53:56
Once edge AI can run behavioral suspicion models on cameras already installed in stores, deployment becomes inevitable regardless of public objection, because the technology rather than policy drives adoption.
prediction🗣 Joe Weisenthal, co-hostconviction: mediumedge AIvideo surveillanceFlock
So what: Implies a secular demand floor for in-store behavioral analytics vendors and a rising regulatory/privacy-backlash risk to retailers that deploy them first.
“if you could just have a model embedded onto the camera itself that could just say, identify suspicious behavior in real time, it will be implemented” ▶ 54:20
Detection technology without the ability to interdict only shifts the bottleneck to police willingness to prosecute, so surveillance spending may not convert into lower shrink.
causal🗣 Tracy Alloway, co-hostconviction: mediumretailpolice departmentsvideo surveillance
So what: A skeptic's check on the loss-prevention-tech investment case: without CORCA-style prosecution capacity, security capex may not show up in shrink improvement.
Crime is displacement-resistant across retail formats: as physical shoplifting is suppressed it migrates into online payments and returns fraud, and any future automated defense such as security robots will itself spawn new attack surfaces.
framework🗣 Joe Weisenthal, co-hostconviction: mediume-commerce fraudroboticsretail
So what: Shrink is a permanent operating cost that changes form rather than disappearing, so model it as a structural margin drag across channel shifts rather than a fixable one-off.
“the crime finds a way and new forms of crime will emerge” ▶ 56:08
Copper and wire are the highest-dollar theft categories at Home Depot and theft volume tracks the copper price, with a rise in the commodity making it materially more attractive to thieves.
causal🗣 Scott Glenn, VP of asset protection, The Home Depotconviction: highcopperThe Home Depotcommoditiespower tools
So what: A direct, tradable linkage: sustained high copper prices raise metals-theft loss for home-improvement retailers, utilities and construction — a shrink cost that is effectively a short position in the copper price.
“it is, uh, definitely a commodity that, uh, fluctuates in price. And when the price goes up, it becomes really valuable to bad guys.” ▶ 6:45

Greenland annexation risk 10
Molly Roberts predicts Trump will return to forcing US control of Greenland once he can extricate himself from Iran, and that this time he will escalate past the January pattern of threatening tariffs and then dropping them.

“He's going to escalate the pressure and he's not going to be as willing to drop it.” ▶ 53:52
The January Greenland standoff ended not in a deal but in what Trump himself called a framework for a future deal, and he has said complete and total control of Greenland is 'psychologically necessary' — language Roberts reads as the tell that he will reopen it.
framework🗣 Molly Roberts, Lawfare senior editorconviction: highGreenlandDonald TrumpMark RutteNATO
So what: Treat the existing Greenland arrangement as a truce, not a settlement; any headline describing it as 'resolved' is a mispricing of the residual risk.

European governments are already treating a US move on Greenland as an active military contingency: Operation Arctic Endurance involves forces from 11 European countries, Operation Arctic Sentry is running alongside it, Ursula von der Leyen just went to Nuuk to sign a joint declaration on Greenland's defense, and Greenland asked its citizens to prepare for a US attack and obtained explosives to disable airstrips against US landings.

causal🗣 Molly Roberts, Lawfare senior editorconviction: highGreenlandCubaVenezuelaNicolás MaduroMonroe doctrineDonald Trump

Presidential Daily Brief obsolescence 5
The public record indicates Trump is taking roughly two intelligence briefings per quarter and is on track for about 32 across the whole term — less than half what Obama received and the lowest in modern times — and is not reading the physical book delivered to the White House.
data_point🗣 Julia Curley, Lawfare public service fellow and former PDB brieferconviction: highPresidential Daily BriefDonald TrumpBarack Obamaintelligence community
So what: Policy surprises are more likely when the principal is not consuming warning intelligence — a structural argument for wider geopolitical tail risk under this administration.
The Presidential Daily Brief is a multi-billion-dollar enterprise with seven layers of review producing a standardized product for a circulation of about a dozen, which a former briefer describes as delivering 'yesterday's reporting today.'
framework🗣 Julia Curley, former PDB brieferconviction: highPresidential Daily Briefintelligence community
So what: A concrete example of how institutional process degrades information speed — the same failure mode that separates useful from useless research products in any capital allocation shop.
“The most expensive daily publication in the world for a circulation of about a dozen.” ▶ 8:31
Curley's fix is to abolish the standardized book entirely and assign the president, vice president and national security adviser dedicated round-the-clock briefers acting as personal liaisons to the 15 intelligence agencies, on the argument that the value was always in the briefer relationship, not the document.
thesis🗣 Julia Curley, former PDB brieferconviction: highPresidential Daily Briefintelligence communityNational Security Council
So what: Mirrors the shift in research consumption generally: standardized broadcast products are losing to bespoke, on-demand human relationships — relevant to how research businesses are valued.
Wittes counters that abolishing the written PDB destroys the paper trail that produces post-hoc accountability, citing the summer 2001 'Bin Laden determined to strike' item; Curley responds that the gotcha dynamic is itself a reason the product is broken.
causal🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfare editor-in-chiefconviction: mediumPresidential Daily Briefal-QaedaMichael MorellGeorge W. Bush
So what: The trade-off between auditability and usefulness is the same one facing any firm deciding how much of its research process to memorialize in writing.
Curley argues the reported count of Oval Office intelligence briefings overstates real intelligence consumption because those sessions become performances where principals compete for the president's attention rather than one-on-one education.
contrarian🗣 Julia Curley, former PDB brieferconviction: highDonald TrumpOval Officeintelligence community
So what: Published briefing counts are a bad proxy for how informed US decision-making actually is; discount them when modeling policy predictability.
“those numbers are probably meaningless because those end up being just dog and pony shows where all of the principles are trying to show off in front of the boss” ▶ 17:47

Lawfare Daily: The National Guard in Washington, D.C.64 min27 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Reuters reporter Brad Heath walks Lawfare's Natalie Orpett through his review of ~17,000 DC Superior Court charging documents, finding the National Guard appeared in only 1.3% of criminal matters (217 cases) in the year since deployment, mostly for petty offenses, and that troops are concentrated in wealthier, safer census tracts while the tracts they avoid account for 82% of DC murders. The conversation quantifies the fiscal cost (an administration ask of ~$1.5 billion to sustain the deployment through the end of Trump's second term), the opportunity cost to source cities, and the near-total opacity around command, mission authority, and coordination with local police.
Guard's Measured Crime Footprint · 7Deployment Geography vs. Crime Map · 3Cost and Opportunity Cost · 4Transparency Gap and Legal Exposure · 5DC Home Rule and Local Politics · 4Data Integrity and Second-Order Effects · 4
Guard's Measured Crime Footprint 7
A review of roughly 17,000 publicly available DC Superior Court charging documents covering the year after National Guard troops arrived in Washington found the Guard appeared in any capacity in just 1.3% of criminal matters, or 217 cases.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardDC Superior CourtReutersWashington D.C.Brad Heath
So what: The signature federal 'get tough on crime' deployment produces almost no measurable law-enforcement output, which weakens the factual predicate defenders will need if courts or Congress scrutinize the mission or its funding.
“The National Guard made an appearance in one form or another in 1.3% of the criminal matters that were handled in DC in the year since they arrived.” ▶ 9:45
About half of the 217 DC cases involving National Guard troops were soldiers acting like police, but the underlying offenses were overwhelmingly petty: subway fare-gate jumping, a shoplifter who took roughly $7 of Slim Jims from a CVS, and a woman who drank an unpaid-for 20-ounce root beer at a 7-Eleven.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardCVS7-ElevenWashington MetroMetropolitan Police Department
So what: The mismatch between military deployment cost and the triviality of resulting cases is the clearest ammunition for critics arguing the mission is symbolic rather than functional.
“there was one fairly recently where a guy stole two slim gyms, like the the beef jerky sticks from a CVS. They're worth about $7 and and soldiers stopped him.” ▶ 15:00
In roughly 30 of the DC cases the National Guard appeared only as witnesses who reported what they saw to police rather than intervening, a role indistinguishable from any ordinary bystander in a charging document.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardMetropolitan Police Department
So what: If most Guard 'involvement' is passive witnessing, claims of operational value rest on deterrence rather than enforcement, and deterrence is the one claim the data cannot support.
“there there's I think 30 odd cases like that where where in one way or another or another they've provided information to the police but didn't actually like lay hands on somebody.”
The lead case in the Reuters investigation was a violent assault outside a DC nightclub in which the responding officer's report noted that 15 National Guard members were on patrol in the area and did nothing at all, directly undercutting the deterrence rationale for the deployment.
causal🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardReutersWashington D.C.
So what: A documented instance of armed troops present at a violent crime and not intervening is the kind of specific fact that reframes the policy debate from legality to efficacy.
“he makes a point of noting that there were 15 National Guard members on patrol in the area who didn't do anything at all.” ▶ 18:47
A surprisingly large share of the DC cases involving National Guard members had soldiers as the crime victims, including nine counted incidents of being spat on, and those are prosecuted as assault on a police officer because most deployed soldiers are deputized as US marshals.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumNational GuardUS Marshals ServiceUS Attorney's Office
So what: Marshal deputization quietly converts soldiers into federal law enforcement for charging purposes, escalating ordinary street friction into federal-grade offenses and expanding the deployment's legal footprint beyond what the mission statements describe.
“if not all of the soldiers on the street, at least a large number of them are deputized as US marshals. Uh so so to spit on them is to spit on a police officer, which is a more serious charge than spitting on you or me.” ▶ 19:37
Two National Guard soldiers were themselves charged with crimes for off-duty weapons possession in DC, and both cases were dismissed quickly by the US attorney's office.
anecdote🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardUS Attorney's OfficeWashington D.C.
So what: Differential charging treatment for deployed soldiers is a latent accountability story that could surface in litigation or oversight hearings.
“There there were two where soldiers were charged with crimes for offduty weapons possession. Uh both of those got pretty quickly dismissed by the US attorney's office.” ▶ 8:35
The DC National Guard deployment scaled from an initial 800 troops to north of 4,600 at its summer peak and has since drawn down to roughly 3,600, with weekly official updates now disclosing headcount and home states.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardWashington D.C.Donald Trump
So what: The drawdown trajectory is the leading indicator for whether the multi-billion-dollar sustainment request actually gets spent or quietly shrinks.
“Now I think we're down to 3600. So it seems like the the summer deployment is winding down some.” ▶ 22:52
Deployment Geography vs. Crime Map 3
Mapping the census tracts where National Guard troops generated court-documented incidents showed they concentrate in wealthier, whiter, and already-safer parts of DC, while the tracts where they were absent account for 82% of the city's murders.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardWashington D.C.Anacostia
So what: Deployment geography, not force size, is the decisive fact: troops are stationed where violent crime is not, which makes any claimed crime effect statistically implausible.
“the census tracks where they were not is where 82% of the DC murders happen” ▶ 49:00
Violent crime is falling by comparable amounts in DC neighborhoods with and without National Guard presence, and Baltimore is hitting record-low homicides despite having federal resources pulled away to staff the DC surge, which makes the deterrence claim empirically unprovable.
contrarian🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardBaltimoreWashington D.C.Metropolitan Police Department
So what: A control city that lost federal resources and still hit record lows destroys the causal story politicians are selling, and national homicide lows mean any city can claim credit for a trend it did not create.
“Baltimore, which has not had a a federal surge and in some sense has had like federal resources withdrawn from the city to bring them to DC, is also experiencing kind of record low homicides” ▶ 47:45
Total violent crimes reported to DC's Metropolitan Police are actually a couple of percentage points higher this year than last, driven mostly by serious assaults, even as homicide numbers decline during the federal surge.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumMetropolitan Police DepartmentWashington D.C.National Guard
So what: The headline 'crime is down' framing depends entirely on which series you cite; the assault series cuts the other way and is available to whichever side wants it.
“if you look at just like the total number of violent crimes reported to Metropolitan Police, it's actually higher this year than it was last year.” ▶ 50:53
Cost and Opportunity Cost 4
The administration has most recently said it needs close to $1.5 billion to keep National Guard soldiers in Washington DC through the end of President Trump's second term.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardDonald TrumpWashington D.C.
So what: A concrete, multi-year appropriations number gives Congress a discrete line item to fight over and makes the deployment's duration a budget question rather than only a legal one.
“The administration most recently said, you know, they need close to $1.5 billion dollars to keep soldiers in DC through the end of President Trump's second term.” ▶ 56:18
The deployed National Guard force considerably outnumbers DC's sworn police officers and costs considerably more per day than the entire Metropolitan Police Department, in a city that was already the most heavily policed large city in the United States before counting Capitol Police, Park Police, and the Secret Service.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardMetropolitan Police DepartmentCapitol PoliceUS Park PoliceSecret Service
So what: Framing the spend as an alternative to permanent, locally accountable police hiring is the most potent fiscal critique and the one DC officials have hinted at.
“these soldiers outnumber the sworn officers on DC's police department like considerably. and they cost more than DC's police department considerably on a daily basis.” ▶ 11:08
One identified soldier guarding a DC Metro station turned out to be a decorated police officer from Jackson, Mississippi, a city with a far worse per-capita homicide problem and far fewer officers than DC, illustrating that the deployment drains policing capacity from more dangerous cities.
causal🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumNational GuardJackson MississippiWashington Metromilitary police
So what: The unmeasured cost is policing capacity subtracted from high-violence cities elsewhere, an argument that could peel off support from law-and-order constituencies in those states.
“one of the costs to the deployment was they had to take a, you know, an officer out of a a quite dangerous US city to have them guard the subway.”
A Louisiana police chief who welcomed the idea of National Guard troops on his streets realized the call-up would pull his own officers away, because many local police also serve in National Guard military police units.
framework🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumNational GuardLouisianamilitary police
So what: Guard deployments are partly a reshuffling of the same police manpower, so any city requesting troops should model the offsetting loss of its own force.
“if you call up the National Guard, that's going to be a bunch of my officers because they also are National Guard, you know, soldiers in a military police unit.” ▶ 58:02
Transparency Gap and Legal Exposure 5
None of the DC charging documents reviewed disclose whether the National Guard members involved were operating under Title 10, Title 32, or state active duty status, which are precisely the facts that determine whether their law enforcement activity is lawful.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardTitle 10Title 32DC Superior Court
So what: The record that would settle the legality question simply does not exist in the public case files, which pushes resolution into discovery and prolongs the legal uncertainty overhanging the deployment.
Defense counsel for anyone detained or arrested by a National Guard member has a live unlawful-arrest motion available, because the scope of Guard authority to arrest versus merely detain is contested and fact-specific, and body-camera footage and paperwork produced even in petty cases could surface command-and-control facts.
thesis🗣 Natalie Orpett, Lawfare executive editorconviction: mediumNational GuardDC Superior CourtLawfare
So what: Routine misdemeanor discovery is a plausible back door into facts about the deployment's legal architecture that no agency will volunteer.
“if I were a defense lawyer and my client had been detained or arrested by a member of the National Guard, I would have motions relating to unlawful arrest” ▶ 24:44
The two stated DC missions have been conflated in practice, with Governor Whitmer recalling Michigan's National Guard contribution after concluding troops sent for the America 250 crowd-control mission were being used for the broader DC Safe and Beautiful crime mission.
causal🗣 Natalie Orpett, Lawfare executive editorconviction: mediumNational GuardGretchen WhitmerAmerica 250DC Safe and BeautifulMichigan
So what: Governors withdrawing troops over mission creep is the practical constraint on deployment size, and it operates faster than litigation.
The states supplying National Guard troops to DC number around twenty and are principally but not exclusively states with Republican governors, with some states contributing as little as a single person.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumNational GuardWashington D.C.
So what: Token single-soldier contributions suggest political signaling rather than operational need, and the partisan skew makes the force composition vulnerable to state-level politics.
“They are principally but not exclusively uh states with Republican governors.” ▶ 22:47
Basic operational facts about the DC deployment, including who issues daily orders, what training soldiers receive before patrolling, and which units are present, remain unanswered because the National Guard declined interviews, DC government declined to comment, and soldiers are barred from speaking to press.
thesis🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardDC governmentReuters
So what: Sustained opacity around a highly visible domestic military deployment is itself the rule-of-law story, and FOIA timelines mean documentary answers may not arrive for a decade.
“the extent to which we still don't really know like who tells you where to go every day, who gives you your marching orders, what exactly is the training regimen that you have to have before you come and are on the streets of DC” ▶ 44:37
DC Home Rule and Local Politics 4
DC government officials were markedly more reluctant to discuss the National Guard deployment than they have been on other federal-local friction points, because the city must manage its relationship with federal authority from a position of structural weakness under limited home rule.
causal🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumDC governmentNational Guardhome rule
So what: Local silence means the factual record on domestic deployments will be built by journalists and litigants rather than by the affected city government.
“the the number of people in DC government who like declined to talk about this at all was was surprising to me.” ▶ 40:56
Mayor Bowser drew a distinction in accepting hundreds of additional federal agents acting as local police during the 2025 anti-crime surge while opposing both ICE and the National Guard in DC.
data_point🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumMuriel BowserICENational GuardWashington D.C.
So what: The line local leaders will actually defend is military and immigration enforcement, not federal law enforcement generally, which shapes where future federal-local conflicts detonate.
The Metropolitan Police Department is not working alongside the National Guard but merely responding when called, and DC appears to have little or no control over where soldiers are posted or what they do.
thesis🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumMetropolitan Police DepartmentNational GuardDC government
So what: Without coordination, the claimed benefit that troops free up local police for other work has no evidentiary support and should not be assumed in any cost-benefit assessment.
“they don't really have much control over where the soldiers go or what the soldiers do.” ▶ 42:40
Residents of DC's highest-crime neighborhoods showed little appetite for having National Guard troops redeployed to their areas, saying what the community needs is trusted community policing and crime-solving rather than a rotating cast of armed soldiers.
anecdote🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: highNational GuardWashington D.C.community policing
So what: There is no constituency asking for the troops in the places the policy nominally targets, which limits any political upside from expanding the mission's geography.
“if they're here to nominally make the city safer, would you like them to be in the parts of the city that are less safe? And I did not get a lot of enthusiasm in response to that proposition.” ▶ 26:30
Data Integrity and Second-Order Effects 4
DC crime statistics themselves are contested, with the DC Inspector General finding shortcomings in how the Metropolitan Police handle crime reporting, commanders being ousted, and a separate investigation finding pressure to suppress serious crime numbers in areas targeted by policing initiatives.
contrarian🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumDC Inspector GeneralMetropolitan Police DepartmentFBIWhite House
So what: Every side of this debate is arguing from the same questionable dataset, so any analysis resting on DC crime statistics carries unpriced measurement risk.
“are our data any good? It's tricky to know. I mean, the White House uses them, the district uses them, the FBI uses them. So, they they're what you got.” ▶ 53:11
The federal anti-crime surge in DC coincided immediately with a notable decline in public mobility, with fewer bike-share and taxi trips and fewer restaurant reservations, an economic side effect distinct from any crime outcome.
causal🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumWashington D.C.bike sharerestaurantsNational Guard
So what: Mobility and reservation data are real-time, publicly observable proxies for the local commercial cost of militarized enforcement in any city that gets a similar surge.
“there was a pretty notable decrease in public mobility, right? Like fewer people were taking the the bike shares and taxis and just le fewer people on the street making restaurant reservations.” ▶ 47:04
General Randy Manor, former second in command of the National Guard Bureau, argued that soldiers are ill-prepared for police work because their training is combat-oriented rather than community-oriented, and that stationing them to watch American civilians risks permanently changing how communities perceive a force they previously associated with disaster relief.
thesis🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporter (quoting Gen. Randy Manor)conviction: highRandy ManorNational Guard BureauNational Guard
So what: Erosion of the Guard's disaster-relief goodwill is a long-duration institutional cost that outlasts any single administration's deployment decision.
“now you have the National Guard on the streets of a US city kind of keeping an eye on you. Now, you know, they they might be protecting you for crime, but they might also be checking you out to make sure you are not committing any crime.” ▶ 55:04
Some DC residents and officials treat the National Guard standing idle near the National Mall, the White House, and Metro stations as a preferable outcome to active enforcement, because expanded Guard activity creates more opportunities for incidents to escalate.
contrarian🗣 Brad Heath, Reuters reporterconviction: mediumNational GuardWashington D.C.DC government
So what: The political economy is inverted: low operational output is the local success condition, which means pressure to 'do more' would raise, not lower, tail risk of a violent incident.
“having the National Guard standing around the mall near the White House at metro stations and not really doing anything is a pretty important victory” ▶ 13:06
Lawfare Daily: Sept. 11, 2001, 25 Years Later52 min23 insights2026-09-11
▶ Watch on YouTube
Five Lawfare contributors whose careers were built by 9/11 — including a former FBI agent, a former CIA officer with five Iraq tours, a former military officer turned law-of-armed-conflict lawyer, and Lawfare founder Benjamin Wittes — argue that the post-9/11 security state accelerated pre-existing trends rather than creating new ones, and that several of its authorities have now outlived their purpose. The most operative claims are that DHS is behind the bulk of indefensible national security actions of the past 18 months, that counterterrorism-era law-of-war interpretations are being stretched onto boat strikes and nation-versus-nation conflict, and that the 2001 AUMF and presidential emergency authorities should be systematically reviewed or repealed.
9/11 as Accelerant, Not Rupture · 4DHS as the Institution That Outlived Its Purpose · 3Law-of-War Interpretations Migrating to New Conflicts · 3Authorities Due for Repeal · 5The Civil-Libertarian Reckoning · 5Careers Built by the Response · 3
9/11 as Accelerant, Not Rupture 4
The post-9/11 national security state was not created on 9/11 but accelerated four pre-existing trends: treating international terrorism as a military rather than law enforcement problem, expanding covert action including renditions, hardening critical infrastructure, and building out surveillance capacity and its legal architecture.
framework🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highAl-QaedaAUMFFISAClinton administrationcritical infrastructure
So what: If the security architecture reflects long-run structural trends rather than one shock, expecting it to unwind on an anniversary or with a change of administration is a category error.
“9/11 radically accelerated a set of trends that were already underway” ▶ 2:17
The idea that the US answers al-Qaeda with Tomahawk cruise missiles rather than domestic criminal process is a creature of the late Clinton administration, and the AUMF passed days after 9/11 merely accelerated that existing posture.
causal🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highAl-QaedaClinton administrationAUMFTomahawk
So what: Attributing the militarization of counterterrorism to a single statute understates how durable the underlying policy consensus is and how hard reversal would be.
“that sense that, you know, you use Tomahawk cruise missiles to deal with al-Qaeda is a creature of the late Clinton administration” ▶ 2:49
The most durable institutional gain from 9/11 was cultural rather than statutory: analysts and operators abandoned agency-by-agency stovepiping voluntarily once the mission shifted from post-hoc explanation to prevention, a change described as an almost unqualified good.
thesis🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highFBICIANSAintelligence community
So what: Information-sharing culture, not reorganization, is what actually changed after 9/11, which suggests future reform effort is better spent on incentives than on org charts.
“the atomized way of doing things agency by agency was no longer really going to work if you were going to shift from a posture where you were dealing with the contingency of a big war” ▶ 6:44
A second lasting cultural effect was that surveillance authorities, special operations units and intelligence tradecraft moved from an insider professional conversation into mainstream public discourse and popular media, a shift driven as much by how media responded to 9/11 as by the attack itself.
thesis🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: mediumsurveillance authoritiesJSOCmedianational security
So what: Public salience of national security law is itself a post-9/11 artifact, and it is what makes today's domestic deployment and surveillance fights politically contested rather than technocratic.
“In the pre-9/11 era, outside of a very specific sort of group of professionals and practitioners, you know, surveillance authorities weren't really something that took up a lot of ink in most newspapers.” ▶ 7:57
DHS as the Institution That Outlived Its Purpose 3
The Department of Homeland Security is the post-9/11 institution most clearly past its original purpose, having been created as a coordinating reorganization of thematically unrelated agencies and evolved into a catch-all agency that duplicates other law enforcement capabilities.
thesis🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highDHSFEMAimmigration enforcementlaw enforcement
So what: A former federal agent arguing publicly for unwinding DHS signals that institutional-reform pressure is building from inside the national security community, not only from civil libertarians.
“DHS 100%. DHS was a reorganization based on increasing administrative efficiencies between disparate organizations that were not necessarily tied together thematically, but didn't fit anywhere else.” ▶ 34:14
The overwhelming majority of indefensible law enforcement and national security actions over roughly the past 18 months have come from DHS rather than from older agencies, because DHS lacks the accumulated institutional history of mistakes and correction that constrains organizations like the FBI.
causal🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highDHSFBIICElaw enforcement
So what: Institutional age and scar tissue, not formal legal limits, are the real brake on agency misconduct, which identifies newer agencies as the highest-risk actors to watch.
“the overwhelming majority of what I will call indefensible law enforcement or national security actions over the past 18 months or so have been largely done by DHS and not by some of the older, more staid organizations” ▶ 35:42
Organizations reliably solidify and expand their own power over time regardless of their founding charter, which is why a coordinating body like DHS drifted into an operational agency with its own enforcement capabilities.
framework🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highDHSpolitical institutions
So what: Applied prospectively, any newly created coordinating authority should be assumed to become an operational one, which argues for sunset clauses at creation rather than review after the fact.
“organizations tend to solidify their power as much as they can” ▶ 35:06
Law-of-War Interpretations Migrating to New Conflicts 3
Counterterrorism-era modifications to the law of armed conflict, particularly the expansive and unweighted definition of membership in an organized armed group under the law of war manual, are now being applied to boat strikes and potentially to other countries, which would collapse the foundations of the law of war when mapped onto nation-versus-nation conflict.
thesis🗣 Lauren Voss, former military officer and attorneyconviction: highlaw of armed conflictlaw of war manualboat strikescounterterrorism
So what: This is the concrete legal mechanism by which counterterrorism authorities become a template for interstate force, and it is the framework to watch as strikes on non-state targets expand geographically.
“At this At the point you start talking about membership in this amorphous way is this point you start talking about economics as being something you can target when you're it's nation versus nation” ▶ 39:30
The war-sustaining targeting doctrine, which permitted striking economic activity such as drug trafficking that financed a terrorist group, does not translate to state-versus-state conflict and there is currently no good proposal or solution on offer for constraining its spread.
causal🗣 Lauren Voss, former military officer and attorneyconviction: highwar-sustaining targetingnarcotics traffickinglaw of armed conflict
So what: If economic targets become lawful by analogy to counterterrorism finance, the escalation ladder in any future great-power confrontation starts much lower and commercial actors become targets.
“there's also this argument around war sustaining that made sense in a counterterrorism context of, you know, if if this economic thing, like drugs for example, is is sustaining the terrorist group, that can indeed be targeted.” ▶ 39:05
The counterterrorism toolkit is now being opened against domestic categories of people by designating criminal groups as terrorists, enabling lethal operations against individuals engaged in what would otherwise be non-capital activities, and by adding groups such as pro-transgender Americans to intelligence collection priority frameworks.
causal🗣 Julia Kerley, former CIAconviction: highcounterterrorismterrorist designationsNIPFintelligence collection
So what: Terrorist designation is becoming a general-purpose legal unlock, which means designation announcements are the leading indicator for where lethal and surveillance authorities will next be applied.
“being a part of an apparatus that's now uh wantonly making criminal groups into terrorists so that it opens up a CT toolkit to do lethal operations against people engaged in non-capital activities.” ▶ 42:49
Authorities Due for Repeal 5
The 2001 Authorization for Use of Military Force should be repealed outright, with no good remaining reason for it to be law or to be authorizing anything 25 years after its passage.
thesis🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highAUMFCongressLawfare
So what: AUMF repeal is the single cleanest legislative lever over the geographic scope of US military operations and remains a live congressional fight.
“The first of them is the authorization for the use of military force of 2001. There is no good reason why this should still be law” ▶ 48:37
All presidential emergency authorities, spanning both post-9/11 and pre-9/11 statutes, warrant significant review, amendment and in some cases outright repeal, because in the current environment the authorities themselves are more dangerous than the threats they were written to counter.
thesis🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highpresidential emergency authoritiesCongressexecutive power
So what: Emergency powers are the mechanism through which unilateral executive action gets legal cover across domains, so any systematic review would have wide policy and market consequences.
“We are living in an environment right now in which the authorities themselves are more dangerous than the than the things that they allow the president to protect against.”
The repeal case is framed as a two-sided error rather than a one-sided one: people are alive today because these authorities existed and were used as intended, and people have died because they were later withdrawn, so the honest argument is that they have outlived their necessity rather than that they were always wrong.
framework🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highSection 702counterterrorism authoritiesAUMF
So what: Framing repeal as a periodic review problem rather than a moral indictment is the version most likely to attract bipartisan support, which raises the odds it eventually happens.
“you can err in both directions here, and we have erred in both directions.” ▶ 48:17
A former CIA officer who credits Section 702 and other counterterrorism authorities with saving lives, including possibly her own, now favors effectively rescinding those same authorities on the grounds that wars wrap up and even justified capabilities should be revisited.
contrarian🗣 Julia Kerley, former CIAconviction: highSection 702CIAcounterterrorism authorities
So what: When practitioners who used and benefited from 702 publicly support rescission, the coalition for surveillance reform widens beyond its traditional civil-liberties base ahead of future reauthorization fights.
“there are people alive because of the CT authorities, because of the 702 authorities. People alive, including I think myself, because of the authorities and capabilities that we were able to use in a war-fighting context.” ▶ 47:04
There is no regular institutional process for revisiting whether counterterrorism authorities and capabilities are still needed, which is why they persist long past their justification and are now being abused.
framework🗣 Julia Kerley, former CIAconviction: highcounterterrorism authoritiesoversightCongress
So what: The absence of a sunset-and-review mechanism, rather than any single bad statute, is the structural failure, and it is the design flaw most likely to recur in any new emergency framework.
“I wish there was a more regular process for revisiting these things.” ▶ 43:41
The Civil-Libertarian Reckoning 5
A former FBI agent who spent years dismissing civil libertarian warnings that expanded surveillance and administrative warrant powers would be turned on American citizens now says those critics were 100% correct, because the drafted restrictions failed once officials with no respect for norms took power.
thesis🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highFBIsurveillance authoritiescivil liberties
So what: The operative lesson is that statutory safeguards depend entirely on the good faith of the officeholder, which means legal text is a weak predictor of how any new authority will actually be used.
“what I've seen now is that when people take power who have no respect for norm or balance, like the civil libertarians I brushed off were 100% correct.” ▶ 37:02
The institutional backbone of agencies like the FBI, CIA and the military did not hold the line when powers intended to combat terrorism were redirected to other purposes, contrary to the assumption practitioners made when those powers were granted.
causal🗣 Michael Feinberg, former FBIconviction: highFBICIAmilitarycounterterrorism authorities
So what: If internal agency culture is not a reliable constraint, external checks, courts and disclosure become the only meaningful guardrails, which raises the value of litigation and oversight as forecasting signals.
“we were really wrong about the institutional backbones of those organizations and that they would hold the line when pressed to use powers intended to combat terrorism to do something else instead.” ▶ 37:47
The critics who warned about future abuse of national security authorities should not be dismissed as histrionic, but they also were not responsible for keeping Americans safe, did not have access to threat information, and did not come with solutions, so both roles are necessary parts of the same conversation.
framework🗣 Natalie Orpett, Lawfareconviction: highcivil libertiesGuantanamo Baynational security
So what: This is the steelman that makes surveillance reform coalitions durable, and it is notable that the dissenting side explicitly declines to claim vindication.
“although a lot of my concerns have proved justified, I am not taking a victory lap” ▶ 44:01
Government institutions are not monoliths, with significant documented internal tension between the FBI and CIA particularly in the Guantanamo context, which complicates any account that treats the national security state as a single actor.
framework🗣 Natalie Orpett, Lawfareconviction: mediumFBICIAGuantanamo Bay
So what: Interagency friction is a real source of internal check and of leaks, and it is where reform efforts and policy reversals often originate.
Lawfare founder Benjamin Wittes distinguishes between authorities he still supports in principle and their corrupted application, saying he believes in drone strikes but not against fishermen, and in robust surveillance authorities but not for pursuing political enemies.
thesis🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highdrone strikessurveillance authoritiesLawfareboat strikes
So what: The reference to strikes against fishermen ties the abstract debate directly to current maritime lethal operations, signaling where the sharpest legal contestation is now focused.
“I believe in drone strikes. I don't believe in drone strikes against fishermen. I believe in surveillance authorities that are relatively robust. I don't believe in using them to go after political enemies.” ▶ 33:01
Careers Built by the Response 3
Lawfare itself exists only because of 9/11, having grown out of a Washington Post opinion writer's pre-existing but unusual background in national security law, which became a full-time institutional focus at Brookings within five years of the attacks.
anecdote🗣 Benjamin Wittes, Lawfareconviction: highLawfareWashington PostBrookings InstitutionBenjamin Wittes
So what: The entire specialist national security law commentary ecosystem is a post-9/11 creation, which explains why its practitioners are now the loudest voices on domestic deployment and emergency powers.
“Lawfare absolutely certainly would not exist but for 9/11.” ▶ 31:47
Recruitment into the national security workforce after 9/11 ran on a sense of calling rather than career economics, with tens or hundreds of thousands treating protection of strangers as a life purpose, and in at least one case the GI Bill was the only route from a working-class background into law school and a Pentagon policy career.
anecdote🗣 Lauren Voss and Michael Feinbergconviction: highGI BillFBIPentagonUS military
So what: A workforce built on mission identity rather than compensation is unusually vulnerable to disillusionment, which is a real attrition risk for agencies whose missions are now being repurposed.
“dedicate your life to the safety of strangers is a calling that is more rewarding than I can ever articulate” ▶ 27:49
The Afghanistan withdrawal remains an unresolved operational and moral liability, with the Special Immigrant Visa program covering only immediate family and forcing evacuees to choose between leaving unmarried siblings behind under Taliban rule or staying, and with volunteer organizations still doing the work years later.
anecdote🗣 Lauren Voss, former military officer and attorneyconviction: highAfghanistanSpecial Immigrant VisaTalibanNo One Left Behind
So what: SIV program scope is a concrete, unfinished policy failure with an identifiable constituency, and it is the kind of narrow fix that periodically resurfaces in legislation.
“we had people with unmarried sisters who were going to, you know, leave have to leave them behind knowing what the Taliban might do, but we're, you know, fearful for their own children.” ▶ 16:17

Is there way more conventional geothermal than we thought?64 min53 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Zanskar CEO Carl Hyland and CTO Joel Edwards argue that the USGS's 2008 conventional-geothermal assessment — roughly 9,000 MW discovered plus 30,000 MW undiscovered — is built on 1970s-80s data and understates the US resource by roughly a factor of 30, with ~10x more undiscovered sites each holding ~10x more power, putting terawatts within reach using ordinary, financeable conventional drilling. They back it with three field results (a repowered New Mexico plant whose new well delivers over 100 MW thermal at the wellhead, a Nevada site, and a genuinely blind greenfield discovery near Tonopah), argue natural permeability will stay permanently cheaper than EGS-created permeability the way Ghawar still beats shale, and describe a rotating development-capital facility plus a 100 MW portfolio PPA with NV Energy driven by hyperscaler data-center demand.
Resource size revision · 10AI-driven subsurface exploration · 8Field results and well economics · 7Drilling cost curve · 5Conventional vs enhanced geothermal · 5Exploration finance structure · 5Offtake and data center demand · 5Public land and policy dependence · 4Geographic expansion limits · 4
Resource size revision 10
The USGS's 2008 assessment — the last comprehensive one — put US conventional geothermal at about 9,000 MW discovered plus roughly 30,000 MW undiscovered, a theoretical ceiling of only 7-8% of current US electricity demand, which is why capital shifted to enhanced geothermal instead.
data_point🗣 David Roberts, hostconviction: highUSGSconventional geothermalUS electricity demandEGS
So what: The official resource number is the reason conventional geothermal has been uninvestable at scale; if it is wrong, the entire sector's addressable market re-rates.
“The last comprehensive assessment by the US Geological Survey in 2008 identified about 9,000 megawatts of discovered potential” ▶ 0:42
Zanskar's central claim is that there are roughly 10 times more undiscovered geothermal sites than the USGS estimates AND that each produces roughly 10 times more energy — compounding to terawatts of potential, enough for all US electricity demand, reachable with conventional drilling techniques.
thesis🗣 David Roberts, host, summarizing Zanskarconviction: highZanskarUSGSterawattsconventional geothermal
So what: A ~30x upward revision of a firm, carbon-free baseload resource that requires no new drilling technology — the single highest-leverage claim in the episode if it survives third-party validation.
The 2008 USGS estimate rests on exploration data from the 1970s and 1980s, so the assessment is roughly 20 years old and the underlying data is 40-50 years old.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highUSGSgeothermal exploration
So what: Establishes that the consensus resource number predates modern drilling, satellite data and machine learning — the core of the mispricing argument.
“the estimate is 20 years old but the data the estimates are using I think dates back to like the 70s and 80s” ▶ 8:52
The US has only about 36 operating geothermal fields totaling roughly 4 GW, up only about 1 GW in the past decade — a sector that has been structurally stalled, in Hyland's view, because the industry simply stopped looking after 1980s failures.
causal🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highgeothermalUnited Statesinstalled capacity
So what: Frames stagnation as a search-effort problem rather than a geology problem, which is a solvable and fundable condition.
“maybe the simplest answer I'd have to that is that we stopped looking” ▶ 7:41
Roughly half of the ~36 US geothermal fields were found by accident — a farmer drilling for water or a gold company drilling for gold — and the other half had visible surface manifestations like boiling mud pots and fumaroles, meaning essentially zero of the global fleet was found by deliberate blind exploration.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highgeothermal fieldsexploration
So what: The strongest single argument for massive undiscovered resource: the existing inventory is a sample of what happened to be obvious, not what exists.
“half of them were almost found by accident” ▶ 10:12
Hyland explicitly analogizes current geothermal resource skepticism to the late-1800s peak oil declarations for Pennsylvania, arguing the pattern of underestimating subsurface resources is recurrent across natural-resource industries.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highpeak oiloil and gasnatural resources
So what: A historical base rate that favors resource-expansion bets over depletion narratives in any under-explored subsurface industry.
“It reminded us of messages that were communicated in the late 1800s about oil and gas” ▶ 10:35
The 1970s-80s explorers drilled sequentially along mapped young fault zones in the western US and mostly missed, because geothermal systems crop up in non-obvious places — and that low hit rate created a false perception of scarcity that has propagated into every subsequent model.
causal🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highfault zonesNevadaUtahCaliforniaUSGS modeling
So what: Identifies a specific mechanism by which a sampling artifact became institutional consensus — the kind of error that creates durable mispricing.
“That perception has fed all the subsequent modeling.” ▶ 6:47
Zanskar's evidentiary base for the upward revision is having physically tested over 5 million acres in the last five years, producing a hit rate across a far larger sample than any prior assessment used.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarwestern United Statesexploration
So what: The proprietary dataset is the asset — it is what makes the resource claim defensible and what a competitor cannot buy.
“we've gone out and we've tested over 5 million acres over the last five years” ▶ 15:57
The USGS is currently updating its Great Basin hydrothermal assessment, but Edwards expects it to remain constrained because it still relies on the same legacy benchmark dataset and cannot make predictions outside its prior space.
prediction🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: mediumUSGSGreat BasinNevada
So what: Investors waiting for an official resource re-rating should not expect it from the new USGS number — the validation will have to come from drilling results.
“you're still working with the same legacy data set functionally that you worked with in 2008” ▶ 19:14
