Zanskar cannot share its data with the USGS because the agency's publication mandate would make its discovery sites public before leases are secured — a structural reason the official resource estimate will stay stale.
causal🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highUSGSZanskargeothermal leases
So what: Creates a persistent information asymmetry between the private explorer and the public benchmark — good for Zanskar's land position, bad for sector-wide capital formation.
“if we were to share, you know, then that would make all our sites public” ▶ 19:57
AI-driven subsurface exploration 8
Zanskar's founding insight was that modern neural networks — post-AlexNet 2012 and transformers 2017 — could finally handle the sparse, messy, unstructured data problem that defeated earlier statistical approaches to geothermal exploration.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highAlexNettransformersneural networksZanskar
So what: Dates the technology unlock precisely — the same timing argument applies to other sparse-data subsurface industries (minerals, groundwater, carbon storage).
“One of the founding insights for the company was seeing how quickly neural networks were evolving.” ▶ 21:06
Hyland's mental model for the AI advantage is inference from partial observation: if a geothermal system is a cat underground and you can only see its ears at the surface, the model infers how big the animal is and where its tail lies from a fragment of the data.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highmachine learninggeothermal exploration
So what: A transferable framing for where AI creates real edge — completing a latent structure from sparse indirect signals, not just organizing old records.
Edwards is explicit that probabilistic models are not the moat — national labs, universities and other companies build them, and 'you could go into Claude right now and probably build a probabistic model of your own' — the defensible asset is the iterative loop between model predictions and field testing.
contrarian🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarnational labsAI models
So what: A blunt statement that in physical-resource AI the model commoditizes and the closed-loop field-verification dataset is what holds value — a test to apply to every 'AI for X' industrial startup.
“you have to pair your model predictions with tests in the field of those predictions” ▶ 36:39
Zanskar's models started out materially worse than expert human explorers when benchmarked against expert predictions, and only began outperforming in the last year or two.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarAI modelsexpert benchmarking
So what: An unusually honest capability timeline — and a warning that industrial AI takes roughly five years of field data before it beats domain experts.
“In the early days, our models were frankly much worse than expert explorers.” ▶ 39:09
Edwards concedes Zanskar has missed far more often than it has hit, and that model performance improves non-monotonically — periods of successive hits alternate with cold slumps, producing a spiky, streaky discovery rate rather than a smooth curve.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarexploration hit rate
So what: Discovery lumpiness means single-quarter drilling results are noise — the right unit of evaluation is the portfolio and the multi-year hit rate.
“And to be clear, we've missed way more than we've hit.” ▶ 39:00
Model gains come from two independent sources — more data with the same architecture yields higher precision, and architecture changes with the same data also improve predictions — and apparent step-backs usually reflect a more honest estimate of uncertainty rather than a worse model.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarmodel architectureuncertainty quantification
So what: Two compounding improvement vectors, and a reminder that calibration improvements can look like accuracy regressions to an outside observer.
Zanskar measures its hit rate two ways — whether a drilled well's temperature and permeability outcome falls within the model's predicted probability range and how close it lands to the median, and separately via a physical flow test that measures flow, heat content and volume per unit time.
framework🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarflow testwell performance
So what: A concrete diligence checklist for anyone underwriting AI-led exploration claims: calibration against stated probabilities, not just headline discoveries.
The same AI-for-exploration pitch is being run in mineral exploration by KoBold, where traditional discovery amounted to a person walking the surface looking for visible manifestations — an astonishingly analog process with a very low hit rate and correspondingly high per-discovery cost.
framework🗣 David Roberts, hostconviction: mediumKoBold Metalsmineral explorationAI
So what: Identifies a repeatable template — AI applied to legacy analog exploration data — that is now being run across at least two separate resource sectors.
Field results and well economics 7
At Lightning Dock in New Mexico, Zanskar acquired a failing, underproducing geothermal field and within a year drilled a single deeper well that restored the plant to full nameplate capacity and made the facility profitable.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highLightning DockNew MexicoZanskargeothermal plant
So what: Proves a repeatable acquisition strategy — buy written-off geothermal assets where the model disagrees with the incumbent's resource assessment, then re-drill.
The Lightning Dock well proved potential as much as 10 to 40 times larger than initial industry estimates for that site, and Zanskar says the same under-assessment pattern applies across many other fields.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highLightning DockNew Mexicoresource assessment
So what: If existing brownfield sites are undervalued by 10-40x, the cheapest exposure to the thesis is buying distressed operating geothermal assets, not greenfield exploration.
“as much as 10 to 40 times more than the initial industry estimates were for that site” ▶ 17:56
The new Lightning Dock well delivers over 100 MW of thermal energy at the wellhead — as much heat as a small modular nuclear reactor — from a single wellbore you can wrap your arms around, and it is the most productive pumped geothermal well in the US benchmarked against Nevada's 200+ published production wells.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland and Joel Edwards, Zanskarconviction: highLightning DockSMRNevadapumped wells
So what: The SMR comparison is the relevant one for data-center buyers evaluating firm carbon-free heat and power per unit of capital — and it is available today rather than in the 2030s.
“And that's as much heat as a small modular nuclear reactor would create.” ▶ 27:16
The Lightning Dock well has run at full capacity for over 12 months with no temperature decline, and remains pump-limited — a stronger pump would allow materially higher flow rates than it currently produces.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland and Joel Edwards, Zanskarconviction: highLightning Dockreservoir declinepumps
So what: Twelve months of flat temperature is the key reservoir-durability datapoint offtakers need; pump-limitation implies further upside at trivial incremental capex.
“It is actually still pump limited.” ▶ 35:34
Big Blind in Nevada, west of Tonopah, is a genuine blind greenfield discovery — no prior wells, no surface manifestations — found from model predictions in late 2023, drilled in 2025, hitting commercial reservoir conditions at only 2,000 to 3,000 feet, with a heat-flow anomaly footprint larger than either Pumpernickel or Lightning Dock.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highBig BlindTonopahNevadagreenfield exploration
So what: The proof point that matters: commercial conditions found with zero prior signal validates that the undiscovered-resource thesis is testable, not just modeled.
The first Pumpernickel well failed not on targeting but on drilling conditions — and Edwards notes the counterintuitive correlation that the better the geothermal resource, the harder it is to drill, because the open pathways that make it productive destabilize the well.
causal🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highPumpernickelNevadadrillingpermeability
So what: Means dry-hole statistics systematically understate discovery success — some 'failures' are actually resource hits that could not be completed.
“It's actually sort of fairly correlated that the better the resource, the harder the drilling.” ▶ 31:51
Zanskar made its first discoveries in late 2021 and has many undisclosed discoveries beyond the three public projects, with announcement timing gated by the land work required to assemble federal, state and private lease positions.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highZanskargeothermal leasesBLM
So what: Public project count materially understates the pipeline; leasing cadence, not discovery cadence, is the observable that will reveal it.
“So our first discoveries back towards the tail end of 2021.” ▶ 1:01:25
Drilling cost curve 5
Geothermal is importing the oil and gas drilling stack wholesale — PDC bits, directional drilling assemblies, mud motors and automated rigs — collapsing geothermal well times from 40, 50 or 60-plus days down to weeks.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland and Joel Edwards, Zanskarconviction: highPDC bitsdirectional drillingmud motorsshaleoilfield services
So what: A direct read-through: the 15 years of shale-funded drilling R&D is now a free cost tailwind for geothermal, and oilfield service providers gain a new end market.
“a well would take 40, 50, 60 plus days to drill in geothermal. And now those timelines are collapsing down to, you know, weeks” ▶ 24:47
Zanskar has achieved 50% to sometimes 90% cost declines in exploration data collection by bringing drilling and data collection in-house and optimizing for which well types most efficiently vector toward a target.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarexploration costsvertical integration
So what: Exploration cost, historically the killer of geothermal economics, is falling faster than drilling cost — the main lever on levelized cost of electricity.
“we've seen significant cost declines, 50 to sometimes 90% cost declines” ▶ 42:15
Depth cost is non-linear because two things scale together: rig time, described as operating a small city daily, and construction materials — cement and steel — since a well must be constructed, not merely bored.
framework🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highdrilling economicswell construction
So what: Explains why shallow commercial conditions (2,000-3,000 ft at Big Blind) are disproportionately valuable relative to deep resources.
Hyland's stated target is to exit subsidy dependence entirely, claiming that with current off-the-shelf technology and Zanskar's own cost of capital, geothermal would already be cheaper than all other power sources unsubsidized if you knew exactly where to drill and never missed.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highlevelized cost of electricitysubsidiesgeothermal
So what: Reduces the entire cost problem to exploration accuracy — meaning model hit rate is the single variable determining whether geothermal becomes the cheapest firm power.
“if you just knew exactly where to drill every time and you never missed, it would already be cheaper than all of those other power sources on an unsubsidized basis” ▶ 57:17
Future cost declines come from five stacked sources: better and bigger resources, resources closer to existing grid infrastructure, faster cheaper drilling, AI applied to operations, and software deployed across development and asset operation.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highZanskarcost curvegrid interconnection
So what: Proximity to grid infrastructure as a screening criterion is the underappreciated one — interconnection cost is now a first-order site-selection variable.
Conventional vs enhanced geothermal 5
Artificially created permeability in engineered geothermal systems is one to multiple orders of magnitude worse than natural permeability — fracking-style hairline cracks versus natural openings that can be cavern-sized.
causal🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highEGSFervopermeabilityfracking
So what: A physical ceiling on EGS flow rates and therefore on EGS cost — the core bear case against the enhanced-geothermal investment thesis.
“the type of permeability that we've been able to create artificially in engineered systems is an order of magnitude and sometimes multiple orders of magnitude less than the type of natural permeability that we find in the earth” ▶ 33:33
Asked directly whether a good natural geothermal resource will always be cheaper than any conceivable EGS site, Hyland answers that it is definitely true today and probably indefinitely into the future.
contrarian🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highEGSconventional geothermalcost curve
So what: A permanent, not transitional, cost hierarchy claim — if right, EGS is capped as a secondary technology rather than the sector's future.
“Definitely true today and we think probably indefinitely into the future.” ▶ 35:03
The supporting analogy is oil and gas: decades into the shale revolution, the conventional Ghawar field in Saudi Arabia still produces at a significantly lower cost per barrel than the best US unconventional shale fields — natural permeability beating induced permeability.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highGhawarSaudi ArabiaSaudi Aramcoshale
So what: A well-tested precedent transferred to a new sector — the strongest single argument for prioritizing conventional geothermal exploration over engineered systems.
“the conventional fields like the gaw oil field in Saudi Arabia still produce oil at a significantly lower cost per barrel than even our best unconventional shell fields” ▶ 35:13
Against the skeptic case that only small leftover pockets remain, Hyland points out that one of the most valuable companies in history was created by an oil discovery made a hundred years after the first peak-oil declaration — Ghawar and Aramco — arguing the best resources are still ahead.
contrarian🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highSaudi AramcoGhawarpeak oil
So what: The explicit bull framing to institutional investors: this is a discovery business with Aramco-scale precedent, not a niche-site roll-up.
“it's more likely that the best resources are still ahead of us” ▶ 50:20
Edwards endorses Quaise's deep-drilling thesis on the grounds that conventional tools break down at extreme depth — scientific wells to 30,000-35,000 feet took years because bits died every few hundred feet and holes collapsed — while conventional oil and gas fields already operate to 20,000 feet.
framework🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: mediumQuaisedeep drillingoil and gas
So what: Defines the depth boundary — roughly 20,000 ft — that separates today's financeable conventional geothermal from speculative deep-drilling technology bets.
Exploration finance structure 5
Geothermal's historical financing problem was that traditional project finance required all exploration and wellfield risk to be retired before capital would arrive, forcing exploration to fund itself at punitive cost of capital and loading soft costs into the final levelized cost of electricity.
causal🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highproject financecost of capitalLCOEgeothermal
So what: Names cost of capital, not geology, as a principal driver of geothermal's uncompetitive LCOE — meaning a financing innovation moves the cost curve as much as a technology one.
The key unlock with financiers was not accuracy but quantifiability — Hyland says lenders care more about whether you can robustly quantify the uncertainty than about what the actual hit rate is, because a quantified risk profile can be priced.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highproject financerisk pricingZanskar
So what: A generalizable insight for any frontier-asset class: statistical legibility, not performance, is what converts an uninvestable activity into a financeable one.
“they care more about just can you actually quantify the uncertainty to a robust degree more so than what the actual accuracy is” ▶ 44:00
Zanskar's development capital facility is a rotating fund that sits ahead of bank-style project finance: it proves out a resource, then is replaced on that project by cheaper debt and tax equity, recycling the capital back into earlier-stage projects, with an accordion feature allowing larger draws as facility asset value grows.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highdevelopment capitaltax equityproject financeZanskar
So what: A replicable capital structure for pre-bankable resource development — the closest thing in the episode to a new financial product for the energy transition.
The facility manages exploration risk through portfolio diversification rather than per-project certainty — failures are absorbed by successes, with the statistical base rate determining how to size and diversify the portfolio.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highportfolio riskexploration finance
So what: Converts a binary wildcat risk into an insurable, poolable risk — the same transformation that made oil exploration and venture capital financeable.
Hyland's stated ambition is that quantifying industry-wide performance — including legacy-tool performance — turns geothermal into an investable asset class, and that the facility structure spreads beyond Zanskar.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: mediumasset classinstitutional investorsgeothermal
So what: Watch for third-party adoption of this structure as the leading indicator that geothermal capital formation has genuinely changed.
“Our hope is that by quantifying not just our performance but really the industry even with legacy tools performance that you make this a more investable asset class.” ▶ 45:37
Offtake and data center demand 5
Zanskar signed a 100 MW portfolio PPA with NV Energy, structured so the power can be delivered from any combination of a basket of already-discovered and leased sites rather than from a single well or from speculative future discoveries.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highNV EnergyPPANevadaZanskar
So what: The portfolio PPA is the contractual counterpart to the portfolio financing — a template that lets pre-development resources sign firm offtake.
“this is what they call a portfolio PPA” ▶ 47:19
NV Energy's primary motivation for seeking geothermal is new hyperscaler data-center demand in its territory looking for carbon-free baseload — and another developer has signed roughly 150 MW with the same utility.
causal🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highNV Energyhyperscalersdata centersAmazonNevada
So what: Direct confirmation that data-center load growth is now the marginal buyer setting geothermal procurement in Nevada — the clearest demand signal in the sector.
“one of the primary reasons NV Energy was seeking out geothermal power is because of new hyperscaler demand in their geography” ▶ 48:13
Selling to a regulated utility rather than a private offtaker lets geothermal monetize ancillary services on top of energy — spinning reserves and voltage support — because it is genuinely firm power.
framework🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highNV Energyancillary servicesspinning reservesgrid
So what: Ancillary revenue stacking is a structural margin advantage geothermal holds over wind and solar PPAs — relevant to comparative project returns.
Demand is broadening past data centers to manufacturing facilities and to utilities seeking decarbonization or grid resilience — Zanskar's Lightning Dock output goes to PNM in New Mexico purely to meet that utility's own needs.
data_point🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highPNMNew Mexicomanufacturingutilities
So what: Suggests offtake demand is not a single-customer data-center bubble, reducing concentration risk in the revenue thesis.
No greenfield geothermal power plant has been commissioned in the United States in roughly ten years, and Zanskar's stated goal is to get half a dozen greenfield projects to commissioning within the next few years.
prediction🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highgreenfield geothermalUnited StatesZanskar
So what: The single most trackable near-term milestone for the whole thesis — greenfield commissioning is what would prove the industry can actually scale.
“it's been about 10 years since anybody has turned on a green field geothermal power plant in the United States” ▶ 1:00:10
Public land and policy dependence 4
Geothermal has retained bipartisan support, tax credits and DOE engagement at a moment when wind and solar are being actively disadvantaged, because it is small-footprint, baseload, carbon-free, domestic and free of at-risk foreign supply chains.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highDOEtax creditswindsolarenergy policy
So what: A rare politically durable clean-energy exposure in the current US policy environment — the relative-policy-risk trade versus wind and solar.
“geothermal has kind of remained a bipartisan love child” ▶ 56:01
90% of identified US geothermal resources sit on public land — mostly Bureau of Land Management acreage rather than national parks — making the federal government a single landlord that is simultaneously the sector's largest risk and its largest opportunity.
framework🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highBLMpublic landsfederal governmentpermitting
So what: Single-counterparty land exposure means federal permitting policy is effectively a switch on the entire sector's growth rate — the key non-geological risk to underwrite.
“90% of identified geothermal resources are on public land” ▶ 57:52
Both the Biden administration at the tail end of its term and the Trump administration in its early days improved geothermal permitting, giving the sector current tailwinds — but Edwards says the regulatory process remains slow and outdated from years of the industry being ignored.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highBiden administrationTrump administrationpermittingBLM
So what: Permitting streamlining, not resource or technology, is the identified trigger for a sector boom — a specific policy catalyst to watch.
“if we can get that streamlined, the industry will boom.” ▶ 58:59
Edwards flags an internal worry that geothermal may now be attracting too much attention, which could turn its current bipartisan immunity into the same political exposure wind and solar face.
contrarian🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: mediumenergy policygeothermalpolitical risk
So what: A candid acknowledgment that the policy premium embedded in geothermal valuations is a function of obscurity and may not survive scale.
Geographic expansion limits 4
Zanskar concedes the resource is a basin-and-range story confined to the US West for the next decade or potentially two, limited primarily by drilling costs rather than by geology.
thesis🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highbasin and rangewestern United Statesdrilling costs
So what: Sets a realistic geographic boundary on the thesis and makes drilling cost the gating variable for any expansion beyond the West.
“For the next decade or potentially two, that's true. Primarily driven by drilling costs” ▶ 51:00
Hyland's answer to geographic concentration is that if you hold a terawatt of the most cost-advantaged power on the planet, demand relocates to the supply rather than the reverse.
thesis🗣 Carl Hyland, CEO Zanskarconviction: highwestern United Statesdata centersindustrial load
So what: The load-migration argument that underpins siting data centers and energy-intensive manufacturing in the intermountain West.
“we also believe demand will come to where you are” ▶ 51:16
Every deep scientific well drilled — the German well to 10-11 km and the deeper Russian well — unexpectedly hit water and wellbore stability problems, meaning permeability exists at depths where geologists assumed rock would be dry and sealed.
data_point🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: highKola-type deep wellsGermanyRussiapermeability
So what: The strongest physical evidence that conventional geothermal could eventually work far outside the West — heat is a given, and permeability now appears less depth-limited than assumed.
“all of those cases where they went deep, they all ran into conditions that they didn't expect” ▶ 52:40
Because nobody has drilled below a certain depth in places like Iowa, subsurface conditions there are simply unknown, and Edwards argues it is likely rather than merely possible that permeability exists at some depth in geographies never considered geothermal.
prediction🗣 Joel Edwards, CTO Zanskarconviction: mediumIowaeastern United Statespermeability
So what: The long-dated optionality in the thesis — a genuinely national geothermal resource, contingent entirely on drilling cost declines.

Cognitive Revolution · Nathan Labenz 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

Nathan Goes to China #3: US-China Relations, the Art of the AI Deal & the Road to Pax Robotica196 min50 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Nathan Labenz, back from two weeks in China, argues Chinese technological strength is a reversion to historical norm rather than a post-ChatGPT fast-follow, that US chip export controls are only marginally effective while being strategically counterproductive, and that ahead of a Trump-Xi summit America should trade chips and chipmaking know-how for Chinese solar, battery, robotics and manufacturing expertise. His core risk thesis is paradigm divergence: if the two AI hardware and algorithm stacks decouple far enough that neither side can read the other's capabilities, today's competition becomes a genuine arms race.
China Net Assessment · 4Depth of the Chinese AI Stack · 6China's Structural Constraints · 5Chinese Perspective and Olive Branches · 10Export Controls: Efficacy vs Blowback · 7Paradigm Divergence Risk · 5State Capacity with American Characteristics · 5The AI Deal and Trust Technology · 8
China Net Assessment 4
Measured in purchasing power parity China's economy is roughly $45 trillion versus about $30 trillion for the US, inverting the nominal comparison of roughly $20 trillion versus $30 trillion, so the more accurate framing is that China's economy is about 50% larger than America's rather than 50% smaller.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinaUnited StatesGDPpurchasing power parity
So what: Anyone sizing Chinese end markets, industrial capacity or domestic consumption off nominal dollar GDP is systematically understating the addressable base for Chinese domestic champions.
China's current position at the technology frontier is a reversion to its multi-century historical norm, and the late-20th-century period of Chinese weakness that shaped Western intuitions was the anomaly, which implies any strategy premised on containing or delaying Chinese technological development can work only temporarily.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinaUnited Statestechnology policy
So what: Investment theses that price Chinese tech as a structurally capped or containable competitor are betting against the base rate; expect Chinese entrants in essentially every frontier category.
“And the reality is really the reverse. It was the downtime that was the exception.” ▶ 23:53
The Chinese government currently enjoys strong popular legitimacy under 'mandate of heaven' logic because it has delivered two generations of rising prosperity, making Western strategies premised on internal regime change or popular uprising fantasies comparable to the recent US misread of Iran.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinese Communist PartyChinaIran
So what: Scenarios that assume Chinese domestic instability as a path to US advantage should be discounted; China risk should be modeled as continuity, not fragility.
The proposal to solve America's AI talent gap by offering green cards to Chinese researchers misreads current conditions, because top Chinese AI talent increasingly prefers to stay or return home, as illustrated by Moonshot's founder doing a PhD in the US and then building his company in China.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highMoonshot AIChinaUnited Statesimmigration policy
So what: Talent-arbitrage assumptions embedded in US AI-labor forecasts are stale; Chinese labs should be modeled as retaining, not leaking, elite researchers.
Depth of the Chinese AI Stack 6
AI first appeared in a Chinese five-year plan adopted in 2016, the same year Google declared itself AI-first and only a few years after the deep learning breakthrough, so Chinese AI capability reflects a decade of coordinated state prioritization rather than a post-ChatGPT scramble.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinaGoogleSundar Pichaifive-year plandeep learning
So what: Treat Chinese frontier labs as the output of a ten-year national program, which means capability gains should persist through chip constraints rather than collapse with them.
Roughly equal numbers of American and Chinese companies have shipped frontier or near-frontier LLMs in the last couple of years — about 11 American and 12 Chinese — and the Chinese talent bench is probably the deepest in the world once Chinese nationals working at American labs are counted.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumOpenAIAnthropicDeepSeekMoonshot AIMiniMaxChinaUnited States
So what: Model-layer commoditization pressure is structurally higher than a two-lab duopoly framing implies, which compresses pricing power for proprietary API providers.
Chinese companies were already competitive with American ones on the SuperGLUE leaderboard as of 2022, with a Chinese company holding the number one spot on its final update, meaning the apparent US lead visible at ChatGPT's launch was a brief anomaly rather than a structural gap.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highSuperGLUEChinaOpenAIChatGPT
So what: Historical benchmark evidence undercuts the 'China only fast-follows' narrative that underpins bullish US-lab moat arguments.
DeepSeek's R1-Zero paper published in January 2025 showed that pure reinforcement learning without supervised fine-tuning can produce metacognitive chain-of-thought behavior, demonstrating genuine Chinese algorithmic innovation well beyond distillation.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highDeepSeekR1-Zeroreinforcement learningreasoning models
So what: Chinese labs are contributing paradigm-level research, so the assumption that restricting compute alone caps their capability trajectory is weak.
China's open-weights model releases have made it the leading global provider of AI public goods, a posture that is simultaneously a sincere aspiration and a strategic positioning move, and one that could be reversed at any time.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumDeepSeekMoonshot AIMiniMaxopen weightsChina
So what: Free frontier-adjacent weights suppress global willingness to pay for proprietary APIs; the tail risk for open-source-dependent businesses is a Chinese policy reversal to closed releases.
The Chinese government blocked and unwound Meta's roughly $2 billion acquisition of agent startup Manus on critical-technology grounds, and Chinese insiders viewed it as an own goal that damages venture exit expectations and may push founders to establish themselves in Singapore or the US before starting companies.
anecdote🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumManusMetaChinese governmentventure capitalSingapore
So what: A blocked-exit precedent raises the risk premium on Chinese venture assets and could shift AI startup formation offshore — watch Singapore-domiciled Chinese-founder startups.
China's Structural Constraints 5
China's aging population is the dominant domestic driver of its AI and robotics investment, with elder care, AI doctors and companionship products framed as the answer to a demographic gap where one grandchild must support four grandparents and two parents.
causal🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highAnt Grouphumanoid roboticselder careAI companionshipChina
So what: Demographics, not geopolitics, is the demand engine for Chinese robotics and health AI; that demand is non-cyclical and policy-supported.
China's one-child demographic structure is a meaningful brake on military adventurism because sending an only child and only grandchild to die in a war is the single thing most likely to cost the government its legitimacy, and Beijing also has genuine doubt about the untested strength of its own military after Russia's failure in Ukraine.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumChinaTaiwanRussiaUkraineTyler Cowen
So what: Argues for a lower implied probability of a Taiwan invasion than consensus geopolitical risk pricing assumes, which matters for semiconductor supply-chain risk premia.
The absence of any visible successor to the 73-year-old President Xi is a major unpriced source of future Chinese instability, and one Chinese interlocutor argued war is most likely after a leadership transition when a new president needs to consolidate power.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumXi JinpingChinese Communist Partysuccession risk
So what: The measures producing near-term Chinese stability are pushing tail risk into the transition window — a long-dated geopolitical risk rather than a near-term one.
China is respected but not trusted internationally, with observers in Uganda and Brazil describing Chinese engagement as extractive and expressing doubt that open-source model releases will continue rather than being a bait-and-switch before proprietary API fees begin.
anecdote🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumChinaBrazilUgandaopen source AIUnited States
So what: The trust deficit is China's binding constraint on winning a global AI sphere of influence, and the bait-and-switch fear is a real adoption risk for firms building on Chinese open weights.
Chinese society is more socially conservative about frontier biotech and human enhancement than Western projections assume — He Jiankui received three years in prison and a 3 million yuan fine for 2018 human embryo gene editing, and tech-forward young Chinese professionals expressed clear reluctance about neural implants.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumHe JiankuiNeuralinkgene editingembryo selectionChina
So what: The 'China will race ahead on enhancement so we must too' argument used to justify US deregulation lacks evidentiary support.
Chinese Perspective and Olive Branches 10
The operative Chinese strategic doctrine is that falling behind the technology frontier or becoming dependent on foreign powers for critical technology is existentially dangerous — a lesson from the century of humiliation that remains in living memory since the cultural revolution ended in 1976 — which makes the chip ban hit a political nerve far beyond its economic cost.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinacentury of humiliationexport controlssemiconductors
So what: Chinese domestic semiconductor investment is near price-insensitive and will continue regardless of export-control relief — a durable bid under the domestic chip supply chain.
The second half of Dario Amodei's 'Machines of Loving Grace' — building a coalition of democracies to box out China until the West can make an offer it cannot refuse — reads to Chinese elites as an explicit threat to repeat the century of humiliation, and will strengthen rather than deter Chinese resolve.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highAnthropicDario AmodeiMachines of Loving GraceChina
So what: US lab rhetoric is itself an input to Chinese escalation, meaning the policy positions of frontier labs carry geopolitical externalities markets don't price.
Chinese officials read US civil-military fusion complaints and Huawei spying accusations as transparent hypocrisy given the American military-industrial complex, the Defense Production Act and the Snowden revelations of US spying on its own allies, which hardens the conclusion that Washington's real goal is containment.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highHuaweiSnowdenDefense Production Actcivil-military fusion
So what: Explains why targeted Chinese-company sanctions produce escalation rather than compliance, and why Huawei has become a national-pride asset rather than a negotiable one.
“it's the classic meme, right, of their wicked civil military fusion, our blessed military-industrial complex.” ▶ 1:13:27
The case against model distillation is weak because American labs appropriated the entire written inheritance of humanity to build their models and now claim knowledge cannot flow back out of their products, and state enforcement of an anti-distillation policy is inappropriate even if individual companies may refuse service.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicdistillationintellectual property
So what: Signals that legal and policy moats around frontier model outputs are fragile, weakening a key defensibility argument for closed-model valuations.
Chinese national pride is a hard constraint on how much insult the relationship can absorb, with the head of a Chinese AI policy think tank describing the pattern of hosting Trump lavishly only to see new tariffs imposed on his return, and warning verbatim that it could lead to war.
anecdote🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highDonald TrumpXi JinpingtariffsChina
So what: Tariff announcements timed after diplomatic gestures carry disproportionate escalation risk relative to their economic content.
“There could be war. I'm really worried about that.” ▶ 2:24:09
Asked what single action would most repair the US-China relationship, a Chinese AI policy think tank head answered that he would relax the export controls first.
anecdote🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highexport controlsChinaUnited StatesNvidia
So what: Identifies export-control relief as the highest-leverage deliverable at a summit, and therefore the most likely surprise catalyst for chip exporters.
“The first thing I would do is I would relax the export controls.” ▶ 2:24:30
Xi Jinping opened his WAIC speech by crediting Dartmouth College in the United States with founding the field of AI and publicly argued against overstretching national security concepts in AI, which China watchers read as deliberate conciliatory signaling.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highXi JinpingWAICDartmouthAI governance
So what: In the Chinese system such a speech gives officials throughout the bureaucracy something citable, so it functions as a genuine permission structure for cooperation rather than mere rhetoric.
“We should jointly oppose overstretching the national security concept in the field of AI and placing one country's security over that of others.” ▶ 1:28:30
Across two weeks of meetings in China nobody framed AI as a race, claimed China was ahead, or described a plan to build decisive advantage and force the US into an alliance — a stark asymmetry with American rhetoric — even though Chinese interlocutors freely acknowledged US leads, including a car salesperson volunteering that Tesla's autonomy is better.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highTeslaChinaAI race narrativeautonomous driving
So what: The 'China is racing us' premise driving US AI policy is not reflected in on-the-ground Chinese discourse, suggesting the race is partly a self-fulfilling American construction.
“I really never even heard anybody talk about AI as a race.” ▶ 1:32:09
China is institutionalizing international AI safety research, with a new Tsinghua University AI safety hub explicitly citing Constellation in Berkeley, LISA in London and SASH in Singapore as inspirations, funding two-way researcher exchange, and seating a European professor among its five founding board members.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highTsinghua UniversityConstellationLISASASHAI safety
So what: Concrete institutional evidence against the claim that China will never slow down or engage on safety, which is the load-bearing assumption behind US racing policy.
The US declaration that only American citizens could use Anthropic's Fable model — which ended with Anthropic pulling it entirely — was a global wake-up call that made other countries newly wary of depending on US AI providers subject to executive whim.
causal🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highAnthropicFableUnited Statesopen weightssovereign AI
So what: Political access risk is now a real procurement criterion internationally, structurally favoring open-weights and sovereign AI deployments over US API dependence.
Export Controls: Efficacy vs Blowback 7
Meituan trained its LongCat model exclusively on Huawei Ascend chips, providing an existence proof that near-frontier training runs are already possible on domestic Chinese silicon with state-subsidized electricity absorbing the efficiency overhead.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highMeituanLongCatHuawei AscendNvidiatraining compute
So what: Undermines the core premise that training-grade compute remains a US chokepoint, and is bearish for the long-run strategic value of export controls.
The stated rationale for export controls has ratcheted down over time from preventing Chinese military AI, to preventing frontier model development, to merely limiting how many AI agents China can run — a drift that reads internationally as an effort to suppress ordinary Chinese businesses and consumers.
causal🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highexport controlsUnited StatesChinaAI agents
So what: Goalpost migration signals the policy is failing on its original terms while accumulating diplomatic cost — a setup for eventual relaxation.
Chinese inference capacity is effectively abundant for consumer use, with free tiers of DeepSeek, MiniMax and Kimi continuously available on the ground in China, even though Kimi K3 briefly suspended new paid signups due to a chip-driven scaling limit.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highDeepSeekMiniMaxMoonshot AIKimi K3inference compute
So what: Inference, unlike training, is not a binding constraint in China, so Chinese consumer AI adoption will not be throttled by export controls.
Export controls have chilled US-China AI safety research collaboration, with American safety organizations declining engagement out of fear that the US government rather than the Chinese government will prosecute them, often on excessively conservative legal advice.
causal🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highAI safety organizationsexport controlsTrump administrationChina
So what: A low-cost unilateral clarification that safety collaborations are permitted is available before any summit and would be a tangible de-escalation signal to watch for.
Banning Chinese open-weights models in the US would be self-harming, forcing domestic firms onto more expensive American APIs when US open models from Nvidia and Thinking Machines are not yet at the level of the Chinese ones, and pressuring other countries into the same ban would likely push them into China's camp.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinese open-weights modelsOpenAIAnthropicNvidiaThinking Machines
So what: A model-ban policy risk exists but would be value-destructive for US application builders even as it would be a windfall for US API pricing.
The better mechanism for managing Chinese model risk is a market-priced insurance requirement rather than a ban, and insurance rates between American and Chinese models would probably not differ dramatically, with any gap reflecting the safety classifiers and misuse prevention that American labs bundle in.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumAI insuranceOpenAIAnthropicKimi K3risk pricing
So what: An AI liability-insurance market is a plausible emerging category and the clean way to monetize the claimed safety premium of US models.
“let's maybe have a market mechanism discover what the appropriate relative prices are.” ▶ 2:19:41
The argument that selling chips into China creates dependency — as advanced by Jensen Huang and David Sacks — no longer holds, because China is now irrevocably committed to building its own chip ecosystem after the export controls, so chips should be traded for concrete concessions rather than given away for leverage that no longer exists.
contrarian🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highJensen HuangDavid SacksNvidiaHuaweiexport controls
So what: Reframes chip sales as a bargaining chip with a shrinking expiry window rather than a durable lock-in strategy, arguing against the dependency thesis Nvidia bulls invoke.
Paradigm Divergence Risk 5
The most underappreciated strategic asset in US-China AI is mutual legibility — both sides currently build on the same paradigm, publish research in English, and release inspectable weights — and preserving that legibility should be the primary near-term AI policy objective.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highopen weightsinterpretabilityChinaUnited States
So what: Watch for Chinese labs shifting to Chinese-language publication or closed weights as the leading indicator that AI competition is turning into an arms race.
If Chinese hardware diverges onto Huawei silicon while the US stays on Nvidia, hardware-algorithm co-design will push the two ecosystems onto different branches of the tech tree, and the resulting inability to assess each other's capabilities is the mechanism by which today's competition becomes a cold or hot war.
causal🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highNvidiaHuaweihardware lotterytransformershardware-algorithm co-design
So what: Divergence risk is the second-order cost of export controls that markets ignore; it argues for keeping China at least partly on the American chip ecosystem as a stability mechanism.
“Imagine a world where it was like we don't even know the shape of their AI. We don't even know the architecture.” ▶ 2:49:18
In a decoupled world China would be far better at keeping technological secrets than the United States, because America's openness to immigrants and the global prevalence of English make it structurally easier to spy on — the same asymmetry by which the Soviets obtained the bomb.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumUnited StatesChinaespionageAI security
So what: A secrecy race is a game America is positioned to lose, which weakens the strategic case for pursuing a closed-lead strategy.
“We are much easier for China to spy on than China is for us to spy on.” ▶ 2:50:09
The entire chip-containment strategy only makes sense if superintelligence and recursive self-improvement arrive within roughly one to three years and confer a decisive, world-order-rewriting advantage; on any longer timeline China closes the chip gap and the policy's main legacy is accumulated hostility.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highAnthropicDario Amodeirecursive self-improvementexport controlssuperintelligence
So what: Makes export-control advocacy an explicit bet on short AI timelines; investors can treat its persistence as a market-implied timeline signal.
Chinese researchers themselves say tech-tree divergence is not yet a worry but will become one precisely when China's domestic chip ecosystem gets good enough to operate entirely independently.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: mediumChinaHuaweisemiconductorsAI paradigm
So what: Gives a concrete tripwire — Chinese chip self-sufficiency — for when geopolitical AI risk should be repriced upward.
State Capacity with American Characteristics 5
The US should not try to copy Chinese high-speed rail, which is beyond current American state capacity, and should instead pursue full self-driving as its mobility moonshot because it uses existing technology, requires no eminent domain, and addresses roughly 30,000 annual US highway deaths.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highTeslafull self-drivinghigh-speed railCaliforniaautonomous vehicles
So what: Frames FSD as the politically achievable American infrastructure program, supporting a policy tailwind for autonomy deployment over rail capex.
“China can have the highspeed rail. We should have the full self-driving.” ▶ 1:51:00
Adversarial US regulation imposes a large hidden tax on frontier technology firms, with antitrust and privacy investigations having paralyzed Microsoft for a generation and created enough internal friction at Meta that a simple Llama 3 red-teaming contract required a shipped laptop and extensive data-privacy training.
anecdote🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highMetaMicrosoftLlama 3antitrustregulators
So what: Regulatory overhang is a real drag on execution speed at large-cap tech, and any shift toward facilitative regulation is a margin and velocity tailwind.
The United States is dangerously dependent on Elon Musk's companies across space, electric vehicles, batteries, solar, neural implants and AI, such that subtracting them from the US side of the US-China economic comparison leaves America looking substantially weaker.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highElon MuskTeslaSpaceXNeuralinkUnited StatesChina
So what: US competitiveness in several strategic sectors is effectively single-founder concentration risk, and political attacks on Musk carry direct national-competitiveness costs.
“But if you subtract Elon companies from the United States side of the US China economic competition, we look a lot worse.” ▶ 1:55:38
The US should reverse the flow and pursue tech transfer from China, admitting Chinese EV, battery and robotics firms on condition of joint ventures, US-sited manufacturing and knowledge sharing — the same terms China once imposed on Western firms — rather than maintaining the current ban on Chinese cars.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highChinese EV makersbatteriesroboticsDetroitjoint ventures
So what: A policy reversal here would shock US auto and battery incumbents while accelerating domestic battery cost curves; it is the natural quid pro quo for chip sales.
Chinese-style surveillance delivers genuine benefits — near-absence of street crime and recoverable lost property — and the US needs a technical synthesis such as weakly encrypted footage that is cheap to decode case-by-case but prohibitively expensive to analyze in bulk.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: exploratoryFlock SafetysurveillanceSan Franciscocryptography
So what: Points to privacy-preserving surveillance architecture as an emerging product category as US cities expand camera networks.
“what does surveillance with American characteristics look like?” ▶ 2:01:57
The AI Deal and Trust Technology 8
The central trade available to the US is selling chips and possibly chipmaking know-how into China in exchange for Chinese solar, battery, robotics and electric vehicle expertise plus China-sited-to-US manufacturing, effectively recoupling the two economies without creating unacceptable dependencies.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highNvidiasolarbatteriesroboticselectric vehiclesChinaUnited States
So what: Defines the shape of a plausible summit outcome; movement in this direction is simultaneously bullish chip exporters and disruptive to US clean-tech incumbents.
Formal methods and AI-generated proofs could create a security standard under which Huawei can demonstrate it cannot exfiltrate data, opening a path to readmitting Huawei to Western markets on verifiable rather than political grounds.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: exploratoryHuaweiformal verificationAI code generationtelecom infrastructure
So what: Formal verification as trust-enabling technology is an emerging category whose success would reshape which hardware vendors are politically admissible.
Confidential computing could enable AI auditing of frontier labs and even governments without exposing trade secrets, running analytical prompts over usage logs and sensitive documents so an outside party learns whether something dangerous is happening without seeing the raw content.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: exploratoryconfidential computingAnthropicAI auditingOpenAI
So what: Verification infrastructure is a prerequisite for any real AI arms-control agreement, which makes confidential-computing and attestation vendors strategically positioned.
Jointly controlled research compute sited in a neutral country such as Switzerland or Singapore, staffed by American, Chinese and global researchers, would maintain shared understanding of AI capabilities and serve as a natural testbed for hardware verification systems.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: exploratorySwitzerlandSingaporehardware verificationAI governanceDaniel Kokotajlo
So what: A concrete institutional proposal to watch for at the Trump-Xi summit; neutral-jurisdiction compute would be a novel demand channel for datacenter capacity.
Antitrust fear currently blocks frontier labs from coordinating on safety, and the government should explicitly carve out safety collaboration and frontier pacing from antitrust enforcement while still policing price collusion.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highantitrustOpenAIAnthropicAI safety
So what: An antitrust safe harbor is the enabling condition for any coordinated industry slowdown, and its absence is why capability racing continues by default.
OpenAI has suspended its largest-scale reinforcement learning training runs, and a comparable move by Anthropic would test whether frontier labs can coordinate on pacing rather than treating a rival's pause as the moment to build a decisive lead.
data_point🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicreinforcement learningfrontier training
So what: A voluntary pause at the largest RL scale is a direct signal about near-term capability trajectory and training-compute demand from the largest buyers.
Both the US and China have a shared interest in never training general-purpose AI to achieve military objectives by any means necessary, because the same optimization pressure that makes models deceptive and collusive in economic settings would produce exactly the systems capable of the takeover both governments most fear.
thesis🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highautonomous weaponsmilitary AIdronesUkraineAI safety
So what: Identifies a plausible first concrete US-China AI agreement — a no-go zone on open-ended military AI objectives — that costs neither side capability today.
Belief itself is a policy variable in an era where scaling laws and Sam Altman's once-absurd $7 trillion datacenter buildout were hyperstitioned into existence, so reflexive certainty that no deal with China is possible is itself the binding constraint on reaching one.
framework🗣 Nathan Labenzconviction: highSam AltmanOpenAIscaling lawsdata centersChina
So what: Argues that consensus impossibility around US-China AI agreements is a soft rather than hard constraint, meaning surprise diplomatic outcomes are underpriced.
“we'll never be able to make a deal with China. Not with that attitude, you won't.” ▶ 2:31:17

The Compound and Friends · Josh Brown 1 episode(s) · 50 insights

The Most Interesting Macro Moment of My Lifetime | TCAF 25966 min50 insights2026-09-11
▶ Watch on YouTube
Jens Nordvig (president of Vanda, founder of Exante Data) argues the defining new macro fact is that hyperscalers are now issuing roughly as much long-end debt as the US Treasury, so AI capex has become a sovereign-scale competitor for capital that is pushing the long end to multi-decade highs and leaving equities close to a non-linear break point. Around that he lays out a weak-dollar-plus-inflation policy contradiction under Bessent's unusually interventionist Treasury, names Korea as the biggest single-country macro shock of his career, and argues AI is inflationary rather than disinflationary for as long as the buildout runs. Josh Brown and Michael Batnick counter with card-spend and same-store-sales data showing the consumer is fine even as discretionary retail stocks are destroyed.
Hyperscaler issuance as sovereign-scale supply · 7Long-end break point for equities · 7Bessent's interventionist Treasury · 5Debt-driven FX differentiation · 5AI as an inflation impulse · 8Korea as the standout macro trade · 3AI equity leadership rotation · 4Consumer: card data versus stock prices · 6AI and the research/data business model · 5
Hyperscaler issuance as sovereign-scale supply 7
Hyperscalers are now issuing about as much long-end debt on a flow basis as the entire US government, making a handful of private companies a sovereign-scale competitor for long-duration capital for the first time.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highhyperscalersUS TreasuryGoogleMicrosoftAmazonMetalong-end yields
So what: Any rates model that only tracks Treasury supply is now missing roughly half the long-end issuance; duration supply risk is structurally higher than deficit forecasts alone imply.
“we now have as much bond issuance by hyperscalers in the long end of the curve as the United States government” ▶ 23:10
Hyperscaler bond issuance scales non-linearly with capex because the first tranche of spending is funded from free cash flow, so every incremental $10bn of capex maps into issuance with a higher and higher beta — implying dramatically more supply next year than this year.
framework🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highhyperscalerscapexcredit marketslong-end yields
So what: Forecast AI-related credit supply off the gap between capex and operating cash flow, not off capex growth rates; the issuance curve steepens even if capex growth merely holds.
“So next year is going to be like dramatically more than this year.” ▶ 25:47
The US is running a roughly 6% of GDP deficit — on par with the largest deficits of the Reagan era — and is simultaneously being matched in the long end by private AI issuers, so the era of clipping carry in credit in a low-inflation world is over.
causal🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highUS fiscal deficitcredit spreadshyperscalersTreasury market
So what: Argues against buy-and-hold carry trades in credit and for expecting term premium to stay elevated; 'always able to sell your carry position' is a regime assumption that no longer holds.
“those days are just gone now we have such competition for capital that yields are being pushed higher” ▶ 24:54
The clearest signal that the AI bull market has broken would be a hyperscaler bond deal that comes to market undersubscribed, prices wide, or gets pulled — a corporate version of bond vigilantes that has not happened yet.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highAlphabetMetaAmazonOraclecredit markets
So what: A specific, observable trigger to monitor: primary-market reception of hyperscaler debt, not equity price action, is the leading indicator for the end of the cycle.
“the market topped when finally like somebody said no to Amazon in the bond market” ▶ 1:03:35
Oracle is viewed by many investors as the weakest credit among the large AI buildout players, and its credit spreads have already been volatile.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumOraclecredit spreadshyperscalers
So what: Oracle CDS/spreads are the cheapest available proxy hedge on AI-capex credit stress and the first place a funding squeeze should show up.
There is an ironic endgame in which the bull market ends because the hyperscalers converted themselves from the biggest cash-generating companies in history into the largest debtor companies in history, deliberately.
thesis🗣 hostconviction: mediumhyperscalersAmazonMetaAlphabetMicrosoft
So what: Reframes mega-cap tech balance sheets from a source of safety to a source of systemic duration risk — a reason to re-underwrite 'quality' allocations that lean on net-cash tech.
“It would be it would be very ironic and interesting if the bull market for stocks comes to an end because the hyperscalers went from being like the biggest cash generating companies in history” ▶ 1:03:08
The White House will not rein in hyperscaler issuance because it needs AI capex to carry US growth, so its only lever is Treasury buyback management — which Nordvig expects to become a problem heading into next year.
causal🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumWhite HousehyperscalersUS TreasuryScott Bessent
So what: Policy will not cap AI-related supply, so the adjustment has to come through price (yields) — favoring steepener and duration-risk expressions over a policy-rescue thesis.
Long-end break point for equities 7
The 10-year is at 4.92%, a level last seen in October 2023, and the 30-year is at 2007-era highs with real rates the highest since 2007 — but with far higher debt levels than the last time yields were here.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: high10-year Treasury30-year Treasuryreal rates
So what: Same yield, different debt stock: the economy's tolerance for a 5% 10-year is structurally lower than in 2007 or even 2023, so the pain threshold arrives at a lower level.
Long-end yield behavior over the past three to four months has been divorced from economic data and Fed communication and is happening globally, which Nordvig reads as evidence that debt levels are starting to drive asset behavior directly.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highlong-end yieldsFederal Reserveglobal bond markets
So what: Data-dependent rates trading is losing its edge; positioning and supply/flow analysis matter more than payrolls or CPI prints for the long end right now.
Equities are close to a non-linearity: another rapid 20 to 35 basis point move in the long end would put the market in what Nordvig calls danger territory for an equity puke, and he would be very surprised if a couple more weeks of this yield move produced no equity reaction.
prediction🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highS&P 500long-end yieldsequity volatility
So what: A quantified trigger level: watch for a fast 20-35bp long-end move as the signal to buy equity downside protection while VIX is still 18.
“we're very close to to an amount of move in in the yield curve where the equities get very vulnerable” ▶ 31:29
Despite yields at multi-decade highs, oil elevated, mortgage rates approaching 7% and the nastiest tech momentum crash on record last month, VIX is at 18 and the S&P equal weight is only about 4% off its highs — there is essentially no fear priced in.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: highVIXS&P 500 equal weightmomentum factormortgage rates
So what: Cheap convexity: the gap between realized macro stress and implied volatility is the trade, whether or not the bear case plays out.
The reason markets are absorbing the geopolitical and rate risk is that the index is not particularly expensive against 20%-plus earnings growth expected to persist — and the market will keep acting as if the AI capex cycle continues until a company says otherwise.
thesis🗣 hostconviction: mediumS&P 500earnings growthhyperscalers
So what: The bull case is capex-guidance-dependent, so a single hyperscaler capex cut is a larger risk event than any macro print.
Mortgage rates near 7% and a possible move to 8% next year would be a massive problem for housing, worsened by the lagged rollover of 3-4% pandemic-era mortgages that still have to reset.
prediction🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediummortgage ratesUS housing markethomebuilders
So what: The housing pain from this rate cycle is still ahead, not behind, because of the reset lag — a reason to stay cautious on housing-levered credit and cyclicals into next year.
“we have the hangover of those mortgages resetting to a higher level with a huge lag” ▶ 1:01:45
A counterargument raised on the show is that a 4-5% bond yield is historically normal and healthier than a decade of financial repression and trillions in negative-yielding debt, and that European and Japanese banks are finally making money because of it.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: mediumEuropean banksJapanese banksECBbond yields
So what: The normalization framing supports being long banks in Europe and Japan even while being cautious on duration-sensitive assets.
Bessent's interventionist Treasury 5
The US now has a de facto intervention regime in two markets at once — dollar/yen in FX and the long end of the Treasury curve via buybacks — which Nordvig says has not been seen in a very long time, while Bessent has said he does not want to intervene in oil.
framework🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highScott BessentUS TreasuryUSD/JPYTreasury buybacksoil
So what: Two markets now have a policy put and one (oil) explicitly does not — which argues for expressing macro risk through oil rather than through yen or long bonds.
Bessent's 'I am the house' claim of asymmetric information was validated: the yen intervention was executed when a September Bank of Japan hike was priced at only three or four basis points, and that hike is now priced at more than 90% probability.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highScott BessentBank of JapanUSD/JPYJapanese rates
So what: Fading US intervention in yen has become materially more dangerous — the Treasury is effectively trading on foreknowledge of BoJ policy, so respect the level rather than the flow.
“So nothing was priced when the intervention happened and now it's priced like more than 90% probability, right? So fully priced. So he wasn't wrong on this.” ▶ 34:37
The purpose of Treasury long-end buybacks is to stop a self-reinforcing debt spiral: because debt is large and they do not want to issue at ever-higher yields, they are intervening to cap long yields rather than simply retiring coupons.
causal🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highUS TreasuryScott BessentTreasury buybackslong-end yields
So what: Buyback announcements are a debt-sustainability signal, not a technical liquidity operation — size and cadence of buybacks are the tell on how worried Treasury actually is.
Preventing Bank of Japan yen intervention is also a cheaper way for the US to protect its own bond market, since Japan reportedly had to sell about $94bn of Treasuries to finance its intervention, and there is talk of Japan using lending facilities instead of selling Treasuries.
causal🗣 hostconviction: mediumBank of JapanJapanese Ministry of FinanceUS TreasuriesUSD/JPY
So what: Japanese FX operations are a direct transmission channel into US long-end supply — track MoF intervention size as a Treasury-market flow variable.
The administration is actively pursuing a weaker dollar against Asian currencies to help domestic manufacturing at the same time it has an inflation problem, an open contradiction that Nordvig says reflects too many conflicting objectives chasing too few policy levers.
contrarian🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highUS dollarAsian currenciesinflationmanufacturingScott Bessent
So what: Policy is structurally biased toward dollar weakness even in an inflationary environment — a reason to be long Asian FX versus the dollar and to expect inflation to be tolerated, not fought.
“it's pretty interesting that we have this kind of currency dimension where the administration is willing to endorse currency weakness and even actively pursue it while we have inflation problems at the same time” ▶ 36:46
Debt-driven FX differentiation 5
Currency markets have begun sorting countries by debt metrics, with low-debt currencies — Switzerland, Singapore and Australia — outperforming, and long exposure to that basket having done very well.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highSwiss francSingapore dollarAustralian dollarFXsovereign debt
So what: A tradeable expression of the debt theme that does not require timing a bond crisis: long low-debt currencies versus high-debt ones.
Among the top 30 economies the United States now has the worst debt metrics, displacing Japan, and the dollar is showing an unexplained residual weakness beyond what short-rate and risk-sentiment models predict.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highUS dollarJapansovereign debtFX models
So what: Model residuals in the dollar are the early warning that reserve-currency privilege is being repriced; watch the correlation between 30-year wobbles and dollar 'leakage.'
“It's it's actually United States now. Japan used to be the worst.” ▶ 30:08
CBO projections have US debt crossing 100% of GDP a couple of years ago and on a trajectory toward 200% within a couple of decades, which Nordvig — explicitly not a career debt scaremonger — says is now starting to show up in how assets behave.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highCBOUS debt-to-GDPasset allocation
So what: The signal is not the projection itself but the fact that allocators are beginning to respond to it — the point at which debt stops being a talking point and starts being a portfolio constraint.
“I do think you can really see assets start to behave differently here” ▶ 29:01
Gold has been decoupled from real rates since 2022 and especially mid-2023, driven first by Chinese central bank accumulation, then broader central bank buying, then a record retail ETF and options speculation wave.
framework🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highgoldChinacentral banksGLDsilver
So what: Old gold models keyed on real rates and the dollar will keep mis-signaling; flow and central-bank demand are the operative drivers now.
Nordvig would temper near-term gold targets because January's record retail options and ETF bonanza is unlikely to repeat, but expects persistent European ETF demand to support gold on a multi-quarter horizon even if real rates keep rising, because investors are unwilling to hold long bonds as their hedge.
prediction🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumgoldgold ETFsEuropereal rateslong bonds
So what: Gold is now functioning as the substitute for long duration in hedging portfolios — so it can hold up in exactly the regime that historically hurt it.
“even if real rates continue to go up, gold's going to have some support” ▶ 49:47
AI as an inflation impulse 8
AI is inflationary in the buildout phase rather than disinflationary: the buildout is creating incredible pressure on DRAM and energy prices, and with capex set to ramp further into 2027 Nordvig sees no near-term relief from that inflationary force.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highDRAMmemoryenergy pricesAI capexinflation
So what: Cuts against the consensus that AI lets the Fed cut; positions inflation-linked assets, memory, and power as the same trade rather than separate ones.
Elon Musk's own estimate is that even stepping hard on the gas on new memory capacity yields roughly a 20% supply increase within a year or two against demand needs of 100%, which Nordvig treats as credible because Musk is himself building data centers.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highElon MuskDRAMmemory capacitydata centers
So what: A 5:1 demand-to-supply-response gap argues memory pricing stays elevated for a sustained period — supportive for memory makers and for capacity-adding equipment.
“if you really step on the gas and build a lot of extra capacity maybe within a year or two you can get a 20% increase and what we need is 100%” ▶ 42:39
Five-year breakevens have moved from about 2.2% to 2.40-2.45% in recent weeks, driven by energy and AI-related inflation, leaving the Fed unable to be relaxed heading into its meeting given inflation is already above target.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highbreakevensTIPSFederal Reserveinflation expectations
So what: Breakevens re-rating without a growth scare is the cleanest confirmation that this is a supply-side inflation, which constrains the Fed's ability to cut into an equity selloff.
The administration's AI-will-be-disinflationary thesis has failed on the labor side: workers in the bottom 10th and 20th percentiles are seeing meaningful wage hikes, people in the trades have never been more employed, and Nordvig notes the labor market has surprised massively to the upside in the last couple of readings.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: highlabor marketwagestradesAIinflation
So what: Undercuts the AI-kills-jobs deflation narrative and supports higher-for-longer rates plus consumer resilience at the lower income tiers.
Warsh publicly argued before his appointment that AI's deflationary effects were a reason not to hike, and Nordvig attributes his inconsistent hawkish-then-dovish messaging partly to that framework not playing out.
causal🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumKevin WarshFederal ReserveAIinflation
So what: Fed reaction-function uncertainty is elevated because a key policymaker's prior on AI disinflation is being falsified in real time — expect noisier guidance and more meeting-to-meeting repricing.
The Iran conflict is more than six months old against an original expectation of a couple of weeks, the deal has fallen apart, the US is expending valuable resources while the opponent deliberately worsens the problem, and administration figures now privately concede it could be a multi-year situation.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highIranoilWTIStrait of Hormuzgeopolitics
So what: The oil risk premium is not a transient spike to fade; a multi-year conflict embeds higher energy costs into inflation expectations and keeps the Fed boxed in.
The administration's implicit inflation plan was that subduing Iran and unblocking the Strait of Hormuz would take WTI from around 100 back to 70, but the delay has already lasted long enough that higher energy prices are becoming embedded in inflation expectations.
causal🗣 hostconviction: highWTIStrait of HormuzIraninflation expectations
So what: The policy bet on a mechanical oil reversal has failed; second-round inflation effects are the risk now, which is far harder to unwind than a price spike.
“it's already it's already gone on for too long. And that's now be and now higher energy prices are embedded like are the new like inflation uh expectation and it's hard to shake that once it starts.” ▶ 37:35
Clients' top worries, per Nordvig, are further oil upside with no solution in sight, the long end's non-linearity threatening equities, and election risk — including the sanctity of the election itself and the market impact of a Democratic sweep after a long absence of one.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highoillong-end yieldsUS electioninstitutional investors
So what: Maps where institutional hedging demand will concentrate: energy upside, duration, and election-dated volatility.
Korea as the standout macro trade 3
Nordvig's top idea into 2027 is Korea in both equities and the currency, which he calls probably the biggest single-country macro shock of his career, with the trade surplus exploding and the won having its best run ever over the last three months.
prediction🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highSouth KoreaKRWKOSPImemorytrade surplus
So what: An explicit, actionable expression of the AI-inflation thesis outside US mega-cap: long Korean equities and the won, in both memory names and domestic consumer beneficiaries.
“one of the most important and and pronounced macro trends is playing out in Korea” ▶ 1:04:08
The microstructure of the Korean equity market has flipped: it used to be driven by foreign flows, but local retail buying has driven it so high that foreigners now have to sell to rebalance, inverting the historical correlation between foreign flows and the KOSPI.
framework🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highKOSPISouth Koreaforeign flowsretail investors
So what: Foreign-outflow headlines out of Korea are now a rebalancing artifact, not a bearish signal — a live example of a flow model breaking because the underlying market structure changed.
“So the correlation between those flows and and and the Cosby is totally flipped.” ▶ 16:32
Nordvig expects Korean growth to surprise massively to the upside on the back of a domestic investment boom in memory capacity, with the benefits spreading to workers, dividends and domestic demand rather than staying in the memory complex.
prediction🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highSouth Koreamemory capexKorean consumerdividends
