AI equity leadership rotation 4
The AI rally has rotated leadership three times in three years — Mag 7 hyperscalers in 2023, semiconductors from mid-2024 through 2025, and now enterprise software in 2026 as semis report blowout numbers while their stocks fall.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highMag 7semiconductorsenterprise softwareSalesforceS&P 500
So what: Narrative half-life is roughly a year; leadership rotation, not exposure to AI per se, has been the return driver — argues against concentrating in whichever layer just worked.
“So, we've had like a horse race with three different horses leading in three years.” ▶ 46:00
A host is skeptical of the new software-wins thesis, noting the IGV/SMH ratio is in a long downtrend and has only barely bounced back to its 200-day, so the software rally looks like nothing when zoomed out.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: mediumIGVSMHenterprise softwaresemiconductors
So what: A concrete relative-value chart to monitor: until IGV/SMH breaks its downtrend, treat software leadership as a counter-trend bounce, not a regime change.
A fourth, less visible AI wave is long-horizon capital — pension funds and sovereign wealth funds — going into power plants, grid infrastructure and copper, largely in private markets that require a very big checkbook to access.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highpension fundssovereign wealth fundspower generationgrid infrastructurecopper
So what: The energy and metals derivative of AI is being bid by price-insensitive long-duration capital — supportive for copper and listed power/grid proxies that public investors can actually reach.
Meta was upgraded by a major bank with its price forecast raised 25-30% on the argument that it adds an enterprise AI business — including its newly launched open-weight model Muse — on top of its existing businesses, after trading at roughly 17x forward earnings with nobody wanting it two weeks earlier.
data_point🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumMetaMuseenterprise AIopen-weight models
So what: Illustrates that mega-cap AI valuations are not extreme if the company is a winner; the operative question is whose revenue they take, not the multiple.
“met was like 17 times f earnings nobody wanted it two weeks ago.” ▶ 44:59
Consumer: card data versus stock prices 6
Discretionary retail stocks have been destroyed — American Eagle down 15% in a day, Dick's down 38% in a month, with Burlington, Casey's and Advance Auto Parts also hit — yet a composite of same-store sales across Ross, Bath & Body Works, Starbucks, Costco, Target, AutoZone, Walmart, Macy's, Home Depot and others is up 5.1% year over year.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: highDick's Sporting GoodsAmerican EagleCostcoWalmartTargetsame-store sales
So what: The divergence is a setup, not a warning: use same-store sales and card data as the consumer read and treat discretionary equity weakness as a positioning/valuation story.
“I trust the credit card data more than I trust the stock price of Dick Sporting Goods.” ▶ 57:35
Equal-weight consumer discretionary relative to the S&P 500 is at its lowest level ever, but it has been falling from upper left to lower right for a decade while the equal-weight index made all-time highs — so the ratio has had no predictive content for the market.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highconsumer discretionaryS&P 500 equal weightrelative strength
So what: Retires a widely cited recession indicator; the discretionary/S&P ratio reflects portfolio-manager preference, not consumer health.
“It's crashing. But guess what? It's been crashing. It's been going from the top left to the lower right for the last decade. And it's told you nothing, nothing at all about the stock market.” ▶ 52:01
Bank of America data shows total aggregated credit and debit card spending per household up 5% year over year, household savings still above 2019 levels in inflation-adjusted terms, and discretionary spending booming — with CEO Brian Moynihan confirming August spending up about 4% versus last August after 5% the prior quarter.
data_point🗣 hostconviction: highBank of AmericaBrian Moynihanconsumer spendingJPMorganCiti
So what: The banks with the actual transaction data are not corroborating the consumer-is-cooked thesis, and Brown notes they would have an incentive to guide down if they could.
“Consumers spent in the month of August about 4% more than they spent last August. Last quarter it was 5%. So, it's kicking along. That's consistent with a strong growing economy.” ▶ 57:04
A host argues the useful split is not discretionary versus staples but experiences versus items: golf courses, airlines, hotels and Live Nation track the card-spend data, while goods retailers like Dick's are weak because consumers no longer want to fill their garages — with Delta's record Q2 and business-class expansion as supporting evidence.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highDelta Air LinesLive NationhotelsDick's Sporting Goodsconsumer discretionary
So what: A re-segmentation that turns a confusing sector into a pair trade: long experience-economy names against goods retailers.
Nordvig's caveat is that 2026 consumer strength was partly juiced by unusually large tax refunds from the no-tax-on-overtime and no-tax-on-social-security provisions, with most filers receiving money in May — so the key question is whether that is spent over two months or six.
causal🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumtax refundsconsumer spendingfiscal policy
So what: A dated fade risk on consumer data: if the refund impulse was short, the spending deceleration shows up in autumn card data — worth watching monthly rather than quarterly.
The economy has absorbed tariffs, inflation, a frozen housing market and high gas prices without budging, which Brown calls a 'Terminator economy.'
thesis🗣 hostconviction: highUS economytariffshousing marketgasoline prices
So what: Repeated failure of macro shocks to dent growth argues for respecting the resilience rather than positioning for recession — which is also why rates can stay high.
“It's amazing how much has been thrown at this economy.” ▶ 58:02
AI and the research/data business model 5
Data-only businesses fail because portfolio managers are too busy to find what matters, so Nordvig has merged Exante Data into Vanda around a hybrid model where proprietary data is paired with a human insight layer telling clients what is relevant right now.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: highExante DataVandadata providershedge funds
So what: The margin in financial data is migrating from feeds to vetted interpretation — relevant for valuing data vendors as AI commoditizes raw information.
“Data only business is very tricky, right? because investors are busy, portfolio managers are incredibly busy” ▶ 11:10
High-end institutional clients want more phone contact than they did a couple of years ago, precisely because so much unvetted information now exists — a dynamic the host sums up as rich people not talking to robots, they pay someone else to talk to the robots.
contrarian🗣 hostconviction: highinstitutional clientsAIwealth managementresearch
So what: AI abundance is increasing, not decreasing, the premium on human accountability — a bullish structural read for advice businesses rather than a disintermediation risk.
“I have this heruristic where I tell people rich people don't talk to robots.” ▶ 14:25
LLMs are good at induction and pattern matching but cannot generate novel explanatory hypotheses, which is why models built on historical market microstructure go stale when the structure changes — as it did in Korea.
framework🗣 hostconviction: highLLMsAI researchquantitative modelsSouth Korea
So what: Defines where human macro analysts retain an edge over AI tooling: regime changes and new themes the training data has never seen.
“it can't generate novel explanatory hypothesis” ▶ 17:51
Data vendors now face a real tension over whether to license proprietary data to model providers, since many clients want to consume everything through Claude but distributing proprietary data that way threatens the proprietary advantage.
thesis🗣 Jens Nordvig, president of Vandaconviction: mediumAnthropicClaudedata licensingVanda
So what: A structural constraint on AI model coverage of financial data — the best proprietary datasets may deliberately stay outside the models, preserving vendor moats.
The hosts expect Apple's $2,000 iPhone Duo to sell out on October 16 because tech blogs expected $2,500, meaning Apple surprised with a lower launch ASP and left itself room to raise price with a future Duo Pro rather than starting high and risking a flop narrative.
prediction🗣 hostconviction: highAppleiPhone DuoiPhone 18 Pro Max
So what: A pricing-ladder read on Apple's product cycle: initial units are supply-constrained and the ASP uplift comes in the next generation, so the revenue impact lands later than the launch hype.

Macro Voices · Erik Townsend 1 episode(s) · 49 insights

MacroVoices #549 Matt Barrie: AI-gent Provocateur79 min49 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Freelancer.com CEO Matt Barrie gives Erik Townsend a hands-on account of running ~40 personal AI agents that burned 4 billion tokens in a single day at $1,300, then walks the cost stack down 500x by switching to Chinese open models and buying his own Nvidia DGX Spark boxes — arguing the open models win the token market, Nvidia is deliberately going direct-to-user around a $1.65 trillion debt-financed data-center buildout whose demand rests on two customers, and white-collar queue jobs are about to be automated wholesale. The back half is MacroVoices' usual market segment: a long-dated Nvidia call trade, oil breaking $100, collapsing S&P breadth, and extreme grain positioning.
Agentic AI inside a live operating company · 6Inference cost curve and model arbitrage · 8Local inference and the hardware scramble · 6AI capex debt bomb and Nvidia going direct · 8White-collar dislocation and the skills that survive · 5AGI, energy ceiling and robots · 4Freelancer's positioning: humans as inference engines · 2Where's the trade: rates, oil and positioning · 10
Agentic AI inside a live operating company 6
Freelancer.com's CEO personally built a fleet of about 40-44 agents in roughly a month, automating team workflows such as project-approval queue processing that previously required about 11 people to staff 24/7.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highFreelancer.comClaudeAI agentsAnthropic
So what: Queue-shaped headcount is the first cost line to fall in any services business; screen portfolio companies and labor-intensive listed businesses for the share of staff sitting in repetitive queues.
“in the space of about two sessions of half an hour to an hour you can now completely automate that queue processing” ▶ 3:23
After a performance marketer resigned, the role was replaced by an agent that reads Google Ads, Google Analytics, the database, financial performance, shipped code and the ticketing system overnight and delivers a tuning report at 4:10am — against a roughly half-million-dollar monthly ad spend.
anecdote🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highGoogle AdsGoogle AnalyticsFreelancer.comAI agents
So what: The economics are decisive when the agent manages a large spend line: a 10% improvement on $500k/month of ad spend dwarfs the token bill, which is the template for where agents get deployed first.
“no human, no matter how much I looked into the market to find someone great. There's no way that they'll be getting up every morning at 4:00 a.m. and producing a report by 4:10” ▶ 5:13
The agent fleet consumed 4 billion tokens in a single day at a cost of roughly $1,300, of which about $900 was Anthropic's Sonnet-tier model — and roughly 3 billion of those 4 billion tokens were prompt-cache reads generated by agents looping on polling schedules.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highOpenRouterAnthropicSonnetFableGrok
So what: Prompt caching means headline token counts massively overstate real inference demand; anyone modelling token-growth-to-revenue for model providers needs to net out cache reads.
“it came in at um 4 billion tokens in one day and that's 4 billion with a B” ▶ 6:53
A single prompt instructing the agent system to optimize itself for cost cut token usage by a further 85%, on top of the savings already available from model switching.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highAI agentsFreelancer.com
So what: Agent inference spend is highly elastic and self-optimizing, which argues against extrapolating current per-customer token revenue for the labs.
“by just typing in that one single prompt I managed to optimize the token usage down by another 85%” ▶ 9:48
AI now handles 100% of Freelancer.com's tier-one customer interactions, delivering instant 24/7 responses in any language with answers described as four times longer, four times better and ten times more empathetic than human agents could sustain.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highFreelancer.comcustomer supportAI agents
So what: A concrete, already-shipped datapoint for the BPO and contact-centre disruption thesis rather than a projection.
“AI now does 100% of our tier one uh interactions with customers” ▶ 36:19
The agentic stack is no longer gated on specialist talent — a building maintenance handyman with no coding background taught himself to script APIs and build his own dashboards with Claude Code as a hobby.
anecdote🗣 Erik Townsend, hostconviction: highClaude CodeAnthropicAPIs
So what: If adoption does not require expensive consultants, the diffusion curve is far steeper than enterprise-software analogues imply — bullish for token demand, bearish for implementation-services businesses.
“That says to me that this whole thing can happen way faster with far less specialized human skill than I ever imagined.” ▶ 34:19
Inference cost curve and model arbitrage 8
The same day's agent workload costs roughly $80,000 on an Opus-class model, $1,300 the way it was actually run, and about $150 on the Chinese open model GLM 5.3 — a 500x spread available purely by switching models.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highGLM 5.3Z.aiDeepSeekQwenAlibabaAnthropicOpus
So what: A 500x price dispersion for near-equivalent output is the single strongest argument that frontier-model pricing is not defensible; it caps the revenue case underwriting the data-center buildout.
“that's $80,000 in Opus, $1,300 the way I did it, $150 on GM 5.3, which is a 500x spread, just switch between models” ▶ 10:20
There are effectively zero switching costs between models — a slash-command in a coding harness reloads the context into a different model, and an agent can be migrated between harnesses by asking it to write its own transfer file.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highOpenRouterClaude CodePiAnthropicOpenAI
So what: No customer lock-in means model providers have no pricing power at the commodity tier; the durable margin has to sit in hardware, distribution or verticals rather than the model itself.
“There's zero switching costs. The agents have no loyalty.” ▶ 49:58
Every conventional moat is absent in foundation models: the science sits in roughly 40 public technical papers, weights are open, and new state-of-the-art labs keep appearing from nowhere — DeepSeek, then Z.ai — making it the most brutally competitive market in the world.
framework🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highDeepSeekZ.aiOpenAIAnthropicventure capital
So what: Treat model-layer businesses as commodity producers, not software franchises, when underwriting their valuations.
≈ “the AI foundational models are like opening a Thai restaurant in a row full of other Thai restaurants in Thailand on steroids” paraphrase
Free frontier access is a training-data acquisition channel: when GLM 5.3 launched as 'Ox Alpha' it gave away 100 trillion tokens free, the model visibly improved over that week, and roughly 42 trillion tokens of user data went in as training material.
causal🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highGLM 5.3Z.aiAnthropicOpenAI
So what: Free or heavily discounted inference should be read as a data trade, which is the core reason enterprises will eventually pay a premium for private inference.
“if you're kind of wondering why, you know, why it's free, it's because you're probably the product” ▶ 11:24
Current token prices are a teaser rate: the subsidised pricing is expected to reset upward once lenders financing the data-center buildout start repricing the underlying debt.
prediction🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.com, with Erik Townsendconviction: highOpenAIAnthropichyperscalersprivate credit
So what: Businesses building agent workloads on current token prices are running an unhedged input-cost exposure; owning hardware is the hedge.
“We're on still on the teaser rate for token pricing.” ▶ 27:25
Anthropic's refusals are a commercial liability — the model declining to review the user's own legal documents on policy grounds is described as materially hurting adoption, while the open-source community removes refusals from every released open model within days through 'obliteration' fine-tunes.
contrarian🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highAnthropicHugging Faceopen-weight modelsAI safety
So what: Two implications: safety guardrails carry a measurable adoption cost for closed labs, and model-level safety policy is unenforceable once weights are public.
“all this AI safety stuff that's going on, it's all pretty pointless with open source because every model out there has been obliterated” ▶ 16:14
Astra is not delivering on its AGI marketing in practice — it failed six straight attempts to draw a border on a graphic, and users report it rewriting entire authentication systems when asked for a small change — while excelling narrowly at things like Blender 3D modelling.
contrarian🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highAstraOpenAIBlenderAnthropic
So what: Frontier launch cycles are accompanied by paid-influencer hype; the 'is everyone switching back to OpenAI' question is, on hands-on evidence, no.
“I don't think Astra is kind of there yet” ▶ 51:59
Anthropic may restrict access to its high-end API because Chinese labs buy subscriptions and distil the knowledge out of the models, pushing the market toward stratification: commodity tokens from Chinese open models, and the Western labs moving upmarket toward consulting-style engagements for pharma or defence.
prediction🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumAnthropicOpenAIChinaMcKinseyDeloittedefencepharma
So what: If the labs migrate into consulting-shaped revenue, they compete with McKinsey and Deloitte rather than with software vendors — a direct threat to high-end consulting margins.
“you'd hate to be a Mckenzie or a deoid at this point in time” ▶ 23:06
Local inference and the hardware scramble 6
A pair of Nvidia DGX Spark boxes (~$4,000 each at purchase, now around $5,000) with a 200GB memory link can run a 300-billion-parameter DeepSeek V4 Flash model at 45-60 tokens per second, described as Opus-level quality, at about $9,000 of hardware to power one programmer.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highNvidiaDGX SparkDeepSeek V4 FlashOpus
So what: A concrete price point at which local inference substitutes for a frontier subscription — the threshold that determines how much of the token market ever reaches the hyperscalers.
“effectively a pair of DGXs can run DeepSc V4. You get kind of Opus level quality” ▶ 14:19
Replicating the full 4-billion-token-per-day agent workload locally would take about 16 DGX Sparks at roughly $65,000 of hardware — about $100 a day amortised over two years plus roughly $8 a day of electricity, since each box draws only about 100 watts.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highNvidiaDGX Sparkdata centers
So what: Owned hardware at ~$108/day versus $1,300/day of rented frontier tokens is the arbitrage that pulls inference to the edge and caps hyperscaler pricing.
“It draws collectively less power than a kettle uh to boil because they're about 100 watts each.” ▶ 15:12
The scramble for self-hosted inference hardware is already visible in supply: DGX Spark prices rose between two purchases, distributors went out of stock in the US and globally, and the Mikrotik switch that chains four boxes into 512GB of unified memory sold out worldwide.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highNvidiaDGX SparkMikrotikRTX 6000
So what: Consumer and prosumer AI hardware shortages are a real-time demand signal for Nvidia's direct channel and for the memory chain feeding it.
“by the time I hit reload on the page, they're out of stock” ▶ 18:05
A secondary market in used data-center GPUs is booming as Hopper-series kit comes off lease ahead of Blackwell and Vera Rubin — it is about to become the second-largest category on escrow.com.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.com and escrow.comconviction: highescrow.comNvidiaH100HopperBlackwellVera Rubin
So what: A rare non-public window into GPU resale volumes; a liquid secondhand market puts a floor under residual values but also undercuts new-build leasing economics for the SPVs.
Enterprise is heading for an 'emperor has no clothes' moment on data privacy — everything in G Suite, on the public web and in hyperscaler-hosted email is being scraped and trained on, and traffic from AI scrapers to freelancer.com is up 1,300% in twelve months.
prediction🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highGoogleMicrosoftAmazonAWSAppleFreelancer.com
So what: Names an explicit trillion-dollar opportunity in confidential/private AI, and flags a real cost line for content sites: CDN bills rising while blocking scrapers risks damaging search-driven customer acquisition.
“I do think there's a trillion dollar industry available right now around confidentiality and privacy” ▶ 22:40
Hugging Face has become the distribution hub where open models are quantized, obliterated and hardware-tuned by enthusiasts, which is framed as the reason Nvidia acquired it.
causal🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumNvidiaHugging FaceDeepSeekGLMQwen
So what: Reads Nvidia's move as buying the open-model distribution layer to pair with direct hardware sales — the vertical play that hedges its data-center customer concentration.
AI capex debt bomb and Nvidia going direct 8
Hyperscalers have loaded on roughly $1.65 trillion of debt over five years — about $500 billion from data centers and $200 billion from private credit — before counting off-balance-sheet vehicles, versus subprime peaking at $1.3 trillion in 2007 with 55 million mortgages behind it, while the AI buildout is underwritten by effectively two customers.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.com, citing Ed Zitronconviction: highOpenAIAnthropichyperscalersprivate creditEd ZitronMeta
So what: The central bear case on AI credit: the collateral pool has almost no diversification, so a stumble at either OpenAI or Anthropic transmits straight into data-center SPV debt and private credit marks.
“Subprime peaked at 1.3 trillion in 2007. And that that was had 55 million mortgages behind it. While this AI explosion has two customers, OpenAI Anthropic.” ▶ 0:06
Data-center special-purpose vehicles are structured to hold the chips, the debt and the risk off the sponsor's balance sheet — Meta is cited as carrying $46 billion of exposure to Hyperion in its filings with none of it on the balance sheet.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.com, citing Ed Zitronconviction: mediumMetaHyperionhyperscalersSPVsEd Zitron
So what: Balance-sheet leverage screens understate hyperscaler AI exposure; read the filings' off-balance-sheet commitments before sizing credit risk.
“data center special purpose vehicles are this bubble cos clarized debt obligations and AI data centers are at subprime mortgages” ▶ 25:34
Customer concentration in cloud AI revenue is extreme: OpenAI and Anthropic are cited as accounting for roughly 73% of Amazon's AI revenue, 74% of Microsoft's, and over half of all Google Cloud by 2027.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumAmazonMicrosoftGoogle CloudOpenAIAnthropic
So what: Hyperscaler AI revenue growth is not a diversified secular trend but a two-counterparty story; stress-test cloud segment forecasts against those two names' funding.
Nvidia is deliberately sidestepping the coming data-center credit problem by selling boxes directly to end users, prosumers and enterprises — DGX Spark at roughly $5,000, a ~$100,000 station with about 748GB of memory — and letting the SPVs fight over the leases.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highNvidiaJensen HuangDGX Sparkdata centersOpenAI
So what: The core bull case for Nvidia decoupling from an OpenAI blow-up: direct hardware revenue survives even if the rental/leasing complex breaks.
“Nvidia is still out there in the market selling direct” ▶ 32:00
Four of Nvidia's largest customers are now building their own chips, including an OpenAI inference chip described as roughly twice as efficient in watts per inference, which is part of why Nvidia is opening a direct channel.
causal🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumNvidiaOpenAIcustom siliconinference
So what: Customer-designed inference silicon compresses Nvidia's future data-center share, making the direct/edge channel strategically necessary rather than opportunistic.
Nvidia resembles a software company in its cost structure — it packages high-bandwidth memory sourced from only three suppliers (SK Hynix, Micron, Samsung) around a GPU at roughly 70% margin — leaving it dependent on that memory chain.
framework🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumNvidiaSK HynixMicronSamsungHBM
So what: HBM suppliers are the chokepoint with pricing leverage over Nvidia's margin; a reason to look at the memory names as a levered expression of the same demand.
Open models will win the bulk of the token market, which is the strategic premise behind Nvidia's direct-hardware and Hugging Face moves, even though the Western labs retain an agentic-reliability edge for now.
prediction🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highNvidiaDeepSeekGLMQwenHugging FaceAnthropicOpenAI
So what: If token volume migrates to open weights running on owned hardware, the revenue never reaches the hyperscalers that are financing the buildout — the crux of the AI capex bear case.
“I think the open models are going to win here” ▶ 29:23
On the amortised economics of owning data-center-class hardware: secondhand eight-GPU H100 boxes run $150,000-$200,000 for 5,000-10,000 tokens per second, while Blackwell B200 boxes at roughly $500,000 deliver 20,000-40,000 tokens per second — about $400-600/day and $700-900/day respectively.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumNvidiaH100HopperBlackwellB200
So what: Gives a defensible per-token cost floor for owned compute against which rented frontier token prices can be benchmarked.
White-collar dislocation and the skills that survive 5
The unit of organisational change is the functional team collapsing to a team leader plus AI — where a leader previously managed five people processing a queue, the AI becomes the team and the leader directs it.
framework🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highFreelancer.comAI agentslabor market
So what: A specific model for headcount reduction that preserves management layers while eliminating individual contributors — the shape to look for in company disclosures.
“It would be the team leader controlling the AI and the AI will be the team effectively.” ▶ 43:40
Barrie would not want to be long commercial real estate — not because of work-from-home, but because AI is about to remove a large fraction of the white-collar jobs that occupy the buildings; an Australian bank with 50,000 employees and 700 branches could plausibly run on 10,000 or fewer.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highcommercial real estatebanksAustraliaAI agents
So what: An explicit short/avoid on office CRE tied to a headcount mechanism rather than to hybrid work, and a template for screening bank cost bases.
“I wouldn't be want to be long commercial real estate at the moment. It's not because the work from home. It's because it's the AI is going to chew through a lot of these jobs.” ▶ 38:43
The scarce human skill in an agentic world is agency and initiative rather than technical specialisation, because the ability to execute an idea has collapsed to writing English in a chat harness.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highlabor marketeducationAI agents
So what: A hiring and career filter: the survivors of automation are the people who generate and direct work, which also argues for an explosion in one-person businesses.
“So the most important thing you need to develop is as a human is agency.” ▶ 47:39
The endpoint of white-collar automation is a hyper-competitive entrepreneurial society resembling India's informal economy — everyone running hustles and micro-businesses — following the historical pattern of agricultural and factory mechanisation.
prediction🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: mediumlabor marketIndiagig economyprediction markets
So what: If correct, demand shifts toward tools, marketplaces and payment rails serving micro-entrepreneurs rather than toward enterprise seat-based software.
University value is now degree-specific: engineering-style degrees still teach analytic method and MBAs still sell a network, but most curricula teach material from ten to twenty years ago and will have to move to oral exams as AI breaks take-home assessment.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.com, former adjunct professorconviction: mediumuniversitiesStanfordeducation
So what: Structural pressure on the higher-education business model, with continuing/professional education the segment most likely to hold value.
AGI, energy ceiling and robots 4
Energy is the binding constraint on the singularity: if every person on earth burned 4 billion tokens a day it would require roughly 30 terawatts — many multiples of current world energy production — against planned data-center additions of only 200-300 gigawatts.
framework🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highenergydata centerselectricityAI
So what: Quantifies why 'age of abundance' framings fail and why power generation, not chips, becomes the binding bottleneck and the durable investment theme.
“energy is kind of the final boss that had need to be solved I think for the singularity to occur” ▶ 57:41
The meaningful singularity milestone is AI improving AI without human participation, at which point outcomes are determined purely by who commits the most energy and GPUs — and research published this week on how much frontier-model code is now written by other AI suggests the process has begun.
framework🗣 Erik Townsend, host, with Matt Barrieconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicElon MuskRay KurzweilAGI
So what: Gives a falsifiable marker for AGI progress — the share of model code written by AI — that is more useful than benchmark or marketing claims.
“The equation blows up when it can accelerate itself without human participation.” ▶ 55:46
Jensen Huang has proclaimed AGI has arrived with Astra and Elon Musk publicly assigns an 80% base case of an abundance outcome alongside a 10-20% probability of human extinction — a range wide enough that the host treats it as unactionable framing.
data_point🗣 Erik Townsend, hostconviction: mediumJensen HuangNvidiaElon MuskAstraOpenAI
So what: Executive AGI proclamations are marketing inputs into the capex cycle; treat them as sentiment indicators rather than forecasts.
“there's also unfortunately an outlier case of 10, maybe even 20% probability of outcome, which is that it's an extinction event for the human race” ▶ 56:13
Humanoid robotics has solved locomotion and is entering commercial scale — Unitree has IPO'd, is described as profitable with billions in revenue, and units start at around $6,000 with superhuman running and jumping performance.
data_point🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highUnitreeTesla Optimushumanoid roboticsdrones
So what: Names the specific listed vehicle and the price point at which humanoid robots become deployable — the next capex wave venture capital is financing after AI models.
“these things are selling starting at $6,000” ▶ 58:43
Freelancer's positioning: humans as inference engines 2
Freelancer.com positions its 90 million-person marketplace as 'artificial artificial intelligence' — humans framed as 20-watt inference engines with free pre-training who already have access to every model — to sell AI automation projects into companies lacking in-house expertise.
thesis🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highFreelancer.comNASAAI agents
So what: The labor marketplace is repositioning as the implementation channel for agentic AI rather than as roadkill — the key question for anyone valuing gig marketplaces in an AI world.
“these humans are effectively 20 watt inference engines with free pre-training” ▶ 1:00:18
The commercial weakness of raw AI is its nondeterminism — described as pay-to-pray, a slot machine where you sometimes go in circles — which is why paying per outcome rather than per token is pitched as the superior procurement model.
framework🗣 Matt Barrie, CEO of Freelancer.comconviction: highFreelancer.comAI agents
So what: Outcome-based rather than token-based pricing is where service providers can defend margin against model commoditisation.
“it's pay to prey. It's a slot machine.” ▶ 1:01:36
Where's the trade: rates, oil and positioning 10
The recommended expression of the AI structural thesis is long-dated Nvidia optionality rather than the shares: with the stock around $224.31, a January 2027 at-the-money call trading near $21.60 at roughly 38% implied volatility and 128 days to expiration, because January 2027 implied vols have collapsed to the lowest levels of the past year.
thesis🗣 Patrick, MacroVoices co-hostconviction: highNvidiaoptionsimplied volatilitysemiconductors
So what: Defined-risk, positive-vega exposure that survives a correction into the midterms while retaining upside if semis re-accelerate — the cheap-vol argument is the actual edge here, not the direction.
“January 2027 option implied volatilities have collapsed towards the lowest levels in the past year” ▶ 1:03:49
The current market regime runs oil as the catalyst, the bond market as the transmission mechanism and equities as the absorber of the damage, with WTI printing $100 and Brent clearing it, forcing the front end to reprice toward more hawkish central bank action across the US, Canada and Europe.
framework🗣 Patrick, MacroVoices co-hostconviction: highWTIBrentTreasuriesFederal ReserveSOFR
So what: A clean causal chain for positioning: trade the oil shock through rates and equity multiples rather than only through energy equities.
“oil has just been the catalyst and the bond market now is the transmission mechanism and now equities are absorbing all of this damage” ▶ 1:06:00
The 10-year sits at 485 basis points and the 30-year back above 530, driven by heavy Treasury supply, fiscal concerns, rising term premium and enormous corporate borrowing competing for capital — and a $6 billion buyback announcement disappointed a market expecting $10 billion.
data_point🗣 Patrick, MacroVoices co-hostconviction: highUS TreasuryTreasuriesterm premiumcorporate credit

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea 1 episode(s) · 47 insights

Saronic's 4 Co-Founders on Building a $9.25B Shipbuilder | Part III60 min47 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
An on-site tour of autonomous shipbuilder Saronic ($9.25B valuation, $2.6B raised, ~4 years old) in which co-founders lay out the hard numbers of the US-China shipbuilding gap (230:1 overall, 1,000+ commercial ships vs 5 in 2024, China at 57% of global capacity) and the operational playbook they are using to close it: unmanned-first hardware/software co-design, modular shipyard construction at the $3B Port Alpha, and pre-buying scarce US aluminum and steel. Only three co-founders (COO Doug, CCO Rob, CTO Viv) are interviewed in this part despite the title's '4 Co-Founders'; the opening stats segment is delivered by an unnamed co-founder.
US-China shipbuilding gap · 6Defense budget vs autonomy spend · 5Saronic's scale and product ladder · 6Reindustrialization chokepoints · 7Acquisition reform mechanics · 4Unmanned-first co-design · 9Maritime autonomy's data and regulatory gap · 5Where the moat goes next · 5
US-China shipbuilding gap 6
China outbuilds the United States in shipbuilding by roughly 230 to 1 in aggregate, but the gap is only about 6-to-1 or 7-to-1 in warships (US launches 5-10 military vessels a year versus China's ~30) — the true discrepancy is commercial.
data_point🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highChinaUS NavyshipbuildingSaronic
So what: The headline 230:1 statistic used to justify defense-shipbuilding budgets is driven by commercial tonnage, so policy that targets only naval yards will not close the industrial-capacity gap.
“China outbuilds the US 230 to 1” ▶ 1:34
China built over a thousand large ocean-going commercial vessels in 2024 while the United States built five.
data_point🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highChinaUnited Statescommercial shipbuilding
So what: Quantifies the collapse of US commercial yard capacity — the base load that keeps naval yards, welders and suppliers viable.
“They build over a thousand commercial ships last year. And the United States built five.” ▶ 7:10
China holds 57% of total global shipbuilding capacity and is taking capacity from the rest of the world by undercutting on price in the commercial market, with its entire shipbuilding industry subsidized across construction, raw materials, labor and free shipyard financing.
causal🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highChinaglobal shipbuildingsubsidies
So what: A subsidized-price mechanism, not efficiency, is driving the capacity shift — meaning tariffs or allied reshoring subsidies are the plausible policy response and a tailwind for domestic yards.
“Their entire shipbuilding industry is completely subsidized. Not only direct construction subsidies, but they subsidize the raw materials, they subsidize the labor, and they basically give them free financing for these shipyards.” ▶ 6:30
Allied shipbuilding is not a fallback: South Korea and Japan, the world's next two largest shipbuilders, are themselves sourcing a lot of their commercial modules and labor from China, so allied capacity erodes alongside US capacity.
thesis🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highSouth KoreaJapanChinaTaiwan Strait
So what: Undercuts the 'build in allied yards' thesis that underpins several Korean and Japanese shipbuilding trades — their supply chains are China-dependent in a conflict scenario.
“They're actually on the commercial side, they're actually getting a lot of their modules from China. They're getting a lot of their labor from China.” ▶ 7:34
Naval power has historically been determined by who can build the most ships, not who can build the most exquisite or expensive ones — the explicit founding thesis behind Saronic's cheap, mass-produced autonomous vessels.
framework🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highSaronicUS NavyWorld War II
So what: The mental model that separates attritable-mass defense companies from exquisite-platform primes — a structural bet on volume manufacturers over legacy shipbuilders.
“what has typically just determined naval power is who can build the the most ships. It's not who can build the most exquisite, the most expensive” ▶ 5:38
Chinese shipbuilding is an opaque market where it is very hard to get reliable information, which limits the ability to verify the capacity comparisons being used to set US policy.
anecdote🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: mediumChinashipbuilding
So what: Data quality caveat for anyone sizing the shipbuilding TAM off Chinese-sourced capacity figures.
Defense budget vs autonomy spend 5
Only about 1% of the Department of War's roughly $1 trillion FY26 budget went toward autonomy, despite top-down pressure from the administration and Capitol Hill to buy scalable and economical systems.
data_point🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highDepartment of Wardefense budgetautonomy
So what: The single most important sizing number for autonomous-defense investors: the addressable budget line is tiny today, so the thesis is entirely about mix shift, not current spend.
“for '26. 1% 1% went towards autonomy. So, that that just gives you a sense of how far we actually have to go.”
The US naval fleet is shrinking to around 290 ships against a 2018 statutory minimum of 355, which the Navy revised upward to 381 in 2024 — and those counts exclude the unmanned ships the fleet will also need.
data_point🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highUS NavyCongressunmanned surface vessels
So what: A codified, widening gap between statutory requirement and actual fleet is the structural demand signal for any shipbuilding or USV supplier.
The Navy's 30-year plan to grow from 290 to 381 ships is budgeted at $1.2 trillion, against a presidential budget request of $1.5 trillion — and that assumes the country can execute the plan at all.
data_point🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highUS Navy30-year shipbuilding planSHIPS Act
So what: Sizes the cost-reduction prize that autonomous shipbuilders are pitching against; the execution risk in the plan is itself the sales argument.
Aircraft carrier cost estimates are escalating in real time — a figure of $13 billion quoted internally was contradicted by the president quoting $19 billion at an event a week earlier — while each carrier takes over 10 years to build and the schedule keeps slipping.
anecdote🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highaircraft carriersUS Navydefense procurement
So what: Concrete evidence of exquisite-platform cost escalation, the wedge that low-cost autonomous vessels use to win budget share.
“I I was actually quoting 13 billion dollars for an aircraft carrier, and I was at an event where the president spoke just last week. And he quoted 19 billion dollars.” ▶ 3:36
The addressable customer set for autonomous surface vessels spans all services rather than just the Navy — the Army actually has more boats than the Navy does, and Saronic is also targeting the Marine Corps, Coast Guard, Air Force combat search and rescue, commercial buyers and allied militaries.
thesis🗣 Saronic co-founder, opening segmentconviction: highUS ArmyUS NavyMarine CorpsCoast GuardSaronic
So what: Broadens the revenue base beyond the Navy shipbuilding line item, which is the usual bear case on maritime-only defense startups.
“The Army actually has more boats than the Navy does.” ▶ 4:31
Saronic's scale and product ladder 6
Saronic has reached a $9.25 billion valuation on $2.6 billion raised in roughly four years, with around 1,600 people rolling up through the COO's operations organization and a $3 billion Port Alpha shipyard announced.
data_point🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicPort AlphaAustin
So what: Benchmarks the capital intensity and headcount of a credible defense-manufacturing scale-up for anyone underwriting the sector.
Saronic's product strategy is a deliberate size ladder — two lesser-known predecessors (Spyglass and Cutlass) preceded Corsair, and Marauder is the analogous stepping stone toward 400-foot, 800-foot and even Panamax-class 1,200-foot ships.
framework🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicSpyglassCutlassCorsairMarauderPanamax
So what: A concrete roadmap signal: the company intends to move up-tonnage into full ship classes, expanding TAM well beyond small USVs.
Saronic's first autonomous boat was a plastic box bought from Amazon with electronics put in it, which became a 6-foot autonomous boat and then Corsair — evidence that the capital-intensive shipbuilding thesis was validated at near-zero cost first.
anecdote🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicCorsair
So what: A pattern to look for in hard-tech diligence: cheap proof of the autonomy stack before committing to heavy capex.
“our first autonomous boat was like a was like a plastic box we bought from Amazon and put some electronics in it”
Saronic closed its first government contract within 90 days of founding via a CRADA — a no-cost cooperative research and development agreement that buys early access to non-public customer requirements in exchange for codifying the relationship and managing IP guidelines.
framework🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highSaronicCRADAUS government
So what: A replicable go-to-market unlock for defense startups: a free contracting vehicle that provides requirements visibility before any program of record exists.
Saronic sources requirements bottom-up from fleet-level operators rather than from program executive offices or Capitol Hill, treating the gap between what operators are ordered to do and what they actually have as the gold-standard product signal.
framework🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highSaronicUS NavyPEO
So what: An operating model that explains why some defense startups beat primes on relevance — worth testing against any defense-tech investment.
“It was really finding operators and understanding what are the things you're being asked to do that you have to say yes sir, yes ma'am. And then you look at each other and say, we don't have what we need to be able to do that.” ▶ 29:15
Saronic deliberately fuses program management with growth and business development into one flywheel rather than separating them, because the autonomous maritime community is small enough that program teams and sales teams talk to the exact same customers.
framework🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: mediumSaronicautonomous maritime
So what: Org-design detail that compresses the customer feedback loop — a differentiator in a niche market with few buyers.
Reindustrialization chokepoints 7
Saronic had to buy out the vast majority of the aluminum in the Louisiana area simply to build one Marauder, and is now pre-securing the steel pipeline for Port Alpha ahead of need.
anecdote🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highaluminumsteelLouisianaPort AlphaMarauder
So what: Regional raw-material markets are thin enough that a single program clears them — bullish for domestic metals suppliers and a real bottleneck on any shipbuilding scale-up.
“we wound up buying out a vast majority of the aluminum in the Louisiana area where we went ahead and built a Marauder” ▶ 14:23
Casting and forging are the industries most likely to bottleneck US reindustrialization over the next three to five years because they have largely migrated offshore, while wire harnessing previously done in Ukraine or Mexico is only now starting to come back.
prediction🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highcasting and forgingwire harnessingUkraineMexicoreshoring
So what: Names the specific upstream segments where reshoring capital and pricing power will concentrate — a targeted supply-chain trade.
“Casting and forging is a big one. You know, I see that being a challenge here in the next 3 to 5 years.” ▶ 16:52
Two decades of certification and qualification requirements in defense manufacturing built a real quality system but also locked a large portion of the American labor force out of the industry — a systemic barrier that must be solved through automated inspection, better work instructions and design simplification rather than more training alone.
causal🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highdefense manufacturinglabor forceautomated inspection
So what: Reframes the defense labor shortage as a credentialing problem, which is far more tractable — and favors companies investing in inspection automation and part-count reduction.
“There's no reason somebody who can't change a tire shouldn't be able to go and build a Corsair or work on a Marauder or work on a larger ship.” ▶ 16:26
The shipyard productivity strategy is to move final outfitting work that traditionally happens on the ship into sub-assemblies built elsewhere, then assemble modules into larger modules into super modules — with robotic aluminum welding from Path Robotics as the enabling partner.
framework🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highPath RoboticsPort Alphamodular shipbuildingaluminum welding
So what: The specific manufacturing change that could actually compress shipbuilding cycle times — the operational proof point to watch at Port Alpha.
The hard part of robotic aluminum welding is not tacking metal together but doing it in a way that passes ABS, IMO and Coast Guard certifications — the real gating factor on automating shipyard work.
causal🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highPath RoboticsABSIMOUS Coast Guardaluminum welding
So what: Certification, not robotics capability, is the binding constraint — a useful milestone test for welding-automation companies.
Reindustrialization requires the government to move first on contracting, because it is a chicken-and-egg problem where commercial industry only invests once demand signals appear — Saronic is fortunate enough to be able to invest ahead of need.
thesis🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highUS governmentdefense contractingreindustrialization
So what: Government contracting velocity is the leading indicator for the whole reshoring supply chain, not just for prime contractors.
Trainable-skills programs can radically compress industrial onboarding — a peer company reduced complex wire-harness training from about two years to roughly four weeks — and adults are best taught through apprenticeship and risk/reward skill-building rather than classroom learning.
data_point🗣 host Molly O'Shea and Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: mediumSundra SystemsJordan BlackSpaceXwire harnessing
So what: Suggests the industrial labor bottleneck is compressible on a months-not-years timeline, which materially changes ramp assumptions for manufacturing scale-ups.
Acquisition reform mechanics 4
This wave of defense acquisition reform is different from prior cycles because there is real money, real teeth and individual-level visibility into where the system breaks — versus the historical pattern of NDAA language producing a report nobody reads while everyone keeps getting promoted.
thesis🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highNDAAdefense acquisitionDepartment of War
So what: If accurate, procurement cycle times compress and non-traditional defense suppliers gain share — the core bull case for the whole defense-tech cohort.
“an NDAA piece of language that ultimately yields a report that no one reads and nothing's done about it and people keep moving up the ladder” ▶ 31:11
The binding constraint in defense procurement is that a single contracting officer can veto a program that the program manager, leadership and combatant commander have all aligned on — so accountability structures, not incentives or punishment, are the only lever that actually works on government employees.
causal🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highcontracting officersprogram executive officersdefense acquisition
So what: Identifies the precise process choke point to monitor in acquisition-reform legislation — reforms that don't touch contracting-officer authority won't move timelines.
“you can't have one human who's not tied to the mission making arbitrary decisions based on a guidebook that doesn't have any wiggle for them” ▶ 31:40
A concrete near-term AI use case in defense is augmenting the acquisition workforce, because contracting officers repeatedly and adamantly insist they cannot do things that new regulations actually permit — they simply are not aware of the maneuver space they have.
thesis🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highAIdefense acquisitioncontracting officers
So what: A specific, unglamorous software market inside defense — regulatory-awareness tooling for the acquisition workforce — that would directly shorten procurement cycles.
“I think one thing that could help is using AI to augment the acquisition workforce because we've come across multiple times where there's a lack of awareness about a new regulation, a new policy” ▶ 33:19
Contracting officers default to playing it safe and adhering to old rules because that is what has built their careers, so reform requires giving them a tool that affirmatively tells them a faster path is permitted.
causal🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highcontracting officersdefense acquisition
So what: Explains why top-down policy change alone historically fails to speed procurement — the behavioural layer is where reform succeeds or dies.
Unmanned-first co-design 9
Removing humans unlocks an order-of-magnitude performance envelope in boats: commercial vessels are designed around humans tolerating roughly 0.4 to 0.6 Gs, whereas Saronic has seen accelerations north of 20 Gs on its craft.
data_point🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicautonomous surface vesselsnaval architecture
So what: The clearest quantification of why unmanned maritime is a step-change rather than an incremental improvement — the physics advantage legacy hulls cannot match.
“I think a lot of commercial boats, like you kind of target the human beings to like take like 0.4 to 0.6 Gs maybe. Um you know, our boats like we've seen accelerations north of 20 Gs before.” ▶ 49:32
AI coding tools have inverted the robotics development sequence — because code and debug cycles are now fast, the prudent move is to front-load hardware bench assembly, observability and simulation, so Saronic built Marauder's genset, propulsion stack and bow thruster in a room roughly eight months before the 180-foot ship existed.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicMarauderAI coding toolshardware-in-the-loop
So what: A genuinely non-obvious second-order effect of AI coding: it changes hardware capital-deployment sequencing and compresses time-to-first-mission for robotics companies.
“we built all of the internal kind of genset, propulsion stack, bow thruster, all of those components of the ship just in a room before like probably eight months before the ship was built” ▶ 41:06
The operating principle for hardware/software companies is to deliberately slow software down to be more methodical about infrastructure choices while speeding hardware up by buying parts early and building hardware-in-the-loop systems — converging the two development cadences.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronichardware engineeringsoftware engineering
So what: A transferable diligence question for any robotics company: have they converged their hardware and software cadences, or are they still serialized?
“hardware engineers tend to measure twice twice and cut once. Software engineers just cut until it looks looks right” ▶ 41:57
Saronic designs to modular open systems with well-known hardware and software APIs specifically so third-party payloads can be integrated quickly — proprietary bespoke protocols or custom plugs make payload integration prohibitively slow.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicmodular open systemspayload integration
So what: Open architecture is both a government procurement requirement and a moat-building ecosystem play — a differentiator versus closed prime platforms.
Electronics are designed so cameras and sensors sit on power distribution modules that can be individually power-cycled remotely in software — needed because operators in contested RF environments often must keep radar off to avoid revealing position.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaroniccontested RFradarelectronics architecture
So what: Mission-driven electronics design decisions that legacy manned hulls can't retrofit — a concrete source of technical differentiation.
Every Saronic platform ships with a PACE plan — a redundant set of beyond-line-of-sight and line-of-sight communications including Starlink and its defense variant Starshield — because heavy sea states and water on antennas degrade quality of service the same way active jamming would.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highStarlinkStarshieldSaronicPACE plan
So what: The ocean itself functions as a permanent jammer, which means maritime autonomy demands comms redundancy that aerial drones can skip — a cost and design burden unique to the domain.
“the nice part about the ocean or maybe the bad part about the ocean is that ocean kind of does that for you regardless” ▶ 54:39
Lost-comms behaviour on Saronic vessels is not fixed by the manufacturer but mission-planned by the operator through open APIs — continue the mission, loiter, return to last comms position, or return to base — with responsibility resting on the operator.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicmission autonomyopen APIs
So what: Pushing autonomy policy decisions to the operator is how the company sidesteps the regulatory and liability debates that slow autonomous systems approval.
Nvidia dominates robotics compute and Saronic uses it not primarily on price but because the software layer is easiest to move quickly with, and Nvidia's large training-side market share makes the inference-side transition seamless.
thesis🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highNvidiaGPU computerobotics
So what: Direct practitioner confirmation that Nvidia's robotics moat is CUDA-software lock-in rather than silicon price — relevant to any bear case built on cheaper accelerators.
“I think it's a combination of price point, but but predominantly this the software layer is is um just the easiest to to move quickly with”
Saronic runs Palantir Foundry as the backbone for its manufacturing execution system and MRP, with an unexpectedly easy adoption curve where supply chain analysts and even PR staff build their own dashboards.
anecdote🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highPalantirFoundrySaronicMRP
So what: A real-world datapoint on Foundry penetration into industrial/manufacturing workflows beyond government analytics — supports the Palantir commercial expansion thesis.
Maritime autonomy's data and regulatory gap 5
Maritime autonomy lacks the open-source data foundation that UAVs and self-driving cars have — hundreds of billions of dollars have gone into self-driving with open datasets and research, while maritime data is sparse for two reasons: manning requirements on autonomous boats blocked the commercial economic push, and acoustic and subsea perception work gets classified very quickly.
causal🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highmaritime autonomyUAVsself-driving carsDHSUkraine
So what: The data scarcity is itself the moat — whoever accumulates proprietary maritime perception data early has a defensible position that drone makers never got.
“you still have manning requirements on autonomous boats so that that you you haven't had the economic push” ▶ 48:20
Legacy marine engineering software is unusable for small autonomous vessels — one of the largest European marine design firms' simulation software produced numerical instability every single time Saronic tried to design a boat under 50 feet in it.
anecdote🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highmarine design softwaresimulationSaronic
So what: Incumbent maritime tooling literally cannot model the new product category, forcing from-scratch builds — a barrier to entry and a whitespace for maritime simulation software.
“every single time we tried to design a boat under 50 ft in their simulation software, there was numerical instability” ▶ 46:45
Operating at the ocean surface is harder than operating subsea because the surface is a dual-fluid environment — ten feet below the surface is a single, plannable fluid, while the surface produces rare tail-risk events like stuffing the boat on a bad wave at the wrong speed.
framework🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highautonomous surface vesselsnaval architecture
So what: Explains why surface autonomy is technically distinct from (and in some ways harder than) subsea — relevant to comparing USV and UUV companies.
Long-duration ocean missions create failure modes absent from land and air robotics: saltwater on antennas degrades quality of service, birds nest on vessels over 30-day missions, and biofouling accumulates.
anecdote🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highbiofoulingautonomous surface vesselsmaritime operations
So what: Sustainment and maintenance cost assumptions for maritime autonomy should not be benchmarked off drone fleets.
Regulatory approval is the gating milestone the company hopes to push through within a year — the goal is autonomous surface vehicles becoming as visible in daily life as Teslas and Waymos on the streets.
prediction🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: mediumWaymoTeslaautonomous surface vesselsregulation
So what: Regulatory approval, not technology, is the catalyst that would open the commercial maritime market — the specific event to watch.
Where the moat goes next 5
The moat of the future will be building real, tangible, manufactured things rather than software, precisely because AI and bot coding are commoditizing software.
contrarian🗣 Doug, COO of Saronicconviction: highhard techAImanufacturing
So what: The core allocation argument for hard-tech and manufacturing over pure software as AI compresses software margins — an explicit sector-rotation thesis.
“being successful in real space, doing hard tech, building real things, and manufacturing with real tangible items is going to be the moat of the future, especially as software becomes a bit more commoditized” ▶ 22:21
Volume changes the fundamentals of how autonomous systems are used — with five or ten units operators baby each one, but with hundreds of Corsairs or twenty Marauders the operational cadence and the set of possible missions changes entirely, and a fully unmanned fleet has never been done before.
thesis🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: highSaronicCorsairMarauderattritable mass
So what: The step-function value inflection is at fleet scale, not first delivery — the milestone that should re-rate an attritable-mass defense company.
“there's a big difference in the operational cadence when you have hundreds of something uh versus when you have like five or 10” ▶ 56:39
The largest untapped opportunity is commercial: the market has not had its aha moment that dramatically reducing the cost of doing things in the ocean opens entirely new categories of activity currently priced out.
thesis🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: mediumcommercial maritimeSaronicocean economy
So what: Frames the long-term equity story as a cost-curve-driven market creation play rather than a defense budget share play.
“if you reduce the overall cost of doing things in the ocean, um there's so many new cool things that you can get into” ▶ 57:44
The CTO's stated excitement is the wave of open-source AI, on the view that it will democratize tooling and resources and that competitive open ecosystems historically make technology better for everyone, drawing the analogy to how much of the internet rests on a handful of open-source repositories.
thesis🗣 Viv, CTO of Saronicconviction: mediumopen source AINvidiacapex buildout
So what: A robotics practitioner betting on open-weight models rather than closed frontier labs — signal on where embodied-AI demand may concentrate.
Saronic's systems have already had documented real-world operational impact, with the team seeking to personally thank two Apache pilots affected by the technology in recent weeks.
anecdote🗣 Rob, Chief Commercial Officer of Saronicconviction: highSaronicApache pilotsUS military
So what: Combat-relevant operational proof is the credibility event that converts defense-tech valuations into programs of record.
“what I'm looking forward to most is shaking hands with those two Apache pilots” ▶ 35:42

Top Traders Unplugged 1 episode(s) · 47 insights

AI, Higher Rates and the Next Big Economic Imbalance | Global Macro | Ep.10762 min47 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Matt Klein (author of The Overshoot, co-author of Trade Wars Are Class Wars) argues to host Alan Dunn that the AI capex boom is a reason for real interest rates to go UP, not down, because the 1990s productivity boom showed up as faster growth rather than disinflation — and that measured underlying US productivity has been roughly zero for 18+ months. He also argues US bond yields near current levels are justified by ~7% nominal growth rather than deficits, and that the real global imbalance story is China's trade surplus exploding from ~$100-200bn to over $1 trillion a year, with US tariff policy aimed at the wrong countries.
Warsh Fed reaction function · 4AI vs the 1990s productivity analogy · 10AI capex: real boom, foreign leakage · 5Bond yields and nominal growth · 7Debt sustainability arithmetic · 4China's trillion-dollar surplus · 6Tariffs aimed at the wrong targets · 5Yen intervention and Japan's constraints · 6
Warsh Fed reaction function 4
Fed Chair Kevin Warsh's Jackson Hole speech walked back fears that he would be a political 'hack', by explicitly restating the inflation target and conceding there is a gap between actual inflation and that target which policy must close.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumKevin WarshFederal ReserveJackson HoleDonald Trump
So what: Removes the 'captured Fed' tail risk that was being priced into the long end; front-end pricing should track inflation data rather than political-pressure narratives.
Market pricing after Jackson Hole shifted toward the Fed RAISING short-term rates rather than cutting, because inflation has made essentially no progress in roughly three years and Fed officials are only now accepting that the 2021-22 inflation impulse will not simply unwind on its own.
prediction🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumFederal ReserveKevin WarshUS inflation
So what: A hiking-biased Fed against consensus cut expectations is the key asymmetry for front-end rates, steepeners, and duration-sensitive equities.
“the market pricing reaction was pretty clear that the assumption okay maybe the Fed is going to raise short-term rates” ▶ 4:30
Warsh's newly announced productivity-and-jobs task force is a red flag for reverse-engineered policy, because before his nomination he had already argued that AI would be inherently deflationary and would create scope for lower interest rates.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumKevin WarshFederal ReserveAI
So what: Watch whether the task force output is used to justify cuts on a productivity story the data does not yet support — that would be a credibility event for the long end.
“there's a concern again trying to sort of predetermine the answer” ▶ 3:02
Of all the Fed task forces, the balance-sheet task force (Raghuram Rajan, Jeremy Stein, Karen Dynan) is the one most likely to produce a genuine policy surprise, because it is the only question where current Fed officials' public views are genuinely unsettled and divided.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumFederal ReserveRaghuram RajanJeremy SteinKaren Dynan
So what: Balance-sheet framework changes move repo, bill supply and duration risk premia; this is the under-watched task force for rates traders.
“the balance sheet tax force I think is very interesting uh potentially um just because of the caliber of people they have there and the nature of the question and the fact that it's really unsettled” ▶ 58:47
AI vs the 1990s productivity analogy 10
There has still been no meaningful acceleration in measured US productivity despite the AI capex boom, so every bullish productivity-driven disinflation forecast is currently pure speculation rather than data.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highAIUS productivityBEA
So what: Positions premised on an AI productivity miracle lowering inflation have no data support yet; the burden of proof sits with the bulls.
“so far we've not actually seen any meaningful productivity acceleration in the data so far” ▶ 5:13
The San Francisco Fed's decomposition of output per hour shows zero growth — possibly slightly negative — in underlying US productivity over the past 18-plus months, with all measured gains coming from workforce experience, more capital per worker and higher resource utilization.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumFederal Reserve Bank of San FranciscoUS productivity
So what: If the gains are 'working harder' rather than 'working smarter', the economy has less non-inflationary headroom than headline productivity suggests — hawkish for rates.
“so far it seems that people are working harder rather than smarter” ▶ 18:08
Greenspan's reputation for calling the 1990s productivity boom early is overstated — he only began making the argument in mid-1996 and the productivity data did not accelerate until 1997, meaning today's AI productivity calls are far further ahead of the data than his were.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highAlan GreenspanFederal ReserveSebastian Mallaby
So what: Removes the historical precedent policymakers lean on for pre-emptively easing into a productivity story.
“He didn't start making that argument until the middle of 1996. So he he was in front of the official data but not radically in front of it”
Even a full replay of the 1990s would imply higher, not lower, interest rates, because the level of rates entering the 1990s boom was substantially above today's, real rates rose once the boom started, and arguably they should have risen more than they did.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS TreasuriesTIPSreal interest rates
So what: The single most tradeable inversion in the episode: the consensus 'AI = disinflation = lower rates' trade may be backwards; favors short duration and long real yields.
“even if you were to sort of naively say it's the same thing, the implication that interest rates would go down, I think is is not correct. I think it's very plausible that if that were to happen, interest rates would go up.” ▶ 10:07
The 1990s productivity boom showed up almost entirely as faster real growth rather than lower inflation; what disinflation there was came from collapsing commodity prices during emerging-market crises, post-bubble Japan and a rising dollar — none of it productivity-driven.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highJapanemerging marketsUS dollarcommodities
So what: Reframes the productivity trade as a nominal-growth trade, not a disinflation trade — bullish nominal GDP, bearish long bonds.
“The productivity boom in the 90s mostly manifested as more growth rather than less inflation.” ▶ 8:51
Today's disinflation forces run the opposite way to the 1990s: since Covid, the war in Ukraine and the war in Iran, countries are stockpiling physical goods and rebuilding domestic productive capacity, which is structurally more inflationary rather than less.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumUkraineIranglobal supply chains
So what: Supports a structurally higher inflation floor and argues against duration on any 'the 90s are back' analogy.
Higher productivity should raise, not lower, the cost of capital, because more attractive investment opportunities raise the demand for investment and expected returns relative to everything else.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highcost of capitalAIcapex
So what: A clean mental model for why an AI boom is a bear case for bonds, not a bull case.
AI's productivity gains are so far concentrated in software engineering, which is only a small share of the economy, and historical technology diffusion (electric motors took decades) suggests economy-wide gains take far longer than the 12-36 month timelines investors are pricing.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumAIsoftware engineeringWaymoSan Francisco
So what: Timing risk: even a genuine AI productivity boom may arrive years after the capex that is being underwritten on it.
Economy-wide productivity is not directly measurable — it is nominal spending deflated by an inflation measure and divided by estimated hours — so measurement error in any of the three inputs can manufacture apparent productivity swings, as it did when low-wage job losses in the pandemic mechanically inflated output per hour.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS productivityBEACovid
So what: Guards against trading a policy pivot off a single productivity print; composition effects can fake a boom.
Treating AI as a separate factor of production changes nothing fundamental for economic models, because AI tokens can be handled as an intermediate input like energy, and because companies pay real money for AI services the national accounts already capture the value flow.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highAIBEAKevin Warshnational accounts
So what: Deflates claims that AI makes existing macro data or models obsolete — a common justification for ignoring inflation data.
“I've seen this before of people saying this like new technologies changes our fundamental understanding of how the economy works” ▶ 20:27
AI capex: real boom, foreign leakage 5
The AI data-center buildout is already showing up in consumer prices, most visibly through memory chips, with Apple raising MacBook and iPad prices by roughly 20% and consumer flash-drive and memory prices rising before that.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highApplememory chipsdata centersCPI
So what: A concrete inflation transmission channel from AI capex to core goods CPI — watch memory pricing as a leading indicator for consumer electronics inflation.
“I think Apple just recently announced they're raising all the prices on their MacBooks and and iPads by like 20% or something” ▶ 6:39
Most of the incremental US spend on data centers flows abroad as imports, with Taiwan and Korea the main beneficiaries, unlike the 1990s when the capex boom mostly translated into domestic US production.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highTaiwanKoreadata centersUS GDPnet imports
So what: A US AI capex bust would hit equity valuations hard but domestic GDP less than headline capex numbers imply; the GDP exposure sits in Taiwan and Korea.
“most of the incremental spend on data centers is actually flowing abroad” ▶ 23:53
The view that the US economy is entirely dependent on AI is a misreading — the economy is growing for multiple reasons and AI is one impulse whose direct GDP contribution is diluted because so much of it leaks into higher net imports.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumUS economyAInet imports
So what: Argues against the 'ex-AI the US is in recession' framing that underpins many bearish macro positions.
“the idea that the US economy is entirely dependent on AI growth I think is uh a misreading of what's been happening” ▶ 24:36
The 1990s were a genuine broad-based real investment boom, not merely a valuation bubble — real net investment saw its biggest sustained boom since the data began around 1929, and industrial capacity growth was unprecedented back to the 1940s even excluding high-tech sectors like semis and computers.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highFederal Reserveindustrial capacitysemiconductorspets.com
So what: Undercuts the comfort that 'this time it's real capex, not a dot-com valuation bubble' — the 1990s were real capex too, and still busted.
The 2000s downturn was worse than commonly appreciated because the collapse in business investment was so large that the Fed offset it with an unsustainable housing bubble and still only produced anemic growth — a genuine risk template for an AI capex unwind.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumFederal Reservehousing bubblebusiness investment
So what: The second-order risk of an AI capex bust is not just the drawdown but the distortionary policy response that follows it.
Bond yields and nominal growth 7
Locking in a 5% fixed nominal return for 30 years makes no sense while the economy is growing around 7% a year in dollar terms — it only makes sense if you believe nominal growth is about to fall toward 3%.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS Treasuries30-year yieldnominal GDP
So what: A simple valuation test for long duration: buying the long bond is an explicit bet on a nominal growth collapse, not a neutral position.
“if you think the growth is going to stay around 7% for a while, which it might not, but if you do, 5% makes no sense” ▶ 27:50
The 10-year Treasury yield has been rangebound with no trend since mid-2023 and has not broken 5%, even though the 30-year has pushed higher — so the 'yields are surging' narrative is really a long-end/term-premium story.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein and host Alan Dunnconviction: high10-year Treasury30-year Treasury
So what: Frames the trade as curve steepening and term premium rather than an outright level move in the belly.
“the 10-year yields basically been in a rangebound for the past 3 years right if you look at a chart since like mid 2023 to now it's no trend whatsoever” ▶ 31:24
Rising yields are a benign sign rather than a crisis signal — they reflect an economy that has escaped the post-financial-crisis regime, and the fact that they are the highest in 19 years mostly reflects how terrible many of those intervening years were.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS Treasuriesglobal financial crisis
So what: Argues against the reflexive 'high yields will break something' trade; supports risk assets alongside higher rates.
“there are worlds where interest rates go up um and it's not bad. I think this is one of those times.” ▶ 32:42
Japan's 10-year yield hitting a 30-year high is the correct market response to Japan finally escaping deflation, not a signal of distress — rates should be different when people stop believing Japan will shrink toward zero forever.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highJapanJGBsBank of Japan
So what: Reframes the JGB selloff as a regime normalization rather than a funding crisis — relevant for carry-trade and global term-premium spillover trades.
The bond term-premium channel from deficits runs only through nominal growth, not through default risk, because the central bank in the US, UK, Japan or New Zealand can always finance the government — the risk deficits create is inflation, not default.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS TreasuriesUK giltsJapanNew Zealand
So what: Directs attention away from 'bond vigilante default' narratives toward inflation breakevens as the correct expression of fiscal risk.
There is no policy combination for a large economy that lowers the budget deficit AND lowers interest rates without hitting growth — tax hikes and spending cuts lower rates only by lowering nominal growth; the exchange-rate-depreciation escape hatch used by Canada, Sweden and Norway in the early 1990s is unavailable to the US.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highCanadaSwedenNorwayUS fiscal policy
So what: Kills the 'fiscal consolidation is a free lunch for bonds' trade — a credible consolidation is a growth-negative, risk-asset-negative event.
