“I don't think there's sort of this this magical independent thing you can do that would lower the budget deficit lower interest rates not hit growth” ▶ 30:29
The rise in US government borrowing is the mirror image of unusually conservative private-sector behaviour: households and businesses are spending more than in the 2010s but not borrowing to finance it, so private indebtedness is falling while public borrowing rises.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS householdsUS corporatesUS Treasury
So what: Deficit-driven doom scenarios miss that aggregate leverage has shifted sectors, not exploded — argues against a private credit blowup thesis on leverage grounds.
Debt sustainability arithmetic 4
US nominal growth (G) still exceeds the interest rate (R), but the primary deficit excluding interest is large enough that debt still grows relative to the economy regardless.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS debt-to-GDPR vs G
So what: Precise framing for debt watchers: the binding variable is the primary deficit, not the R-vs-G spread everyone quotes.
“the deficit excluding interest is big enough that it doesn't matter” ▶ 33:31
US debt service costs relative to the economy are still below their early-1990s peak, because much outstanding debt was locked in at low rates and 1980s rates were far higher — though debt service will rise as low-coupon debt rolls.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS Treasurydebt service
So what: Provides the timing edge: the interest-cost problem is a rolling-maturity story with years to run, not an imminent cliff.
So long as the rest of the world in aggregate runs a current account surplus, somebody in the US must be a net seller of financial assets — and it is better for that seller to be the government, which has the lowest cost of capital and least rollover risk, than the private sector.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUS current accountUS Treasuryglobal savings
So what: A genuinely non-consensus defense of large US deficits: cutting them without fixing the external imbalance just relocates leverage into riskier private balance sheets.
“surely it is better for the entity selling financial assets to be the government, which is going to have the lowest cost of capital, the least risk of being able to roll anything over” ▶ 34:21
The bad debt equilibrium to watch for is the Brazil scenario — extraordinarily high real interest rates and balanced primary budgets but crushing interest costs, a legacy of past hyperinflation episodes — and the US is nowhere near it.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumBrazilreal interest rates
So what: Gives a concrete benchmark for what genuine fiscal distress looks like, so investors can avoid mispricing US fiscal risk against it.
China's trillion-dollar surplus 6
China's aggregate trade surplus has expanded from roughly $100-200 billion a year pre-pandemic to over $1 trillion a year, an unprecedented shift for an economy of its size relative to the global economy ex-China.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highChinaglobal tradecurrent account
So what: The single biggest macro imbalance number in the episode — it is the source of deflationary pressure on European manufacturing and the trigger for the next tariff wave.
“China's trade surplus in the aggregate has increased from something on the order of a hundred billion to200 billion a year on the eve of the pandemic to over a trillion dollars a year” ▶ 44:18
Chinese household consumption as a share of what China produces is lower than in any comparable society ever — the only analogues are corporate tax havens with inflated GDP, oil sheikhdoms, and the Soviet Union under Stalin.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highChinaSoviet Unionhousehold consumption
So what: Quantifies why Chinese rebalancing is structural, not cyclical — a reason to discount every 'China stimulus will fix demand' rally.
China's pandemic response was the inverse of the rest of the world's: rather than supporting consumers, it forced an estimated 50-70 million rural migrants out of cities and back to subsistence farming with no unemployment eligibility, and channelled stimulus into manufacturing capacity and export subsidies instead.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumChinaCovidChinese manufacturing
So what: Explains the mechanism behind the export surge — excess capacity built deliberately, so it will not self-correct with a cyclical recovery.
“the number of people who were migrants from the countryside to cities fell something on the order of 50 to 70 million people during the initial lockdowns in 2020” ▶ 43:14
Global imbalances matter for two specific reasons: they reveal distributional problems where people live below their means, and they correspond to increases in indebtedness that threaten financial stability — the same pattern seen before the global financial crisis and in the 1920s.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highglobal financial crisisChinaMichael Pettis
So what: A checklist for deciding when a current-account imbalance is benign versus a pre-crisis signal.
Borrowing to finance current spending is categorically different from borrowing to buy productive assets, because the former must be continually rolled and creates no future value — the distinction that makes an imbalance dangerous.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highhousehold debtcredit
So what: A credit-quality lens for judging whether a rising debt stock is a red flag or a rational investment cycle.
The cleanest resolution of the imbalance is for Chinese households to spend more on themselves, which is already the Chinese government's official position and a live debate among Chinese academics — it simply has not been prioritised enough to move the numbers.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highChinaPremier Li QiangG20G7
So what: Watch for a genuine Chinese consumption policy shift as the highest-impact, lowest-probability positive catalyst for global growth and EU industrials.
“The cleanest answer is for people in China to spend more on themselves to increase their own living standards. Everyone will be better off if they do that.” ▶ 48:16
Tariffs aimed at the wrong targets 5
US trade policy over the past 18 months does not correspond to the actual data on global imbalances — most tariff energy has been spent on countries that contribute nothing to global imbalances, while China has comparatively been let off the hook.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highUnited StatesChinaCanadatariffs
So what: Implies the tariff regime is less economically binding on China than headlines suggest — and that the real China trade shock still lies ahead, likely from Europe.
“it seems like most of the energy the US has expended in terms of tariffs and trade conflict have been with countries that are not contributing global imbalances” ▶ 0:00
US-China business conditions are reported by people on the ground in China as better than they have been in a very long time and much better than in 2023-2024, which is the opposite of what the political rhetoric would suggest.
anecdote🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumChinaMcKinseyDalianUS-China relations
So what: A contrarian ground-truth data point for China-exposed multinationals versus the headline geopolitical risk premium.
“he said that he thought that the business climate and US China relations now or like as of you know a month ago whatever two months ago was better than it had been in a very long time” ▶ 49:54
Europe is the more likely source of the next major China trade action, because Chinese exports of motor vehicles and chemicals are hitting European industry acutely, and a reasonable estimate that the yuan is ~35% undervalued gives Europe a template for sectoral tariffs of 35-40%.
prediction🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumEuropean UnionChinaautoschemicalsyuan
So what: A specific, sizeable and under-priced tariff scenario: European autos and chemicals are the sectors to watch for policy protection.
“if you take sort of I think a very reasonable estimate that that the Chinese yuan is undervalued on the order of 35%”
Tariffs are a leaky instrument versus the exchange rate, because the first Trump administration's China tariffs mostly caused rerouting — direct US imports from China fell sharply while imports from Southeast Asian countries rose, leaving the net effect minimal.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highChinaSoutheast AsiatariffsTrump administration
So what: Expect the same rerouting if Europe tariffs China — favours Southeast Asian transshipment beneficiaries over the tariff-protected domestic incumbents.
A Plaza Accord 2.0 with China is a non-starter regardless of its economic merits, because the Chinese believe the 1985 Plaza Accord was a trick the US played to destroy Japan as a rival great power — and they believe it worked.
contrarian🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highChinaJapanPlaza AccordGreg IpFriedrich Merz
So what: Fade any currency-accord rally in the yuan or Asian FX; the political constraint, not the economics, is binding.
“the Chinese believe that this was essentially a trick that the US played on Japan to destroy them and and take them out of the running as being another great power and they think that it worked” ▶ 47:02
Yen intervention and Japan's constraints 6
The US Treasury's yen intervention was economically trivial — despite a widely photographed note suggesting $5-10 billion of yen purchases, reported data suggests only about $500 million was actually bought, a rounding error versus FX market size.
data_point🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumUS TreasuryScott BessentyenFT Alphaville
So what: Treat the intervention as signalling rather than flow — it should not be extrapolated into a sustained USD/JPY policy floor.
“my former colleagues at FD Alphaville wrote that as of the data that we can see, assuming they reported everything correctly, they bought only 500 million” ▶ 52:22
The likely motive for the US yen intervention was to stop Japan from selling its dollar assets, because the Treasury Secretary fears anything that pushes US Treasury yields higher — which is also why the Fed's securities-lending facility was promoted as an alternative to outright Japanese Treasury sales.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumScott BessentUS TreasuryJapanFederal ReserveJGBs
So what: Reveals a policy put on Treasury yields operating through FX channels — a key constraint to watch if Japanese official selling resumes.
“I think it's just that he has this fear of anything making Treasury yields go up” ▶ 54:17
Japan has enormous unused ammunition to strengthen the yen: roughly $1 trillion of official FX reserves plus far larger foreign currency assets across public sector entities, all bought when the yen was much more expensive, so selling them for yen would book an immediate massive profit.
framework🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: highJapanyenFX reserves
So what: A large latent yen-appreciation catalyst that is politically, not financially, constrained — asymmetric risk for anyone short yen.
The yen has failed to appreciate despite a large rise in Japanese long-end yields because that yield move coincided with a shift higher in Japanese inflation and growth expectations, so the forward-looking real rate change was smaller than the nominal move implied.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumyenJGBsBank of Japan
So what: Explains why the standard rate-differential FX model has failed on USD/JPY and warns against re-running it on the next BoJ hike.
Japanese non-financial corporate profits have been massively inflated relative to operating profits over the past six years by interest income on huge portfolios of foreign interest-bearing assets, so a sharp rise in Japanese rates or a stronger yen would hit reported earnings through that channel.
causal🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumJapanese equitiesJapanese corporatesyeninterest rates
So what: A non-obvious earnings-quality risk in Japanese equities: profits are partly a carry trade, vulnerable to BoJ normalization and yen strength.
The Korean won is an even more obvious candidate than the yen for being too cheap against the dollar, yet no intervention has occurred there.
thesis🗣 Matt Klein, author of The Overshootconviction: mediumKorean wonUS dollaryen
So what: Flags KRW as the under-watched appreciation candidate if dollar-weakening policy broadens beyond Japan.

a16z Podcast 1 episode(s) · 45 insights

Why Investors Are Rethinking Everything for the AI Era48 min45 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
An a16z growth investor (David George) and a fund-of-funds LP debate why AI has broken the standard playbook for private-market allocation: compute makes capital itself compounding, so the venture power law is now more extreme than at any point in 10-20 years, and of 3,000 US venture firms only about 20 have produced consistent 3x net returns over two decades. The practical conclusions are concentration in 15-20 managers, 5-10% single-position sizing in late-stage funds, and a hard split between AI-resilient software and the 2016-2021 vintage SaaS now stranded across venture marks, PE portfolios and private credit. They also argue diffusion is barely underway — the median US company spends $12 per employee per month on AI versus $7,000 at the top 1% — and that the real bottleneck is speed to power, not demand.
Capital as a compounding input · 9Manager dispersion and LP construction · 8Death of the middle in venture · 4Traction signal quality in AI deals · 4Stranded pre-ChatGPT software · 6Diffusion is barely underway · 4Supply bottlenecks and the next value pools · 5Liquidity and exit paths · 5
Capital as a compounding input 9
For the first time in David George's career you can throw capital at a company and it compounds that company's advantage, because dollars convert into compute that makes the product better — whereas historically excess capital destroyed startups by forcing over-hiring and coordination overhead.
framework🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highAI labscomputeventure capitaleconomies of scale
So what: Explains why mega-rounds are rational rather than frothy in AI and why fund size has stopped being a drag on returns at the top — the opposite of the 2015-2021 rule of thumb.
“you can take capital and throw it at a company and it compounds their advantage” ▶ 0:13
The venture power law is now more extreme than at any time in the last 10-20 years, going back to the emergence of network-effect consumer companies, because economies of scale in AI are real and compounding.
thesis🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highventure capitalpower lawAI
So what: Return dispersion between the top handful of positions and everything else is widening, so index-like exposure to private AI is close to worthless.
“Right now, clearly the power law is more extreme than it has been in the last 10 to 20 years of technology investing.” ▶ 0:06
Three frontier model companies — named in the episode as SpaceX, OpenAI and Anthropic — represent roughly $3.5 to $5 trillion of potential enterprise value, and most LPs and institutional allocators had little or no exposure to them before they came public.
data_point🗣 host, a16zconviction: highOpenAIAnthropicSpaceXLPsfrontier models
So what: Quantifies the cost of the access problem: a multi-trillion-dollar value pool formed entirely outside the reach of conventional institutional portfolios.
AI reached $100bn of revenue in four years, versus 15 years for SaaS to reach the same level, and is nowhere near demand saturation because demand for inference is effectively unlimited.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highAI revenueSaaSinference
So what: The revenue ramp — not the funding ramp — is the strongest argument against the dotcom analogy; it is the number to track for whether the cycle is real.
“We've reached 100 billion in revenue in AI. It took SAS 15 years to get to the same point. AI did that in four years” ▶ 4:02
AI is attacking roughly $30 trillion of GDP simultaneously — transportation, labor, services, capital and coordination — which the LP argues makes venture/growth a core or super-core allocation rather than a satellite position.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highGDPventure capitalasset allocationAI
So what: The allocation pitch is a TAM argument, not a momentum argument: if AI touches labor rather than software budgets, the appropriate portfolio weight is a different order of magnitude.
“there hasn't been a technology paradigm that hits on 30 trillion in GDP at the same time” ▶ 4:30
AI's TAM should be underwritten against the economic value of the tasks being performed, not IT budgets: healthcare IT is $60-100bn a year, but claims, billing and administration are a trillion-dollar labor pool, making AI's TAM potentially 10x-plus traditional SaaS.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highhealthcare ITTAMSaaSlabor
So what: A reusable valuation frame: size AI companies off task value times a capture rate, which makes apparently absurd revenue multiples defensible in labor-replacing categories.
US dollars spent on labor are roughly 40 times dollars spent on software, so framing AI as the next evolution of software is far too limiting — labor gets reinvented rather than eliminated.
framework🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highlabor marketsoftware spendingAI
So what: Supports paying software-like multiples on services-like revenue, and warns against benchmarking AI companies against SaaS comps.
Evidence so far is that AI is expansionary rather than substitutive in professional services: a16z's own legal counsel reports billable hours going up since clients started using Harvey, because clients now spar with lawyers on topics they would previously have deferred on.
anecdote🗣 host, a16zconviction: mediumHarveylegal servicesprofessional services
So what: Directly contradicts the short thesis on human-services businesses; watch billable-hour and headcount data at law and consulting firms as the falsification test.
The frontier-labs-will-cannibalize-the-apps debate has resolved into everything working at once, so zero-sum thinking about which layer of the stack wins is the wrong frame — the power law operates within categories while the number of categories expands massively.
contrarian🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highfrontier labsapplication layeropen sourceCRM
So what: Argues against layer-rotation trading in AI and for owning the leader in each emerging category; second place is playing for scraps.
“second place is like playing for scraps” ▶ 9:06
Manager dispersion and LP construction 8
Of about 3,000 US venture capital firms, only 20 — less than 1% — have delivered consistent 3x net returns over the last two decades, defined as three to four funds at 3x net TVPI over a 20-year period.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highventure capitalTVPILPsmanager selection
So what: The single most important base rate for anyone allocating to venture: absent access to that top 20, the asset class is not worth the illiquidity.
“We've looked at the data of 3,000 venture capital firms in the US. Only 20 have achieved consistent 3x net returns over the last two decades.” ▶ 0:00
Per Cambridge data the average venture return over the last 10 years is 1-2x, which is worse than private equity at roughly 1.5-2x net without a 10-year lockup, and worse than public markets.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highCambridge Associatesventure capitalprivate equitypublic markets
So what: Average venture exposure is a value-destroying allocation on a liquidity-adjusted basis; the case for venture is entirely a manager-selection case.
The firms that compounded consistently did so because they had access to the category-defining company in every vintage — but holding the logo is not enough; early-stage funds must own enough of it and late-stage funds must size it correctly.
causal🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highventure capitalportfolio constructionownership
So what: Diligence should test ownership percentage and position sizing, not deal logos — the most common way LPs get the call right and the return wrong.
Fund-returning math now exists in late-stage venture, which it did not before: the best position should be 5-10%-plus of the fund so a single company can return the whole fund.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highlate-stage venturegrowth equityportfolio construction
So what: A concrete screen for growth funds: if the top position cannot return the fund, the strategy cannot produce venture-like returns regardless of deal quality.
“your best company should be 5 10% plus of your fund. That way you can actually return the fund on a single company.” ▶ 12:16
LPs holding 50-70 venture manager relationships mathematically cannot beat the average, and even getting the manager call right does nothing if the position is 1% of the portfolio — a 10x on 1% returns 10% of the fund and does not move the needle.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highLPsendowmentsportfolio constructionventure capital
So what: Argues LPs should concentrate into 15-20 firms; diversification across managers in a power-law asset class is a guarantee of average returns.
“when I see a portfolio with 50 60 70 venture capital firms, it's very hard for me to imagine that the overall portfolio can generate better than the average” ▶ 28:44
GPs and LPs face opposite career incentives: a GP gets fired for the error of omission — missing the next Facebook or Uber — while an LP only gets fired for an error of commission, and may not get fired at all for investing in nothing.
framework🗣 host, a16zconviction: highLPsGPsincentivesinstitutional allocators
So what: Explains structural, persistent institutional under-allocation to frontier AI and why that gap should be expected to close slowly rather than snap shut.
“you don't get fired for investing in IBM if you're an LP” ▶ 27:01
Best-performing early-stage venture funds run roughly a 60% loss rate, while growth-stage loss rates are 10-20%, and a fund not losing money on some investments is not taking enough risk.
data_point🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highearly-stage venturegrowth equityloss ratios
So what: A benchmark for evaluating manager risk-taking: a suspiciously low early-stage loss rate is a warning sign, not a quality signal.
“if you look at our best performing venture funds over time, I think the loss rate is 60%” ▶ 9:43
CalPERS, which famously lost out on billions by not investing in venture, has shifted its private allocation from 91% to 58% buyout and from 9% to 43% venture and growth.
data_point🗣 host, a16zconviction: highCalPERSbuyoutventure and growthpension funds
So what: Large pensions repricing venture versus buyout is a persistent bid for top-decile venture funds and a persistent drag on buyout fundraising.
Death of the middle in venture 4
Venture is consolidating into four viable strategies — pre-seed/seed funds under about $150m (close to 2,000 of them in the US), the messy middle of hundreds of firms, a handful of full-stack large firms, and dedicated late-stage — with the middle structurally unable to compete.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highseed fundsventure capitalfund sizes
So what: A manager-selection map: allocate to the barbell ends and treat mid-sized generalist funds as the structurally disadvantaged segment.
“Death of the middle” ▶ 13:40
Small specialized funds can win by playing a stage earlier than the large firms — pre-seed at sub-$20-40m valuations where big firms would rather wait a round for relative certainty — but only if they hold fund size and strategy constant, because scaling up puts them into direct competition with the big firms.
thesis🗣 host, a16zconviction: highpre-seedseed fundsa16zfund strategy
So what: The clearest red flag in emerging-manager diligence is fund-size drift, which mechanically moves a fund into the losing middle.
Large-firm advantage is a flywheel financed by fee income: a16z reinvests management fees into roughly 700 employees of operating resources to bend outcomes and win deals, and LP capital follows founder preference rather than leading it.
framework🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: higha16zmanagement feesfoundersLPs
So what: Explains why fund-size criticism keeps being wrong at the top of the market — scale buys deal access, which is the actual scarce input.
“that's why we have 700 employees. Uh that's why we take, you know, the management fees that we make on our funds and we invest them in operating resources” ▶ 15:11
The strongest late-stage franchises are attached to a large early-stage franchise; it is very hard for a de novo late-stage firm to win the right to write a $500m check into a category-defining company without the earlier relationship.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highlate-stage venturegrowth equitya16z
So what: A structural argument against standalone crossover and late-stage-only vehicles, and for the multi-stage platforms that can carry positions from seed through IPO.
“It's really hard to come in as a denovo late stage firm and write a $500 million check.” ▶ 20:22
Traction signal quality in AI deals 4
AI has made underwriting harder, not easier: rounds are larger and faster, companies come out of accelerators claiming zero to $5m ARR in a month with no renewal cycle, cohort companies sometimes sell to each other, and monthly numbers get multiplied by 12 and called ARR.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highARRacceleratorsventure valuations
So what: Treat sub-12-month ARR claims in AI as unverified; the renewal cycle, not the growth rate, is the first real data point.
“There's no renewal cycle yet on that company.” ▶ 21:53
Financial analysis cannot separate real from misleading traction in fast-ramp AI companies — the only method is understanding the customers, their alternatives and whether the market is demanding more of the product — and the LP guest estimates only about one in ten of them turns out to be the next Cursor.
framework🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highCursorAI startupsdue diligence
So what: Confirms why the signal edge sits with firms that have deep early-stage technical presence; cohort and renewal analytics are table stakes that everyone already has.
“is the market demanding more of your product? Like that is always the question.” ▶ 23:37
Harvey is the template for a delayed inflection: strong early commercial logos but mediocre actual usage, which flipped to absolute takeoff in adoption after reasoning models arrived, at which point clients went from fearing hallucinations to demanding their law firms use the product.
causal🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highHarveyreasoning modelslaw firmsAI adoption
So what: Model capability jumps can re-rate an apparently mediocre application overnight — a reason to track usage and engagement rather than logo counts in AI software.
Consensus was repeatedly wrong on Cursor — people were calling it dead right up to the morning its acquisition was announced — despite it having raised roughly a $400m round on about $3m of ARR.
anecdote🗣 host, a16zconviction: mediumCursorAI codingventure valuations
So what: A live example that apparently insane entry multiples in AI have been justified by outcomes, which is why discipline based on multiples alone has underperformed.
Stranded pre-ChatGPT software 6
Only 15-20 public SaaS companies still trade above 10x revenue, down from dozens a few years ago, and essentially every one of them is showing AI-driven growth acceleration in monitoring, security or agent deployment.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highpublic SaaSmonitoringsecurityAI agentsrevenue multiples
So what: A screen for public software: acceleration is the only thing sustaining a premium multiple, so decelerating SaaS should be expected to keep de-rating toward 2x revenue.
In public software one percentage point of growth is now worth about three percentage points of EBITDA, the inverse of the 2021-22 regime when the market demanded profitability.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highpublic softwareEBITDAgrowthvaluation
So what: A quantified trade-off for software management teams and investors: margin expansion cannot offset deceleration at current market pricing.
“Our data shows 1 percentage of growth in the public markets is equivalent to three percentages of IBIDA.” ▶ 32:32
A 2016-2021 pre-ChatGPT vintage software company still growing 30% but without AI-native product is close to unsellable: marked at 10-20x revenue on venture books, trading at about 2x revenue in public comps, unable to IPO, and no longer of interest to buyers like Silver Lake who would have bought it a year ago.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highSaaSSilver Lakeventure marksIPO market
So what: Points to a coming wave of markdowns in late-2010s venture and growth portfolios where the exit path has disappeared entirely, not just repriced.
Roughly $200-300bn of software LBOs were done in 2021-22 at average valuations of 25-32x EBITDA with over $200bn of debt attached, and those companies are worth about half that today, which is why private credit is seeing redemptions as leverage ratios blow out.
data_point🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highprivate creditsoftware LBOsprivate equityleverage
So what: The AI disruption of software transmits into private credit through leverage on stale assets — a specific place to look for forced selling and NAV marks that have not caught up.
“The average valuation for those software deals over 25 to 32 times IBIDA. Those companies today are worth probably half that.” ▶ 38:53
Bolting AI onto a private-equity-owned business is not a fix: hiring AI customer service agents without rebuilding the workflow churns customers used to humans, and every drop in NPS maps directly to a drop in revenue, which spirals when the company is debt-laden.
causal🗣 host, a16zconviction: highprivate equityAI agentsNPScustomer service
So what: Skepticism warranted on the 'AI-enabled buyout' pitch now being marketed by PE firms; the operating-partner-plus-AI model has not been shown to work.
“just because you put Sears on a website didn't make it Amazon” ▶ 39:40
Intercom is cited as the lone existence proof of a legacy software company successfully rebuilding AI-native — founder returned, business revamped, product rebuilt and then sold — a transformation that requires effectively suiciding the existing business and full board and investor alignment, which is very hard inside private equity.
anecdote🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highIntercomprivate equityAI-native software
So what: Sets the bar for turnaround underwriting in software: without founder-level authority and aligned governance, assume the AI retrofit fails.
“it's almost like you're suiciding your existing business, which in private equity is really hard to do” ▶ 33:10
Diffusion is barely underway 4
The median US company spends about $12 per employee per month on AI while the top 1% of the dataset spends about $7,000 per employee per month, and even the most cutting-edge banks are spending roughly 1% of headcount cost on AI tools.
data_point🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highenterprise AI adoptionbanksAI spending
So what: The headline AI revenue is being generated on almost no penetration, so the bull case does not require new adopters to behave differently — only for the median to move toward the leaders.
The fastest revenue ramps in history are being built on an estimated 10-30 million users of real value-getting adoption, which George reads as evidence that the market is extremely early rather than saturated.
thesis🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highAI adoptiontoken consumptionmega-cap tech
So what: Frames current AI revenue growth as an early-penetration phenomenon, which supports duration of growth rather than a near-term saturation cliff.
“these are the fastest growing companies we've ever seen, like of all time. Again, they're adding more revenue per month than the than the mega cap tech companies.” ▶ 37:04
The strongest bear case for AI diffusion is that coding was a head fake: coding is perfectly documented, verifiable and simulatable, and most business tasks share none of those three attributes, so diffusion into other knowledge work could take much longer.
contrarian🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: mediumAI codingknowledge workenterprise software
So what: The clearest falsifiable test of the AI timeline — and the only serious argument for enduring value in incumbent software, which is why it is worth tracking task-level deployment outside code.
“coding is perfectly documented, right? So it has like perfect data uh it's verifiable and it's simulatable” ▶ 35:22
About 95% of a16z's growth NAV sits outside legacy non-accelerating software companies, in businesses that are growing fast and accelerating.
data_point🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: higha16zNAVgrowth equity
So what: A disclosure of where a leading growth investor's exposure actually sits — useful as a benchmark for how much stranded-SaaS risk a manager is carrying.
Supply bottlenecks and the next value pools 5
The AI bottleneck is supply, not demand, and specifically the left side of the stack — energy, grid, data centers — where the US problem is not generation capacity but speed to power: permitting, transmission and regulation, with other countries adding 10x more renewable capacity per year.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highenergyelectric griddata centerspermittingrenewables
So what: Locates the scarcity rent in interconnection and permitting rather than generation assets — favors developers and technologies that shorten time-to-power over raw capacity owners.
“the US doesn't have a problem with energy generation. It has a problem with speed to power.” ▶ 47:06
New AI facilities cannot reuse old data centers because power density is more than 10x higher, so solving the bottleneck means reimagining the data center — an opportunity the LP sizes at $100bn-plus rather than $10-50bn.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highdata centerspower densityinfrastructure
So what: Legacy data center assets are not a call option on AI demand; the capex has to be greenfield, which concentrates value in new build and next-generation chip and memory supply.
The LP rejects the dotcom and COVID analogies explicitly on the grounds that the traction is real and not ephemeral revenue, arguing the risk is a supply constraint rather than a demand mirage.
contrarian🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highdotcom bubbleCOVID pull-forwardAI revenue
So what: Frames the downside scenario as a capacity-driven growth stall rather than a demand collapse — a very different risk to hedge.
“a lot of I've heard LP say this is like the dotcom or this is co. It's not because the traction is real and it's not ephemeral revenue” ▶ 47:37
David George names the domains where he expects the next $10 trillion-plus of market cap: robotics, which he thinks will be bigger than language models and will happen within 10 years; autonomy, where there are fewer than 10,000 Waymos live in the US and far fewer other robotaxis; consumer AI beyond the chatbot skeuomorph into proactive agents; healthcare at 18% of GDP where neither care delivery nor drug discovery has been touched; and the physical world from defense to manufacturing to data centers.
prediction🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highroboticsWaymoautonomyconsumer AIhealthcaredefensemanufacturing
So what: A map of where a top growth investor expects value creation next — notably away from the chat/LLM application layer that currently absorbs most capital.
“we are, um, nowhere on robotics, but I think robotics is going to be bigger than the language stuff” ▶ 45:22
Top venture outcomes have re-rated from about $10bn a decade ago to roughly $40bn today and plausibly $100bn once Anthropic and OpenAI come public, and the last cycle created $25 trillion of new market cap with each successive wave larger than the prior one.
data_point🗣 David George, a16z growth investorconviction: highAnthropicOpenAImarket capventure outcomes
So what: If outcome size scales with each wave, fund sizes and entry valuations that look extreme against historical comps can still clear — the denominator moved.
Liquidity and exit paths 5
The most common LP objection is time to liquidity: the average unicorn stays private 10-plus years, follow-on rounds stack quickly with the same logo appearing across five or six firms, and an IPO is not a distribution — it can take 12-24 months to actually get liquidity, longer if you own 10-15% at listing.
framework🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highIPOunicornsLPsliquidity
So what: IPO-date headlines materially overstate near-term distributions; model DPI off lockup and sell-down mechanics, not listing dates.
“an IPO isn't actually a distribution” ▶ 41:35
The rebuttal is that top-decile firms now generate fund-returning liquidity faster than private equity, citing Anthropic first funded in 2021 and about to go public five years later, and Cursor going from first financing to acquisition in a very short span.
thesis🗣 guest, LP at Accolade Partnersconviction: highAnthropicCursorprivate equityDPI
So what: The AI cycle is compressing venture holding periods at the top, weakening the standard liquidity objection for the best managers specifically.
“so the best venture firms actually have fund returning liquidity pretty quickly maybe even quicker than private equity. But that subset of firms is tiny.” ▶ 42:32
a16z offered its Fund I LPs liquidity on its seed-stage Stripe position at the 16-year mark and every single LP declined, preferring to let it compound, before the firm exited a year later to wrap up the fund.
anecdote🗣 host, a16zconviction: higha16zStripeLPsendowmentsfamily offices
So what: LP liquidity preference is highly segmented — endowments want compounding and family offices want to defer taxes — so secondary supply from top positions is thinner than the discourse implies.
Venture exits now exceed private equity exits in size: the biggest 2026 PE deals cited are the EA buyout at around $50bn and Medline at around $50bn, while Cursor's M&A sale was larger.
data_point🗣 host, a16zconviction: mediumElectronic ArtsMedlineCursorprivate equityM&A
So what: Inverts the usual assumption that buyout offers the bigger exits; strategic M&A for AI assets is now the largest liquidity channel in private markets.
The best GPs manufactured liquidity opportunistically — taking money off the table in 2021 when most did not — and the current-cycle version is taking early liquidity through M&A while letting the winners ride to IPO.
framework🗣 host, a16zconviction: highGPsM&AIPOliquidity management
So what: A concrete diligence question for manager selection: did this GP realize anything in 2021, and are they realizing anything now?

Adam Taggart | Thoughtful Money 1 episode(s) · 43 insights

Bonds To Reverse Soon As Yields Approach 'Line In The Sand'? | Michael Lebowitz56 min43 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Michael Lebowitz of Real Investment Advice argues to host Adam Taggart that US bond yields have been driven higher by narratives (Iran/oil, deficits, AI-hyperscaler debt issuance, BoJ selling, credit downgrades) rather than fundamentals — core CPI at 2.5%, trimmed mean PCE at 2.3% and breakevens at 2.5% are all back at pre-Iran-war levels while employment growth runs at a third of trend. He frames a 5% 10-year yield as a line in the sand where institutional buyers (not the Fed) step in first, and warns that a Warsh hike a week later into a supply shock — on top of 50-75bp of market-driven tightening in mortgage, auto and corporate borrowing rates — would be a major policy mistake.
Fundamentals vs narratives · 6Inflation data vs the hawkish pivot · 6The 5% line in the sand · 5Bond mean-reversion opportunity · 7Fed policy-mistake risk · 9Positioning and portfolio construction · 6AI's ambiguous rate impact · 4
Fundamentals vs narratives 6
Bond yields are set by fundamentals over the long run — inflation and inflation expectations show a very high historical correlation with yields — but narratives can divorce yield from fundamentals for extended periods, exactly as AMC and GameStop share prices detached from company fundamentals before reverting.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highUS TreasuriesAMCGameStopinflation expectations
So what: Separates the tradeable short-term driver (narrative flow) from the anchor (fundamentals), telling investors to size positions for narrative duration rather than assume fundamentals reassert quickly.
“I'm not dumb. I realize that it's the narratives that are the short-term driver of bond yields. Bond yields can go higher from here.” ▶ 0:00
There is currently a clear divergence between bond fundamentals and bond narratives, and the fundamentals that set yields in the long run say yields are already too high.
thesis🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highUS Treasuries10-year yield
So what: The core actionable call of the episode: long duration is a value position against a narrative-driven overshoot, not a momentum trade.
“the fundamentals that that really in the long run set yields are telling you yields are already too high” ▶ 16:16
The specific narratives pushing yields higher are oil and the Iran war, rising deficits and Treasury issuance, AI-related debt, memory chip price spikes, possible Bank of Japan Treasury selling, credit downgrades, and the absence of Fed guidance — each with some truth but each debatable.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highIranoilUS deficitsBank of Japancredit rating downgradesKevin Warsh
So what: A concrete checklist of which headlines to fade versus respect — the ones that resolve (Iran, tariff impulse) are temporary, the ones that persist (deficits, AI issuance) are not.
Large hyperscalers have begun spending beyond their free cash flow and are now tapping both debt and equity markets for AI capital, creating a crowding-out narrative in the bond market.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highhyperscalersAI capexcorporate bond marketcrowding out
So what: A structural, non-transitory source of upward yield pressure — watch hyperscaler debt issuance calendars as a direct input to long-end supply.
“a lot of these big hyperscalers have started going beyond their free cash flow and are now hitting the debt markets and the equity markets for capital” ▶ 13:40
Public perception of inflation is systematically overstated because people compare price levels rather than rates of change — if eggs go from $3 to $6 a dozen and then stay at $6 for a year, there is no egg inflation, despite the price being twice what it was.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highCPIinflation expectations
So what: Explains a persistent gap between survey inflation expectations and market breakevens, and why sentiment-driven inflation trades can stay wrong for a long time.
“If the price of eggs goes from $3 a dozen to $6 a dozen and then stays at six for a year, there is no inflation in eggs despite the fact it's twice as much as it was a year or two ago.” ▶ 15:12
The Bank of Japan intervention narrative may be structurally wrong, because the Treasury and Fed operate a repo program with Japan that lets it raise dollars without selling US Treasury bonds outright — but the story moves the market regardless.
contrarian🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumBank of JapanUS TreasuryFederal Reserverepo
So what: A narrative to fade: Japanese intervention headlines may not produce the Treasury selling flow the market prices in.
Inflation data vs the hawkish pivot 6
Core CPI at 2.5%, trimmed mean PCE at 2.3% and breakeven inflation expectations at 2.5% are all sitting exactly where they were before the Iran war started — meaning inflation itself does not justify where rates are now.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highcore CPItrimmed mean PCEbreakevensIran
So what: The empirical backbone of the long-duration case: the war-driven inflation impulse has not contaminated underlying inflation measures.
“inflation itself does not warrant rates being where they are right now” ▶ 11:32
The Dallas Fed trimmed mean has been stuck at 2.28% for four or five months and median PCE is 2.7% — and trimmed mean is the very measure Fed Chair Warsh said publicly, both at his Senate testimony and after taking the chair, that he prefers over headline PCE and CPI.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highDallas Fed trimmed meanmedian PCEKevin WarshFederal Reserve
So what: Warsh's own preferred gauge contradicts his hawkish rhetoric — an internal inconsistency that makes the hawkish pivot look like jawboning rather than a data-driven reaction.
“when you look at the Dallas Fed mean trimmed, it's 2.28%. It's been there for four or five months” ▶ 33:01
Warsh's Jackson Hole hawkish pivot rested on the statistic that 54% of goods showed price increases above 3%, but unweighted item counts are analytically meaningless because CPI components are weighted — the number of items is less consequential than their weight, just as Nvidia's 8% S&P 500 weight matters far more than the 499th company's.
contrarian🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highKevin WarshJackson HoleCPINvidiaS&P 500
So what: A methodological attack on the Fed's own hawkish evidence — if the market comes to agree, the hawkish repricing in the front end unwinds.
“the number of items is less consequential than the waiting of the items” ▶ 34:08
Fed research itself shows much of the recent inflation impulse is due to tariffs and that this impulse is waning, making the timing of the hawkish pivot especially curious.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highFederal Reservetariffsinflation
So what: Provides a mechanical reason to expect inflation prints to decelerate — the catalyst that would validate the lower-yields thesis.
Labor market strength is unreliable: a strong employment report is contradicted by massive recent revisions, ADP does not confirm it, JOLTS does not confirm it, and the most recent data at the time of Warsh's speech showed minus 23,000 jobs.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highnonfarm payrollsADPJOLTSKevin Warsh
So what: Removes the growth justification for hiking and argues the next data revisions skew dovish — the asymmetry that would break the yield uptrend.
“I know one month does not make a trend, especially given the revisions we've had” ▶ 12:21
Averaging recent trends, the US economy is growing at about a third of the rate it should be, real wages are flat to negative, and if you strip out AI, economic growth has probably been flat to negative.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highUS GDPreal wagesAI capex
So what: The ex-AI economy is arguably already stagnant, which is the recession-adjacent case that would send yields sharply lower.
“if you strip out AI, it's probably been flat to negative” ▶ 12:56
The 5% line in the sand 5
A 5% 10-year Treasury yield is a line in the sand for the economy, the stock market, definitely the Treasury and possibly the Fed, and likely represents the maximum upside in yield in the short run.
prediction🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: medium10-year TreasuryUS TreasuryFederal ReserveS&P 500
So what: Gives a specific level at which to begin scaling into duration, and a level whose breach would invalidate the thesis.
“I do think that 10-year bond yields at 5%. Is potentially a line in the sand for both the economy, the stock market, and definitely the Treasury, and possibly the Fed.” ▶ 0:07
The market put at 5% is more powerful than the central planner put, because insurance companies, pension funds and endowments will buy at 5% for asset-liability matching reasons — and that bid is probably already creeping in via laddering below 5%.
thesis🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highinsurance companiespension fundsendowments10-year Treasury
So what: Reframes the yield ceiling as a private-sector ALM bid rather than a policy intervention — meaning it can arrive without any Fed action.
“I think the market may step in at 5% before central planners.” ▶ 19:12
Treasury Secretary Bessent has already intervened by increasing buybacks, while the Fed is a less likely backstop because Warsh has been adamant that QE is a bad thing.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumScott BessentUS TreasuryKevin WarshQE
So what: Identifies the Treasury, not the Fed, as the active official backstop for the long end — watch buyback announcements rather than FOMC statements.
Equity weakness would be a self-reinforcing catalyst for lower yields, because money that rotated from bonds to stocks chasing 20% annual returns would rotate back once 5% risk-free looks like a no-brainer against falling equity prices.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highS&P 500US Treasuriesasset allocation
So what: Makes long duration a hedge that pays off precisely when equity exposure hurts — a genuine diversification argument at current yields.
Getting yields down quickly would require an event — absent something severely bad happening to the economy, yields will not fall fast because the narratives remain in place.
prediction🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumUS Treasuries10-year yield
So what: Sets expectations for a grind rather than a snapback, arguing against leveraged or short-dated expressions of the duration trade.
“it would take an event to push yields down quickly” ▶ 18:24
Bond mean-reversion opportunity 7
Trailing 10-year annualized bond returns are at generational lows comparable to the 1950s and 1861, and historically forward returns shoot up sharply from those low points — effectively the inverse of the CAPE chart for equities.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highUS TreasuriesCAPE ratiobond returns
So what: A long-horizon valuation signal that argues for structurally raising fixed income allocation from a starting point of maximum unpopularity.
CAPE is sitting at roughly 41, implying zero equity returns over the next 10 years, setting up a juxtaposition where equities have done really well and are poised to head lower while bonds are putting in a horrendous performance.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highCAPE ratioS&P 500US Treasuries
So what: The relative-value case for rotating from equities to bonds rests on two opposing valuation extremes at once.
“Cape is sitting at like 41, implying that returns for the next 10 years are going to be zero” ▶ 22:22
This bond drawdown is worse than prior episodes because rates were held so low for so long that there was no coupon to offset price declines — in the 1950s analogue coupons were 2.5%, not 1.5% — which also means today's near-5% coupons make the next cycle materially different.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highUS Treasuriescoupons1950s
So what: Explains why past bond bear markets are a poor guide to forward returns from here — the carry cushion has been restored.
Buying a five-year bond at 5% is effectively a free option: the worst case is holding to maturity and earning 5% a year, while if yields fall to 2.5% and stocks drop 30% you sell the bond for a price profit and buy equities on sale.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: high5-year TreasuryS&P 500asset allocation
So what: The cleanest expression of the trade for individual investors: hold-to-maturity floor plus optionality to fund an equity drawdown purchase.
“You have that free option to sell the bond, take your price profit, and now buy stocks on sale.” ▶ 25:02
5% yields are already available today in good corporate names because corporates trade at a spread to Treasuries — so investors do not have to wait for the 10-year to reach 5% to access that level of income.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highcorporate bondscredit spreadsUS Treasuries
So what: An immediately actionable entry point at the stated target yield, with credit risk as the tradeoff.
The right entry is confirmation, not the exact low — it is better to buy the 10-year at 4.50% with high confidence it is going to 2.50% than to catch a falling knife at 5%.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: high10-year Treasuryrisk management
So what: A discipline rule that trades away top-ticking for a materially better risk/reward — relevant to anyone tempted to average down into the selloff.
“I'd rather be at a point where we buy it let's just hypothetically say the tenure at 450 versus 5% but a lot more confidence that we're at 450 going to 250 versus catching a falling knife at 5%” ▶ 28:50
The guest openly concedes the call has been wrong for the last couple of years — right for a while, then recently wrong because of Iran — and stresses that bonds require a more patient outlook than stocks.
anecdote🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumReal Investment AdviceLance RobertsIran
So what: Calibrates the track record: this is a repeatedly-early thesis, which argues for laddering in rather than a single large position.
“we've been wrong for the last couple years. You know, right for a while, now recently wrong because of Iran.” ▶ 45:23
Fed policy-mistake risk 9
The market has already effectively hiked for the Fed — auto loans, credit card rates, mortgages and corporate loans are tied to the three-to-ten-year part of the curve and have risen 50 to 75 basis points, with a 3-to-9 month lag before the tightening shows up in the economy.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highmortgage ratesauto loanscorporate borrowingFederal Reserve
So what: Quantifies the tightening already in the pipeline — a Fed hike on top of it compounds an economic slowdown that hasn't yet appeared in data.
“They've gone up significantly, 50 to 75 basis points. So, there has been a lot of tightening in the economy, and that can take anywhere from 3 to nine months to start showing up.” ▶ 16:40
The Fed runs the risk of a big policy mistake if it hikes, especially multiple times, because it would be tightening into a supply shock — before the Iran war nobody thought the Fed would be raising rates, and the employment market has gotten decidedly weaker since.
contrarian🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highFederal ReserveKevin WarshIransupply shock
So what: The clearest articulated risk to the growth and equity outlook — hiking into an oil-driven supply shock is the classic recession-inducing error.
“I think he runs the risk of a big policy mistake if they hike, especially if they hike multiple times” ▶ 36:01
The market prices roughly a two-thirds chance the Fed raises rates at the meeting a week from the recording, but the guest personally does not think Warsh will hike in September, partly because of political pressure ahead of a midterm election.
prediction🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumFederal ReserveKevin WarshDonald Trumpmidterm elections
So what: A contrarian position against a two-thirds market probability — a no-hike surprise would be the near-term catalyst for the whole duration thesis.
“the market is saying there's a twothirds chance they raise rates” ▶ 39:11
Doing nothing is also a yield risk: if the Fed stands pat the market may conclude Warsh is weak on inflation, and that could be precisely what takes the 10-year to 5%.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumFederal ReserveKevin Warsh10-year Treasury
So what: A genuinely two-sided trap: both outcomes of the meeting have a plausible path to higher yields, so position sizing should reflect event risk both ways.
“if he comes out and they do nothing, the market may say he's being weak on inflation, and that may be what's get gets 10-year yields to 5%” ▶ 36:37
The curve flattened after Warsh's speech — short rates up, long rates roughly flat — which indicates the market accepts his inflation-fighting credibility, and further flattening should follow as the market fully prices a hiking path.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highyield curveKevin WarshFederal Reserve
So what: Argues for flatteners as the expression of the hawkish scenario, since hawkish credibility pulls long yields down even as front-end rates rise.
If the Fed hikes this fall, it will most likely be a credibility-establishing move that sets up rate cuts in 2027 — a view the guest shares with Darius Dale, who expects a quarter-point hike and possibly another to establish hawkish bona fides.
prediction🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumFederal ReserveKevin WarshDarius Dale
So what: Implies any hike-driven selloff in the front end is a buying opportunity for 2027 cuts — the tactical trade inside the hawkish scenario.
The hawkish internal case is Beth Hammock's — inflation is still above 2% and the Fed needs to get it to 2% — a goal the guest agrees with while warning to be careful about how it gets there.
thesis🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumBeth HammackFederal Reserveinflation target

“they need to get it down to two, but be careful how you get it down to two” ▶ 38:30
Warsh's refusal to give guidance is itself hurting the bond market, because the market feels rudderless without the Fed walking it on a leash.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumKevin WarshFederal Reserveforward guidance
So what: Communication policy is now an independent driver of term premium — reduced guidance mechanically widens the range of yield outcomes.
The midterms are not yet being cited as a reason for higher bond yields but probably will become a narrative — a split Congress with Democrats taking the House could limit spending, which cuts both ways since dysfunction is also bad.
prediction🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: lowUS midterm electionsCongressfiscal policyUS Treasuries
So what: Flags an emerging narrative to watch before it is priced — election-driven fiscal expectations will become a term-premium input.
Positioning and portfolio construction 6
Real Investment Advice's core 60/40 portfolio currently holds roughly 10% cash alongside a 20-year bond, shorter-term bonds and mortgages — a starting position in duration rather than an empty slate waiting to buy.
data_point🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highReal Investment Advice60/40 portfoliomortgage bonds
So what: Shows the manager is already long the thesis, so incremental buying is add-on rather than initiation — relevant to how aggressively they can press.
The triggers to add duration are technical signs the tide is turning, a shift in narrative, and more inflation data — with a 0.2% CPI print making three months in a row of relatively benign inflation and the start of a trend, while oil back in the low-to-mid 90s still pushes the other way.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highCPIPPIoil pricesFederal Reserve
So what: A specific, checkable trigger list for scaling into bonds rather than a vague directional view.
The expression of the trade will not be limited to Treasuries — depending on whether short yields drop while the long end stays pinned by inflation, deficit and Fed-confidence narratives, the position could be in three-to-five-year sectors, corporate bonds or options.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: mediumcorporate bondsoptionsyield curve
So what: Signals that the highest-conviction part of the curve is the belly if the long end stays term-premium-constrained.
Equity exposure is at record levels across all investor cohorts and rises with investor age, which is backwards given that older cohorts should be de-risking.
data_point🗣 Adam Taggart, hostconviction: highhousehold equity allocationretirement investorsLance Roberts
So what: Identifies a large, demographically concentrated pool of forced sellers if equities correct — and the flow that would fund the rotation back into bonds.
“it seems to go up the older the investor cohort is, which is crazy because they should be de-risking” ▶ 46:58
The correct wealth benchmark is the return you need to meet your goals, not the S&P 500 — if financial planning software says a 5% return gives a 98% chance of meeting goals, then locking 5% in a 30-year bond, a 10-year corporate or a 5-year corporate is the rational choice regardless of equity returns.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highfinancial planningS&P 500corporate bonds
So what: Reframes the bond allocation decision as goal-funding rather than return-maximizing, which is the argument that actually moves retiree allocations.
The psychological workaround for holding bonds through volatility is to buy the actual bond rather than a fund and commit to holding to maturity — worst case you earn 5% for five or seven years, best case you sell at a profit and rotate into other assets.
framework🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highindividual bondsbond funds
So what: A practical implementation detail that changes the risk profile: individual bonds eliminate the mark-to-market forced-sale problem that bond funds impose.
AI's ambiguous rate impact 4
The secular lower-yields thesis at Real Investment Advice is intact — yields should trend back toward the real growth rate over the next couple of years — but AI is the genuine complication because it is inflationary now via memory chip prices while eventually being both growth-positive and disinflationary.

So what: Names the specific variable that could break an otherwise high-conviction secular bond call — timing of AI's inflation-to-disinflation crossover.

Memory chip prices have spiked over roughly the last six months and Apple has raised prices as a result, making AI capex a currently inflationary force rather than a disinflationary one.

So what: A concrete goods-inflation channel from AI to CPI that complicates near-term disinflation prints — watch memory pricing as an inflation input.
The data center buildout is massive and represents a good chunk of US economic growth, which is why stripping AI out leaves growth flat to negative.
causal🗣 Michael Lebowitz, Real Investment Adviceconviction: highdata centersUS GDPAI capex
So what: Makes AI capex the single point of failure for reported US growth — a capex slowdown would rapidly validate the lower-yields call.

The Prof G Show · Scott Galloway 1 episode(s) · 29 insights

Stanford Professor: Don’t Build Your Life Around Your Kids57 min29 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
A live on-stage conversation in San Francisco between Scott Galloway and Stanford's Bill Burnett and Dave Evans (authors of Designing Your Life and How to Live a Meaningful Life) about career design, meaning, and partnership. Despite the title, the parenting line is a single audience-Q&A answer; the bulk is career-market and life-design content — dysfunctional beliefs about passion and majors, the jobs/careers/callings split, why AI resume screening has broken the apply-online channel, and Galloway's view that economic security precedes meaning. Investor-relevant signal is limited but real: concrete labor-market mechanics, hiring funnel conversion rates, post-pandemic quit behavior, and the human-capital case for relational networking over job boards.
