Labor Market & Hiring Funnel Mechanics 7
Online job applications now convert at roughly 3-5%: a Stanford student who hand-wrote 100 cover letters and submitted 100 resumes received only three responses, and most of those applications were never read by a human because AI screening filtered them first.
data_point🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highStanfordAI resume screeningjob boardsLinkedIn
So what: A hard conversion benchmark for the applicant side of the hiring funnel — it quantifies why job-board and ATS-driven models are losing candidate trust and why referral-based hiring products are gaining share.
“I sent in a 100 resumes. I got three responses. I said, 'Well, then you're that's about average. It's three to five percent.” ▶ 51:26
Even in the pre-AI era a single posted design role at Apple drew about 5,000 resumes within a minute, and hiring managers get no extra time allocated for interviewing, so internal referrals are what actually move a candidate onto the shortlist.
anecdote🗣 Bill Burnett, former Apple designer and Stanford educatorconviction: highApplerecruitinghiring managers
So what: Applicant volume has always overwhelmed reviewer capacity at desirable employers; AI screening is a response to that constraint, not its cause, which means referral and network-based sourcing keeps its structural advantage.
“we post a thing for a designer. We get 5,000 resumes in a minute” ▶ 53:23
Weak-tie networks — a friend of a parent's college roommate rather than close contacts — are argued to be the only reliable channel for getting a resume past automated screening to a human reviewer, even in an AI-mediated hiring market.
thesis🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highLinkedInAI resume screeningweak tiesjob boards
So what: If true, the value of employment platforms shifts from listings and matching toward verified relationship graphs and referral distribution — the part of LinkedIn that automation cannot commoditize.
“we tell our students, don't go to the job boards, don't go to LinkedIn, don't do all that stuff.” ▶ 50:38
Job seekers systematically misjudge the size of the employer universe: a Stanford career counselor notes students behave as if the world equals the companies that do on-campus recruiting, which is roughly 0.01% of companies globally.
causal🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highStanfordon-campus recruitingsmall business
So what: The visible-employer bias explains persistent mismatch between elite-graduate unemployment complaints and open roles — and points demand at tools that surface non-recruiting small and mid-market employers.
“The students believe the world is the collection of companies that do on-ampus recruiting. That's 0.01% of the companies on the planet.” ▶ 53:59
Two-thirds of US job creation comes from small and medium-sized businesses, which are also the employers least discoverable through job boards, LinkedIn or business press coverage.
data_point🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highsmall businessSMBsUS labor marketOdoo
So what: SMB hiring is the majority of the labor market but the minority of recruiting-platform inventory — a structural gap that favors SMB-focused software and local hiring channels.
“twothirds of job creation is from small and mediumsiz business” ▶ 54:31
The average starting salary at NYU Stern's business school is now $212,000, and graduate complaints about not finding work are usually about not getting the specific job they wanted rather than an absence of offers.
data_point🗣 Scott Galloway, NYU Stern professorconviction: highNYU SternMBAbusiness schools
So what: Elite MBA compensation remains extremely high even amid white-collar hiring anxiety — a signal that the pain is concentrated in mid-tier credentials, not at the top of the pipeline.
“NY Stern, this the average starting salary now at a business school is $212,000” ▶ 54:53
Roughly 52 million people quit their jobs in the post-pandemic Great Resignation, and when surveyed afterward the dominant reason the move failed to satisfy them was lack of impact rather than lack of fulfillment, at roughly an 80/20 split.
data_point🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: mediumGreat ResignationCOVID-19labor market
So what: Retention programs built on perks and self-actualization are aimed at the minority driver; employers competing on demonstrated organizational impact should see better retention economics.
“after the pandemic and then the great resignation, 52 million people walk off the job looking for something better” ▶ 23:25
Dysfunctional Career Beliefs 6
Ten years after graduation, no more than 20% of students are doing anything related to their college major, making major choice a weak predictor of career outcome.
data_point🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highhigher educationStanfordcollege majors
So what: Undermines the pricing logic of major-specific degree premiums and supports demand for shorter, restackable credentials over four-year specialization.
“10 years out of school, the data is no less than 20% of you will have anything will be doing something that has anything to do with your major.” ▶ 7:35
'Follow your passion' is presented as actively bad advice because Stanford researcher Bill Damon found fewer than 20% of people have a single identifiable passion, and most of that minority's passions are in the arts — leaving eight in ten people feeling defective.
contrarian🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highBill DamonStanfordPath to Purpose
So what: A research-backed rebuttal to the dominant career-guidance narrative — relevant to how education and career-services products should be positioned and to why passion-framed offerings underperform.
“Less than 20% of people have a singular identifiable passion.” ▶ 8:28
Career regret should be treated as a sunk cost: prior training in law or accounting has no bearing on whether to change direction, because the only relevant decision is designing forward from the current position.
framework🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highsunk costcareer change
So what: Explicitly imports an economics concept into career switching — the mental model that supports mid-career retraining demand and later-life professional mobility.
“It's never too late because it's a sunk cost, right? Economics is a sunk cost to start from where you are and design your way forward.” ▶ 7:52
Work should be sorted horizontally into jobs (done for money), careers (done for professional development) and callings (done for mission and values realization), rather than ranked vertically with calling as superior.
framework🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highcareer designStanford
So what: A usable taxonomy for assessing employer value propositions and for why compensation-led employers need not compete on mission — the two axes are separable.
“You do jobs for money, careers for professional development, and callings for missional fulfillment and values realization.” ▶ 12:03
The expectation that meaning-making and money-making should happen in the same activity is a recent Western assumption, and the vocational lifestyle where work supplies all of life's meaning is rare rather than normal.
thesis🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highcareer designlabor economics
So what: If workers decouple meaning from employment, mission-branded employer positioning loses leverage and compensation plus flexibility regain it — a live variable in white-collar retention.
“the overwhelming majority of human history, meaning making and money-making were two different things.” ▶ 10:51
A high-performing heart surgeon is cited as treating his practice purely as a job done for money, and claiming he is better at it precisely because he is not emotionally invested in patients or in the professional identity.
anecdote🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: mediumhealthcaremedicineprofessional services
So what: Counters the assumption that mission alignment drives professional performance — relevant to how service businesses think about hiring for passion versus competence.
“He does it for the money. He doesn't tell his patients because it would make them nervous.” ▶ 12:33
Prototyping as Career Risk Management 5
The design-thinking method applied to careers is to run small, cheap prototypes — a conversation with someone doing the work, a brief trial experience — rather than attempting to reason out the right answer in advance.
framework🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highdesign thinkingStanford d.schoolIDEODavid Kelley
So what: This is optionality-purchasing applied to human capital: small experiments buy information cheaply before a large, irreversible career allocation.
“Prototyping is just run a little experiment.” ▶ 17:10
Building any prototype changes the option set itself, because observers react to a tangible artifact with ideas they could never have generated from a description — an effect the speaker observed repeatedly at Apple.
causal🗣 Bill Burnett, former Apple designerconviction: highAppleprototypingproduct design
So what: Justifies shipping rough artifacts early in product and venture decisions: the information yield comes from the reaction, not the artifact, which lowers the cost of discovering demand.
“As soon as you have a prototype, you change the world as possible.” ▶ 20:29
The correct progress metric when stuck is whether you made a move, not whether you solved the problem — the advice is explicitly to stop measuring 'have I fixed it yet' and measure action taken.
framework🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highcareer designdecision making
So what: An operational rule for allocating effort under deep uncertainty — the same logic that favors iteration over planning in early-stage ventures.
“There is no figuring it out. There's only getting on with it.” ▶ 17:48
Career success is framed primarily as tolerance for rejection rather than talent or timing, with the claim that raising money, finding jobs and starting businesses were never easy even in the strongest bull markets and even with substantial structural advantages.
thesis🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highventure fundraisingstartupsjob search
So what: A founder-side reality check that fundraising difficulty is not a market-cycle signal — useful for discounting 'the market is closed' explanations of failed raises.
“I think the secret to success is rejection. Specifically, your willingness to endure rejection.” ▶ 48:28
Grit is presented as largely a circumstances problem rather than a character trait: Angela Duckworth, asked to make students grittier, returned instead with a list of about 20 material conditions the school was failing to provide, such as hot meals and teacher-funded supplies.
contrarian🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: mediumAngela Duckwortheducation policygrit
So what: Reframes human-capital underperformance as an input-provision failure rather than a motivation failure — the same argument used against workforce programs that train mindset instead of removing constraints.
“Your school system doesn't work because not because the kids aren't gritty, because you aren't giving them the circumstances they need.” ▶ 52:59
Economic Security, Luck & Partnership Markets 7
Galloway argues economic security is a prerequisite rather than an alternative to meaning, describing a deliberately sequential strategy — pure wealth accumulation first at the cost of his hair and first marriage, purpose-seeking only after achieving financial security.
thesis🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highcapitalismwealth accumulationSan Francisco tech
So what: A sequencing framework for human-capital allocation that argues against early-career mission optimization — directly opposed to purpose-first employer branding.
“I had no purpose. I had no meaning. It cost me my hair. It cost me my first marriage and it was absolutely worth it.” ▶ 22:04
Galloway accuses the tech industry of claiming social mission while depressing teenagers to sell more advertising, and says the honest position would be to admit the business is about money and pursue impact later with the proceeds.
contrarian🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: hightech industrysocial mediadigital advertising
So what: Continued elite-commentator framing of social advertising as a teen-mental-health harm keeps regulatory and reputational risk live for ad-funded consumer platforms.
About 75% of women cite economic viability as central in selecting a mate versus roughly 25% of men, and in San Francisco a man in a couple is still twice as likely to be the higher earner despite income parity between men and women up to the point women have children.
data_point🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highSan Franciscohypergamygender pay gaplabor force participation
So what: The motherhood penalty rather than entry-level pay is identified as the break point in income parity — the variable that matters for female labor-force participation and for childcare and parental-leave policy exposure.
“in San Francisco, we now have fortunately this wonderful thing called income parody among men and women until women have kids and then the corporate world takes them down again” ▶ 26:57
Galloway attributes outsized personal advantage to demographics and timing, noting America generated 30% of world GDP growth from 1945 to 2000 with two-thirds of that captured by roughly a third of the population that matched his own profile.
data_point🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highUnited StatesGDP growthpost-war economy
So what: A framing of postwar returns as a cohort and geography windfall rather than individual skill — the base rate to apply when underwriting founder track records from that era.
“America 30% of the world's GDP growth 1945 to 2000 and twothirds of that was crammed into the one-third of the population that looks, smelt and felt like me.” ▶ 15:10
Partner selection is presented as an empirically studied problem with known answers, citing John and Julie Gottman's Love Lab, which observed and videotaped couples to identify behavioral markers separating relationship 'masters' from 'disasters' — chiefly values alignment and a shared image of the future.
framework🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highJohn GottmanJulie GottmanGottman InstituteSeattle
So what: Points to an observational, behavior-first research method — and to a category (relationship assessment) where evidence-based products can differentiate against intuition-driven incumbents.
“we don't need to argue the theor theoretical ideas or psychological psychological models about this. We just need to observe what works and what doesn't.” ▶ 29:55
The Harvard adult development study — the longest-running study of humans, spanning three generations and 75 years — concluded in George Vaillant's summary that happiness is love, full stop.
data_point🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highHarvard Study of Adult DevelopmentBob WaldingerGeorge Vaillant
So what: The single most-cited longitudinal finding on wellbeing points to relationships, not income or achievement — a counterweight when assessing consumer demand for status and achievement products.
“happiness is love. Full stop.” ▶ 35:49
The positive psychology movement's original happiness framework, launched by Martin Seligman and Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi about 20 years ago, has been largely debunked, and the field has shifted from happiness to flourishing and thriving.
causal🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highMartin SeligmanMihaly Csikszentmihalyipositive psychology
So what: A caution on the evidence base beneath the wellness and happiness-app category — the underlying science has already been revised once.
“the first book was called authentic happiness. and there's a happiness equation and the book has largely been debunked as not particularly true.” ▶ 6:38
Parenting as an Allocation Decision 4
The title advice comes from a single audience-Q&A anecdote: a mother drew her son a pie chart at age 18 showing the parenting phase as a cross-hatched slice of an 80-year life, concluding that you should not design your life around children because the role ends when they leave.
anecdote🗣 Bill Burnett, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highparentingempty nest
So what: Frames parenting as a time-bounded allocation rather than a permanent identity — the demographic mindset that supports demand for midlife reinvention, education and second-career services.
“Don't design your life around your children. They don't last. Love them when you have them, but have something else in mind.” ▶ 44:56
Galloway describes parenting explicitly as an investment he knows carries permanently negative ROI, and argues that repeated willing overinvestment in something with no measurable return is itself the reliable signal of genuine purpose.
framework🗣 Scott Galloway, hostconviction: highparentingphilanthropyROI
So what: An inversion of transactional thinking that identifies where someone will keep spending irrespective of returns — useful for reading durable consumer categories and for founder motivation diligence.
“I am never going to get a positive ROI on my boys.” ▶ 33:24
The guests reject purpose as a noun to be found, arguing instead for living purposefully via a be-do-become cycle, so an empty-nest parent has not lost their purpose but has succeeded at it and should grieve, celebrate, then look for the next invitation.
framework🗣 Dave Evans, Stanford educator and co-authorconviction: highpurposelife design
So what: Reframes midlife role loss as a transition rather than a deficit — the demand-side logic behind the growing market for later-life education and encore careers.
“You you didn't lose your purpose. You succeeded at it.”
Galloway calls the state of young men a crisis requiring more mentorship, and a guest counters with his own son's three arrests during college who now serves as chief of staff for a US congressman — arguing parental involvement extends well past the college drop-off.
anecdote🗣 Bill Burnett and Scott Gallowayconviction: mediumyoung menmentorshiphigher education
So what: The young-male-disengagement theme remains a persistent thesis in Galloway's commentary and a recurring driver of his media and book franchise.
“it is a crisis and young men need you know need more mentorship” ▶ 42:28

Forward Guidance · Blockworks 1 episode(s) · 29 insights

The Bond Market Is Trapping The Fed | Weekly Roundup45 min29 insights2026-09-11
▶ Watch on YouTube
Two Blockworks hosts, recording the day after a firm PPI print and the day before CPI, argue that Bessent's $6 billion long-end buyback was called by the bond market — yields rose anyway — with a nominal-GDP regression implying 10-year fair value near 5.8% versus a market being held roughly 100bp below it. They frame the Fed as facing a fork with two bad paths into a $100 oil shock, handicap a September hike (odds moved to ~70%), and argue a term-premium-driven 'credibility hike' would actually pull long-end yields down, while the debasement trade stays intact but path-risky and the levered semiconductor/AI trade needs six to twelve months of healing.
Treasury buybacks versus the bond market · 4The Fed's fork with two bad paths · 8Long-end composition and the credibility-hike trade · 3Reading the inflation data · 4Growth, AI capex and the post-midterm air pocket · 4Debasement trade and the levered momentum unwind · 6
Treasury buybacks versus the bond market 4
Bessent escalated long-end Treasury buybacks from the traditional $2 billion to a $6 billion announcement — above his own $4 billion minimum guidance — and the long end still sold off with yields higher, which the hosts read as the bond market calling his bluff and demanding roughly double the ammunition.
data_point🗣 host (Blockworks, Forward Guidance)conviction: highScott BessentUS TreasuryTreasury buybackslong-end yields
So what: The size threshold matters: if the next buyback escalation is not dramatically larger, the intervention has been proven ineffective and long-end yields have room to keep rising.
“the market saying that look, $6 billion dollars of buybacks is not enough if you're trying to fight us. Um, you got to at least double that thing, buddy.” ▶ 8:17
A regression of the 10-year against nominal GDP puts fair value near 5.8% on roughly 6.6% nominal GDP, meaning the administration is attempting to hold at least a 100 basis point spread below fair value through intervention.
framework🗣 host (Blockworks, Forward Guidance)conviction: medium10-year Treasurynominal GDPUS TreasuryScott Bessent
So what: Gives a concrete anchor for how far the long end could travel if intervention fails or nominal GDP stays hot — a ~100bp gap to close.
“the fair value of the 10-year is somewhere around 5.8% right now roughly speaking” ▶ 7:31
The yen leg of the intervention has quietly worked where the bond leg has not — formal intervention began in the 160s and the pair is now at 153, helped by a more hawkish Bank of Japan.
data_point🗣 host (Blockworks, Forward Guidance)conviction: highJapanese yenBank of JapanUS TreasuryFX intervention
So what: Intervention efficacy is asset-specific: currency intervention is holding while bond-market intervention is failing, which argues against treating 'the trade is manipulated' as a blanket thesis.
“the yen has worked out, honestly, which is, you know, I feel like it's kind of gone understated” ▶ 7:58
Bessent's behaviour is best modelled as rational political incentive rather than ideology — he built his reputation at Soros breaking the Bank of England and would be on the other side of this trade if he were outside government, but with midterms a month and a half away he has to balance suppressing yields and weakening the currency against the inflation those actions create.
framework🗣 Quinn, co-hostconviction: highScott BessentSorosBank of Englandmidterm electionsUS Treasury
So what: Positioning should assume intervention persists until the midterms and then loses its political sponsor — the incentive clock, not the rhetoric, is the trade.
“he's not the devil and he's not the Lord. Like he's he's just another, you know, politician and policy maker trying to keep the ball afloat” ▶ 5:59
The Fed's fork with two bad paths 8
Central banks hiking into an energy supply shock has many historical precedents across Europe and the US and the hikes are typically reversed within twelve months, because rate hikes do not create more oil and in fact restrict production by raising financing costs.
framework🗣 Quinn, co-hostconviction: highFederal ReserveECBoilenergy shockrecession
So what: If the Fed hikes into $100 oil, position for the reversal rather than the cycle — historically the hike is unwound inside a year and sows a recession.
“central bank hikes don't create more oil” ▶ 15:27
The other path is equally bad: weakening the dollar, stimulating commodities, suppressing bond yields and intervening to support asset prices all inject demand into a supply shock, which is the COVID-style inflation mechanism — the correct policy is to sit still and let the supply side resolve.
thesis🗣 Quinn, co-hostconviction: highFederal ReserveUS dollarcommoditiesoilinflation
So what: Frames both policy branches as inflationary or recessionary — the direct implication is that real assets and inflation hedges keep working across both paths.
“you don't want to be stimulating demand into a supply shock otherwise you get COVID crazy levels of inflation” ▶ 17:14
That a hike is even on the table is the direct consequence of the administration's own stimulus — the SPR, bond market and currency interventions, and tariff refunds — rather than of organic economic strength.
causal🗣 Quinn, co-hostconviction: highFederal ReserveSPRtariffsUS TreasuryScott Bessent
So what: The policy mix is self-defeating: fiscal and market intervention is generating the inflation that forces the monetary tightening it was designed to avoid.
The right policy would be to stop hiking on the front end and let the long end find fair value, but political incentives prevent it because the housing market has ground to a halt and financing conditions are already very restrictive for longer-tail small businesses and consumers.
thesis🗣 Quinn, co-hostconviction: highFederal Reservehousingsmall businesslong-end yields

Long-end composition and the credibility-hike trade 3
Historical analysis of long-bond behaviour around first hikes shows the composition of the yield rise matters: when inflation expectations lead, long yields keep rising through the hike, but when term premium is the driver — as it is now — a one-and-done credibility hike is followed by long-bond yields falling.
framework🗣 host (Blockworks, Forward Guidance)conviction: mediumterm premiuminflation expectations30-year TreasuryFederal Reserve

Tom Lee: Inflation Fears Are Overblown — Crypto Could Rip Higher37 min26 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Fundstrat's Tom Lee argues the core PCE-versus-core CPI gap is a measurement artifact driven by portfolio management fees and flash memory prices rather than real consumer inflation, so the Fed has more room than the bond market believes. He stays constructive on equities for the next 12 months on healthy high-yield spreads and an AI-driven capex cycle, and makes his most aggressive call on crypto — a four-year-cycle bottom, extinguished leverage, returning Korean retail leverage and Wall Street tokenization adoption setting up a bullish 12 months, with Ethereum and financial-services disruption as the preferred expression.
Inflation Is Mismeasured · 5Macro Setup and Credit Signals · 4AI, Labor and the Tax Base · 3Growth Investing Discipline · 2Where to Own AI: Bottlenecks to Banks · 5Crypto: Cycle Bottom and Catalysts · 7
Inflation Is Mismeasured 5
The gap between core PCE at 3.3% and core CPI is one of the largest ever, and more than 60% of the roughly 100 basis point difference comes from just two components — financial services and recreational goods — which are heavily weighted in PCE and barely present in CPI.
data_point🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highPCECPIFederal ReserveFundstrat
So what: If the PCE-CPI gap is compositional rather than real, the hawkish read of PCE is wrong and rate-cut odds are underpriced; the decomposition is checkable each print.
The financial services component of PCE is essentially portfolio management fees, which rise mechanically when the stock market rises, so a booming market inflates measured PCE inflation even though the average American — who mostly does not pay an advisor — experiences no price increase.
causal🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highPCEFederal ReserveChristopher Wallerasset management fees
So what: Creates a perverse feedback loop where equity rallies make the Fed look further from target; Waller has already acknowledged the distortion, making it a live policy argument.
Flash memory prices, which sit in the PCE recreational goods bucket but not in CPI, have risen nine standard deviations and account for roughly a third to 40% of the excess PCE inflation versus CPI, a distortion the Fed itself documented in a paper in May.
data_point🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highflash memoryPCECPIFederal Reservememory prices
So what: The AI-driven memory price spike is literally contaminating the Fed's preferred inflation gauge — a second-order link between the semiconductor cycle and monetary policy that few are modeling.
“Nobody really cares that flash memory is got more expensive.” ▶ 8:27
Core CPI is more likely than not to fall to 2.3% year-over-year in the August print, which alongside historical precedent — Volcker cut with inflation near 4%, not 2% — means the Fed is closer to target and moving the right direction than consensus allows.
prediction🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediumcore CPIFederal ReservePaul Volckerinterest rates
So what: A specific, near-dated, falsifiable call: a 2.3% core CPI print would validate the dovish case and repriced front-end rates.
“Volcker was cutting when inflation was around 4% not when inflation was at 2.” ▶ 8:57
Bond markets and pundits are impatient about inflation not because consumer inflation expectations have risen — surveys show they have not — but because the printed measures have been off target for over four years, making the hawkish case a statistical rather than economic argument.
contrarian🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highbond marketFederal Reserveinflation expectations
So what: Watch consumer inflation expectation surveys, not headline PCE, as the true trigger for a Fed policy mistake.
“I think economists are fighting last year's wars.” ▶ 0:00
Macro Setup and Credit Signals 4
High-yield option-adjusted spreads are a far more timely recession predictor than the yield curve, and they are currently behaving well, signalling no meaningful deterioration in corporate credit quality despite rising rates, elevated inflation and large deficits.
framework🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highhigh yield creditOASyield curvecorporate credit
So what: Gives a single monitorable indicator — HY OAS — whose widening would be the signal to abandon the constructive equity stance.
The constructive 12-month equity view rests on solid earnings visibility fueled by AI plus a recovering ISM and broadening expansion, while the identified worry list is hyperscaler debt issuance, US deficits and the Iran war — conditions that keep investors worried enough for markets to climb a wall of worry.
thesis🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highS&P 500ISMAI hyperscalersUS deficitsIran

prediction🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediummidterm electionsFederal Reservemarket leverageequities
So what: A bullish 12-month view coexisting with an expected correction argues for buying weakness rather than de-risking into it.
For more than a decade after the GFC US private investment as a share of GDP ran below depreciation, actively depleting the capital stock and suppressing productivity, because economists' 'new normal' slow-growth consensus stopped boards from being expansionary.
causal🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highprivate investmentcapexproductivityGoldman SachsJP Morgan
So what: Frames the current AI capex boom as replacement of a decade-plus underinvestment backlog rather than a speculative excess, implying a longer runway than a normal cycle.
AI, Labor and the Tax Base 3
Robots and agentic AI will become tax-generating units rather than a hole in the tax base, particularly if their payments settle on blockchain where they are traceable, and could plausibly be taxed at a higher rate than human labor via a human-penalty tax structure.
prediction🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediumagentic AIroboticsblockchaintaxation

“I think robots are and agents are and agentic AI, they'll pay taxes. They're going to be tax-generating units.” ▶ 15:14
The fully loaded economics of AI are still unknown and the fact that hyperscalers must borrow to fund it suggests current AI pricing may be well below true cost, meaning the real per-user price of AI could end up much higher than today's.
causal🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: exploratoryAI hyperscalerscapexAI pricingdebt issuance
So what: Cuts both ways: it questions current AI unit economics while implying eventual pricing power for whoever survives the capex phase.
“if AI was profitable, then hyperscalers wouldn't have to borrow money” ▶ 15:54
Research from Asana, HubSpot, McKinsey and Fundstrat suggests as much as 75% of jobs are work for work's sake and employees spend only 20-25% of their week doing actual work, so AI can absorb enormous amounts of task volume while leaving headcount intact.
data_point🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediumAsanaHubSpotMcKinseyFundstratlabor productivity
So what: Supports a productivity-boom-without-layoffs path, which would be the most equity-friendly and least politically disruptive AI outcome.
Growth Investing Discipline 2
Most investors are bad growth investors because they anchor total addressable market on what the world does today — the classic error of valuing Amazon as a bookseller — and because new technology creates both speculation and innovation while most people only price the risk.
framework🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highAmazongrowth investingtotal addressable market

Where to Own AI: Bottlenecks to Banks 5
AI is a third vector of economic growth alongside labor and capital because it can deliver costless innovation, and the way to own it is bottlenecks that scale with units — Nvidia, semis, memory, energy and power — plus downstream software and the Mag 7.

Financial services is the industry about to be most innovated by AI, which should convert banking into a less cyclical business and drive multiple expansion, making banks and the bank-heavy Russell 2000 small caps a preferred way to own the theme.
thesis🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highbanksRussell 2000small capsfinancial servicesAI
So what: A re-rating thesis rather than an earnings thesis: the upside comes from multiple expansion on reduced cyclicality, which is a multi-year rather than quarterly call.
Incumbent financial firms are hobbled by layered legacy infrastructure — a single credit card transaction passes through seven intermediary roles each served by seven or eight companies, and a single participant like Capital One may run a hundred legacy systems — which is why Robinhood and the mono-line BlackRock can move to tokenization with far more alacrity.
causal🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: highRobinhoodBlackRockCapital Onetokenizationpayments
So what: Identifies structural simplicity, not size, as the winning attribute in the tokenization transition — favoring new entrants and mono-line incumbents over diversified legacy banks.
Using Robinhood and BlackRock's own figures, roughly $100 trillion of assets could be tokenized, and capturing just 1% of that as net income is a $1.1 trillion earnings opportunity, which at 20 times is a $20 trillion equity value opportunity.
data_point🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediumtokenizationRobinhoodBlackRockcrypto
So what: Gives an explicit TAM to underwrite crypto-infrastructure equity valuations, and the framing is that this creates many new companies rather than one winner.
Energy should not be treated as a growth sector despite being an AI bottleneck, because energy has too often disappointed on hoped-for re-rating and commodity prices exert more influence than expected; the simple expression is just XLE.
contrarian🗣 Tom Lee, Fundstrat head of researchconviction: mediumXLEenergy sectorcommoditiespower demand

Agentic Payment Rails 6
About 76% of the agentic commerce transactions Coinbase observes are under 30 cents, which is below the roughly 30-cent flat minimum fee on a card transaction, meaning card rails are structurally uneconomic for the majority of agent-to-agent commerce.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbaseVisaMastercardstablecoinsagentic commercecard networks
So what: The single hardest number in the episode: it defines a payment volume category card networks cannot serve at all, and is the core bull case for stablecoin rails capturing agent spend.
“about like 76% of the agentic commerce transactions we're seeing are under 30 cents” ▶ 0:11
Coinbase is issuing AI agents their own self-custodial crypto wallets with no KYC process, provisioned by pasting a single prompt into an agent, because agents have no government ID and cannot open conventional bank accounts.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbaseAI agentsself-custodial walletsKYCstablecoins
So what: Regulatory arbitrage is the whole point — crypto rails serve a customer class that identity-based banking structurally cannot, which is the most defensible crypto use case articulated here.
“We don't want the AIs to be unbanked. You know, we want to bank the AIs.” ▶ 0:17
Coinbase incubated the x402 agentic payment protocol and contributed it to the Linux Foundation, with Google, Cloudflare and AWS now collaborating on it, and publishes live flow statistics at agentic.market.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbasex402Linux FoundationGoogleCloudflareAWS
So what: Neutral-foundation governance plus hyperscaler participation is how a payment standard becomes default; agentic.market is a public dataset for tracking the category's growth.
Sub-30-cent agent payments are overwhelmingly for data access — venture firms compiling paywalled research, recruiters scraping LinkedIn, and agents calling other specialized agents for source data as if it were a tool call — making paywalled data the first native product of the agentic economy.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highLinkedInpaywalled dataAI agentsagentic.market
So what: Per-call micropayment monetization of proprietary data is arriving now, which is a live revenue option for any company sitting on a walled dataset.
Small open-weight models fine-tuned on proprietary data — for example 100,000 internal compliance cases at Coinbase — can outperform frontier models on that specialized task, which will populate the agent marketplace with narrow specialist agents rather than generalists.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highopen-weight modelsfrontier modelsCoinbasefine-tuningreinforcement learning
So what: Proprietary data plus small-model fine-tuning is where incumbent moats convert into AI advantage, and it caps how much of the value frontier labs capture.
Money will not disappear as Elon Musk has suggested, because scarcity in land, energy and chips persists even if robots and AI drive price deflation in commodities, food and housing, so a medium of exchange remains necessary and crypto rails are the natural complement to AI.
contrarian🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: mediumElon MuskcryptoAIenergysemiconductorsland
So what: Frames crypto as infrastructure for a machine economy rather than a speculative asset, and names land, energy and chips as the durable scarce assets in a deflationary AI world.

So what: An explicit valuation-dislocation argument from the CEO: the business has diversified away from Bitcoin beta while the equity has not re-rated for it.
“actually 88% of our revenue is from non-Bitcoin trading at this point” ▶ 18:45
Coinbase's newly announced tokenized stock product is the first that is a real security rather than a synthetic, derivative or debt instrument, backed one-to-one by the actual share held in custody, and is available only outside the US while the company works with the SEC on a domestic path.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbasetokenized stocksSECtokenization
So what: SEC approval for US distribution is the discrete catalyst to watch; until then the revenue is offshore-only and the structure is the differentiator versus synthetic competitors.
Roughly 4 billion people worldwide have no access to a brokerage or US investment account and therefore cannot buy Nvidia, Apple or Coinbase shares, and tokenized equities should replicate Tether's non-US success in dollar-denominated accounts.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highTethertokenized stocksNvidiaAppleemerging markets
So what: The Tether analogy is the key precedent: unmet offshore demand for US-denominated exposure drove enormous adoption once a frictionless wrapper existed, and equities are the next wrapper.
Stablecoin payments keep growing even when Bitcoin prices fall, moving money in under one second for under one cent anywhere in the world, which makes them a structurally non-cyclical business line inside a cyclical company.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highstablecoinsCoinbaseBitcoincross-border payments
So what: A crypto revenue stream decoupled from token prices is the strongest argument for re-rating crypto infrastructure away from beta multiples.
“money can now move at the speed of information, just like a WhatsApp message” ▶ 20:37
Crypto currently powers only about half of 1% of global GDP with roughly 700 million people having used or held it but likely only 50 to 100 million using it monthly, so the growth path is getting monthly actives to a billion or more.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highcrypto adoptionglobal GDPCoinbase
So what: Gives the denominator for the entire sector thesis and a concrete metric — monthly active crypto users, not holders — to track penetration against.
“crypto is really only powering about half of 1% of global GDP right now” ▶ 36:20
Prediction Markets Beyond Sports 2
Coinbase's prediction markets hit roughly a $100 million revenue run rate within months of launch and are growing over 100% quarter over quarter, making it one of the fastest-scaling product lines the company has disclosed.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbaseprediction marketssports betting
So what: Prediction markets are becoming a material revenue line for exchanges, not a novelty — directly relevant to how incumbent sportsbooks and brokers should be valued.
The larger opportunity in prediction markets is policy and opinion markets — pricing what unemployment would be one, two or three years after a given policy, or open-ended questions with no resolution date that reprice as new evidence arrives, much like a stock.
framework🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: mediumprediction marketsopinion marketspolicy analysis
So what: Perpetual, never-resolving opinion markets would be a genuinely new asset class with continuous trading revenue rather than event-driven spikes.
Recursive Self-Improvement Inside the Company 5
Coinbase is building institutional 'brains' — markdown files per team, per repository and per individual containing every incident, financial control, A/B test and accepted or rejected pull request — that coding agents ingest before making any change to a service.
framework🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbasecoding agentsGitHubinstitutional memory
So what: A concrete, low-tech template for converting tacit organizational knowledge into agent context; the simplicity — markdown files — is the point and is replicable at any company.
The critical enforced step in Coinbase's software development lifecycle is that when a human reviewer catches an agent's mistake they must edit the agent's thinking back into the brain rather than just patching the code, which pushes the one-shot pull request acceptance rate up over time and creates a recursive self-improvement loop.
causal🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbaserecursive self-improvementsoftware development lifecyclecode review
So what: The measurable KPI for AI-native engineering orgs is one-shot PR acceptance rate trend; a rising line is evidence the compounding loop is real rather than aspirational.
Armstrong now ships features himself by having an expensive frontier model write a three-phase plan of ten components each and then spinning up ten parallel agents — deliberately routed to cheaper models including open-source ones and Grok — to execute a phase in minutes.
anecdote🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highGrokopen-source modelscoding agentsCoinbase
So what: Orchestration is splitting into expensive planning models and cheap execution models, which is bearish for uniform frontier-model pricing and bullish for cheap inference supply.
AI adoption eliminates tasks rather than people, so existing companies will not shrink to ten-person headcounts; instead current teams ship far faster while some genuinely tiny new companies do what was previously impossible.
contrarian🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highCoinbaseAI adoptionlabor marketsoperating leverage
So what: Pushes back on the popular tiny-company thesis; the tradeable implication is margin expansion at existing companies growing revenue faster than headcount, not headcount collapse.
“people are not being eliminated. I think tasks are being eliminated.” ▶ 17:08
Coinbase is drafting policies to let employees take their personal AI brain with them when they leave the company, treating accumulated individual agent context as portable rather than as corporate property.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: mediumCoinbaseAI agentsemployment policyinstitutional memory
So what: An early and unusual stance on ownership of AI-accumulated personal context — a policy question every large employer will face and one with real IP implications.
New Limit and Epigenetic Reprogramming 4
New Limit has demonstrated successful reprogramming of at least one human cell type in humanized mouse models, is now testing in non-human primates, and will launch its first phase one clinical trial next year with three to ten additional drug candidates expected off the platform in coming years.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEO and New Limit co-founderconviction: highNew Limitepigenetic reprogrammingclinical trialslongevity biotech
So what: A dated catalyst — first-in-human trial next year — for the most credible venture-funded longevity platform, and a readable milestone for the whole reprogramming category.
New Limit built what Armstrong calls the leading frontier model for epigenetic reprogramming, which searches the vast combinatorial space of transcription factors to recommend which large pool screens to run next in its 50-to-60-person South San Francisco lab.
framework🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEO and New Limit co-founderconviction: highNew Limittranscription factorsAI in biologyhigh-throughput screening
So what: A concrete instance of AI compressing a combinatorial biology search space, with the wet-lab throughput rather than the model as the binding constraint.
New Limit's first three target cell types are hepatocytes, vasculature cells and immune T cells, with the initial phase one in alcoholic liver disease where patients have no option besides a transplant most never receive — an indication Armstrong estimates could alone be worth $20 billion.
data_point🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEO and New Limit co-founderconviction: highNew Limitalcoholic liver diseasehepatocytesT cellsGLP-1
So what: Classic high-unmet-need entry strategy mirroring GLP-1 development; ALD is the near-term commercial case while rejuvenation is the option value.
The strategic goal is a cocktail of cell-type-specific reprogramming therapies for healthy people — restoring a 40-year-old's liver, immune system, vasculature, brain, muscle and skin to their 20-year-old function — with skin rejuvenation alone framed as roughly a trillion-dollar market.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEO and New Limit co-founderconviction: highNew Limitlongevityskin rejuvenationepigenetic reprogramming
So what: Sizes the consumer longevity opportunity far above the therapeutic indication, and explains why disease-first regulatory entry is a wedge rather than the destination.
Regulatory Sandboxes and Human Enhancement 4
The absence of any sandbox to iterate on regulated innovation is the primary brake on progress, and federal land could host 10-to-100-square-mile freedom cities exempt from EPA, FAA and local permitting where data centers, nuclear plants and unlimited drone delivery could be tested before going through normal national approval.
framework🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highfreedom citiesspecial economic zonesEPAFAAnuclear powerdata centersdrone delivery
So what: A named policy proposal previously floated in the campaign; if it advances it would be a step-change for nuclear, drone and datacenter buildout timelines.
US federalism already functions as fifty competing special economic zones and that internal regulatory competition is a major reason America leads globally, though the deregulatory environment under the current administration has reduced entrepreneurial interest in offshore charter cities.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: mediumUnited StatesfederalismShenzhenSingaporeDubaispecial economic zones
So what: Explains the capital rotation away from network-state projects back toward US-domiciled hard tech as the domestic regulatory substrate improves.
“the US is 50 special economic zones cuz of federalism and the state system” ▶ 44:31
Pew research shows roughly 80% of Americans support embryo editing for disease prevention, and Armstrong — an investor in embryo editing company Preventative — predicts it will eventually be considered abnormal to have children without such screening and editing.
prediction🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: highPreventativeembryo editingPew Researchgenetic screening
So what: Public support for disease-prevention editing is far higher than the Gattaca-style discourse implies, which shortens the expected commercialization runway for the category.
Cognitive enhancement is the next meta-problem Armstrong is exploring but without a thesis yet, motivated by evidence that nutrients such as folic acid raise childhood IQ in some countries and by the view that humans must take control of their own evolution as AI gets smarter.
thesis🗣 Brian Armstrong, Coinbase CEOconviction: exploratorycognitive enhancementIQbrain machine interfacesAI
So what: An explicit open call for founders with a thesis here — a signal of where a well-capitalized incubator is likely to deploy next.

Johnny Harris 1 episode(s) · 21 insights

I don't get democracy51 min21 insights2026-09-10
▶ Watch on YouTube
Johnny Harris and collaborator Adam unpack why 'democracy' is used to mean incompatible things, landing on V-Dem's five measurable dimensions — electoral, liberal, egalitarian, participatory and deliberative — as the working framework, and noting that V-Dem publishes annual country-level scores on all five. The episode is primarily a civics explainer with limited direct market content, but it carries real political-risk material: why elections-only country indicators miss elected-dictator backsliding, how material inequality and unlimited super PAC money convert economic inequality into unequal policy influence, evidence that cold public-opinion polling manufactures non-opinions, and Ireland's 2016 citizens' assembly as a template for breaking policy gridlock.
Five measurable dimensions of democracy · 4Elections vs liberal safeguards · 5Inequality and policy capture · 4Reliability of public opinion · 4Deliberation as gridlock-breaker · 4
Five measurable dimensions of democracy 4
V-Dem (Varieties of Democracy) scores every country in the world annually across five separate dimensions — electoral, liberal, egalitarian, participatory and deliberative — rather than as a single binary democracy/not-democracy label.
framework🗣 Johnny Harris, hostconviction: highV-DemVarieties of Democracycountry risk
So what: A free, published, multi-dimensional panel dataset usable as an institutional-quality input for sovereign and EM political-risk models — far more granular than headline democracy indices.
“actually scores every country in the world on these five dimensions that are here in this cube. They do that every year” ▶ 49:26
A 2024 study of what ordinary citizens mean by 'democracy' found free elections rated most important, but gender equality, civil rights, state aid for the unemployed, and taxing the rich to subsidize the poor all rated close behind — meaning the public conflates the term with desirable policy outcomes rather than a system of government.
data_point🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highpublic opinionredistributioncivil rights
So what: Explains why 'defending democracy' rhetoric maps onto redistributive policy expectations in polling — relevant to how political messaging translates into actual fiscal agendas.
“People often use democracy as a term that means whatever they think is good government.” ▶ 4:44
The five dimensions actively trade off against each other rather than being jointly maximizable — maximizing participatory mechanisms like town meetings and public comment periods cuts against egalitarian democracy, because the people with time and resources to attend are disproportionately old and wealthy.
causal🗣 Adam and Johnny Harrisconviction: highpublic commenttown hallsparticipation
So what: Directly relevant to permitting and local-government process: more public input systematically over-weights incumbent property owners, which is the mechanism behind NIMBY capture of development approvals.
“If you max out one of these, like the participatory one, then, you cut against the egalitarian one because people with the loudest voices are often the people who have the most economic resources” ▶ 45:44
Even the premise that competitive elections are the foundational requirement is contested — some argue that electing representatives creates a permanent politician class amounting to a form of oligarchy, and that sortition (random selection, as used for juries and citizens' assemblies) would be more democratic.
contrarian🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: mediumsortitionoligarchyelectoral democracy
So what: A live academic challenge to the assumption underlying every elections-based governance index — worth tracking if sortition-based bodies spread beyond advisory roles.
“we've actually created like a class, a permanent politician class, and some people call that a kind of oligarchy” ▶ 46:47
Elections vs liberal safeguards 5
Holding elections is insufficient for electoral democracy — the contests must be real competitions with broad equal voting rights and a free press, which is why Russia under Putin, whose serious opponents are in prison, exile or dead, does not qualify despite holding votes.
framework🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highRussiaVladimir Putinpress freedomV-Dem
So what: The reason simple 'does it hold elections' screens misclassify authoritarian states — press freedom and opposition viability are the operative variables for country risk.
“The elections need to be real competitions.” ▶ 8:36
Liberal democracy is defined by constraints on government between elections — rule of law, checks on power, and rights the state cannot violate — and its specific function is preventing an elected government from becoming an elected dictatorship.
framework🗣 Adam, co-host and researcher, quoting a political scientistconviction: highrule of lawconstitutional limitsdemocratic backsliding
So what: Isolates the dimension that actually protects property rights and contract enforcement — the one that matters most for capital allocation, and the one that erodes first in backsliding episodes while elections continue.
“Some basic guarantees that constrains the government in between elections so that you don't end up with an elected dictator.” ▶ 22:08
The word 'liberal' in liberal democracy refers to the early-1800s meaning of limiting state power, not to left-of-center politics — the split occurred when the Industrial Revolution's wealth concentration led 'new liberals' to argue the state must intervene to create conditions for freedom.
framework🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highliberalismIndustrial Revolutionfree markets
So what: Clears up a recurring source of misreading in cross-country political analysis, where the same label denotes opposite economic policy.
The same party label carries opposite economic content across countries: Australia's Liberal Party platform emphasizes free enterprise, private property and minimizing regulatory burden on business, while Canada's Liberal Party platform emphasizes a just society, human dignity, equality of opportunity and diversity.
data_point🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highLiberal Party of AustraliaLiberal Party of Canadapolitical parties
So what: A concrete warning against inferring policy direction from party names in cross-border political analysis — read platforms, not labels.
“if you look at Australia's Liberal Party's platform, it talks about things like free enterprise, private property, minimizing the regulatory burden on business” ▶ 20:16
States that put 'Democratic' in their name — the DPRK, the former German Democratic Republic — are invoking a Marxist-Leninist concept of a party representing working-class interests, one that explicitly does not win power through electoral competition.
framework🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highNorth KoreaEast GermanyMarxism-Leninism
So what: A reminder that self-declared regime type is uninformative; only behavioral indicators like opposition viability distinguish regimes.
Inequality and policy capture 4
US super PACs can legally accept unlimited contributions, and while most Americans give nothing at all in elections, some billionaires give hundreds of millions — converting inequality outside politics directly into unequal influence over policy.
causal🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highsuper PACscampaign financeUnited Statesbillionaires
So what: The named transmission mechanism from wealth concentration to policy outcomes — the structural case for why regulatory and tax outcomes track donor preferences rather than median-voter preferences.
“Even though we all get one vote, our ability to actually impact the democratic process and decision making is actually not equal at all.” ▶ 23:39
Egalitarian democracy holds that extreme material inequality and democracy are mutually corrosive — that citizens cannot participate as free and equal decision-makers without housing, food and minimal education.
thesis🗣 political scientist interviewed in the episodeconviction: highinequalityoligarchyV-Dem
So what: Frames inequality as an institutional-stability variable rather than only a distributional one — the analytical bridge between Gini-type metrics and political-risk scoring.
“Material inequalities, if they get too large, do undermine democracy.” ▶ 25:33
In most US states an employee can legally be fired for expressing political opinions their employer disagrees with, and employers are not universally required to grant time off to vote — private employment power constrains political rights that exist on paper.
data_point🗣 political scientist interviewed in the episodeconviction: highat-will employmentUnited Stateslabor lawpolitical speech
So what: A concrete, under-discussed corporate-governance and employment-litigation exposure, and a lever that could be targeted by state-level legislation.
“In America, in most states, somebody could be fired for expressing political opinions that their boss disagrees with.” ▶ 24:47
The operative distinction is between a right on paper and a right that is practically exercisable — a right with no practical mechanism to use it is, in the words of the Harvard professor cited, just a fake right.
framework🗣 Harvard professor interviewed in the episodeconviction: highcivil rightsinstitutional quality
So what: A test applicable to any jurisdiction's investor protections: formal legal guarantees are worth what their enforcement mechanism is worth, not what the statute says.
“you can say you've got a right on paper, but if there's no actual, practical way to make it real, then it's just a fake right” ▶ 23:01
Reliability of public opinion 4
A landmark survey asked respondents whether the 1975 Public Affairs Act should be repealed: 16% said repeal, 18% said do not repeal, and two-thirds had no opinion — but there is no 1975 Public Affairs Act, meaning roughly a third of respondents produced opinions about a law that did not exist.
data_point🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highpublic opinion pollingsurvey methodology
So what: A hard calibration point for anyone using survey data as a market or policy input: a meaningful share of any poll's responses are manufactured rather than held.
“There is no 1975 Public Affairs Act. They asked about a fake law, right?” ▶ 35:01
The political-science takeaway from the fake-law study is not that voters are stupid but that asking people to weigh in cold produces non-opinions rather than real ones — implying the low-information voter problem is a design failure in how input is solicited, not an inherent flaw in democracy.
contrarian🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highlow-information voterspollingdeliberation
So what: Argues polling-based sentiment measures are improvable by method rather than fundamentally unreliable — relevant to the design of prediction markets and deliberative polling products.
“when you ask people to weigh in completely cold on questions like this, the problem is not that you're getting the opinions of dumb people. It's that you're not really getting real opinions at all” ▶ 35:44
Ballot-measure language is often functionally unreadable — a 2025 Ohio proposed amendment on authorizing state bonds for local infrastructure capital improvements runs to dense multi-clause legal text that voters are expected to evaluate cold.
anecdote🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highOhioballot measuresmunicipal bondsinfrastructure
So what: Direct-democracy funding mechanisms like state infrastructure bond authorizations turn on comprehension that demonstrably isn't there — a source of outcome variance in referendum-driven fiscal policy.
Two of the most widely circulated anti-democracy quotes are fabricated: the 'four wolves and a lamb voting on dinner' line attributed to Benjamin Franklin first appeared in a 1990 newspaper column, and Churchill's 'five-minute conversation with the average voter' comes from a 1992 message board post.
data_point🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highBenjamin FranklinWinston Churchillmisinformation
So what: A specific illustration of how fabricated authority propagates unchecked through social platforms — the same dynamic that drives narrative formation in markets.
“Winston Churchill absolutely never said that. As far as we can tell, it is from a message board post from 1992.” ▶ 33:36
Deliberation as gridlock-breaker 4
Ireland convened a citizens' assembly of 99 randomly selected citizens in 2016 on its highly restrictive abortion law, meeting over successive weekends to hear evidence and question experts jury-style — breaking a political deadlock the country's ordinary politics could not resolve, and leading to a referendum that scrapped the constitutional ban.
anecdote🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highIrelandcitizens' assemblysortitionreferendum
So what: A working, replicable institutional mechanism for resolving policy logjams — relevant wherever regulatory paralysis is the binding constraint on investment.
“The Irish people have voted in favor of scrapping a constitutional ban on abortion.” ▶ 41:10
The identified vulnerability of citizens' assemblies is that the organizers choose the experts, the testimony and the evidence presented — so the mechanism's legitimacy depends entirely on the neutrality of whoever convenes it, and the assembly itself only recommends rather than decides.
causal🗣 Adam, co-host and researcherconviction: highcitizens' assemblyIrelandinstitutional design
So what: The capture risk to watch if deliberative bodies are adopted for contested economic questions — control of the evidence agenda is control of the outcome.
“the people who organized it chose the experts, and they chose whose stories they heard, and they chose what evidence was presented” ▶ 43:23
Deliberation's measurable output is not opinion change but justifiability — participants rarely flip positions, but end the process able to explain why the other side holds its view and to ground their own conclusion in exposure to multiple viewpoints and expert input.
framework🗣 deliberation practitioner interviewed in the episodeconviction: highdeliberative democracypolarization
So what: Resets the success metric for depolarization efforts from persuasion to mutual comprehension — a more achievable and more measurable target.
“I understand why the other side would vote the way they do, and that's okay” ▶ 42:17
Harris argues the absence of politicians in the room is a feature of sortition-based bodies, because politicians form an elite class whose incentives — reelection and fundraising — are systematically misaligned with the majority of citizens.
thesis🗣 Johnny Harris, hostconviction: highsortitionpolitical incentivescampaign fundraising
So what: Names fundraising dependence as the specific incentive distortion that randomized bodies remove — the testable claim underpinning the sortition case.
